#10571. Google will disable Search for students unless schools accept AI features in ‘Guided Learning’ push

Google is today launching new AI settings for schools, but with the big catch that Search is fully disabled if AI features are turned off entirely.

In a blog post today, Google says it is “focused on building technology responsibly to help students thrive” and, as such, will tailor its AI efforts to help students. “Guided Learning” in Gemini, Search, and Classroom will include helping students “work through problems without skipping straight to answers.”

…if a student pastes a complex physics problem or asks for help writing a book report in Search, they’ll see prompts to help them think critically and work through the concepts, without going directly to the answer.

That’s launching in a pilot program this month in select US schools, with Guided Learning in Google Classroom launching globally today. Google describes this as a “1-1 study tool, anchored in class material to help [students] stay on task” while also giving the teacher insights into how their class is handling the material.

Beyond this new Guided Learning push, Google also says that it will now turn off “Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search” for K-12 student accounts by default “until school administrators explicitly turn them on.”

As The New York Times points out, having those features fully off has a big side effect: it cuts students off from Google Search entirely.

Google says that this is because Search and AI “are deeply integrated” and confirms that students in schools with AI features turned off (which, to reiterate, is now the default setting) “will not have access to search.”

As a reminder, Google Search’s AI features can largely be turned off using a simple modifier.

Google’s post says that the “goal is simple: provide school leaders the control and confidence to use AI products in classrooms as they see fit.” Yet, speaking to The New York Times, Google further explained that two options will be available to schools in “early 2027,” one that has AI Overviews and other AI features turned on, the other with Guided Learning in their place, leaving schools with no choice other than to accept AI features or give up access to Google Search for their students entirely.

NYT points out repeatedly that Google’s prior AI pushes in schools have been met with fierce opposition from educators and parents alike.

Hi, friends! Welcome to Installer No. 151, your guide to the best and Verge-iest stuff in the world. (If you’re new here, welcome, get psyched because it’s officially Bad Holiday Movie Season, and also you can read all the old editions at the Installer homepage.)

This week, I’ve been reading about Andrew Garfield and GTA VI and sexy joke thieves, finally actually watching The Sopranosfor the first time (I know, I know), mourning the end of Salon, reinstalling the new and extremely improved Notion Calendar, testing the new Keurig Alta coffee machine, reviewing the iPad Mini and the touchscreen MacBook Pro, and really thinking about just throwing it all away and buying an Enough Phone.

I also have for you the long-awaited return of a classic Zelda game, a monster movie worth the trek to the theater, a couple of great new phone accessories, awesome retro headphones, and more. Let’s do it.

We talked last week about Readwise Books, the new app that’s a mix of Kindle, Goodreads, and Strava. I’m a longtime fan and user of Readwise Reader, so I wasn’t shocked that I liked the new app a lot, too. And I’ve heard from a bunch of you that you do, too!

I’ve talked to Tristan Homsi, one of the Readwise cofounders, a few times over the years, and have always enjoyed hearing his thoughts about reading, apps, and digital products in general. I asked him to share his homescreen to see if I might be able to get a hint about what he’s reading and the other stuff he thinks is good. (I also asked Daniel Doyon, Readwise’s other cofounder, to share, and he didn’t respond. Daniel is dead to me.)

Here’s Tristan’s homescreen, plus some info on the apps he uses and why:

The phone: iPhone 13 (!).Believe it or not, I chose to buy this relatively outdated phone last year… not because it’s my favorite form factor or anything, but because it’s closer in specs to the median device our Readwise users have and helps me build a bit more user empathy with them. I’m able to catch performance regressions and bugs in Readwise that the developers on our team with Duos and Pro Maxes and such miss.

Let me tell you though, iOS 26 itself does not work amazingly on an iPhone 13. Lots of lagging and janky animations. I am somewhat bought into the conspiracy theory that they make the old phones suck so you buy a new one… not so much intentionally, but rather by virtue of just making each successive iOS use more and more resources, doing fancier and fancier things.​

The wallpaper: My lovely wife and my cat Sophie :)

The apps: Clock, Settings, Google Calendar, Camera, Google Maps, Spotify, Bay Wheels, Oura, Eight Sleep, Auto Eight, BeReal, ChatGPT, Runmeter, Strava, Readwise Highlights, Readwise Reader, Phone, Messages, Safari.

Of course, I have all three Readwise apps front and center. Highlights and Reader as app icons, and Readwise Books in widget form at the top! It’s a great reminder to jump back into your current book (I’m reading Apple in China right now… speaking of Apple!) instead of scrolling twitter (on a later page), and seeing my friends currently reading really makes me want to jump in and join them :)

I also am a huge fan of the somewhat-defunct social app BeReal. It had its moment in 2022, but quite a few of my friends are still on there! You post once a day, can see what your close friends around the world are doing, and most importantly can’t get sucked in for more than two minutes since it limits you to a random photo or two a day! I still post basically every day, and have almost five years of memories in there.

Speaking of getting sucked in, I don’t have it pictured on my home screen, but I also have Clearspace installed, which helps me stay off of all social media.

Some other apps shown on the homescreen: “auto eight” is something I vibecoded to better control my Eight Sleep mattress (lol)… Strava and Runmeter are awesome for tracking my runs… and Bay Wheels is my main mode of commute around San Francisco daily.

I’ve mentioned Kagi a bunch of times in this newsletter — it has been my default search engine for the last few years, and I can’t recommend it highly enough. (What if Google, but good, you know?) The challenge with Kagi is it costs $10 a month, which is a lot to ask when Google is free. But as of this week, you can now at least try it for yourself, since Kagi will give you 50 free searches before even asking you to sign up. You get 100 more when you do, even before you pay.

If you search a lot, and miss what Google used to be, give Kagi a whirl. I am a very happy customer, and suspect I will be for a long time.

#10568. Top 5 AI Animation Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A few years ago, animating a ten-second scene meant storyboards, rigging and a lot of patience. Today you can type a sentence, upload a drawing or point at a reference clip and get moving footage back in a minute or two. The results are uneven, but they are good enough that designers, marketers and indie developers now use them for storyboards, product teasers, game trailers and social content.

The catch is that these tools are not interchangeable. Some excel at cinematic camera moves, some at editing footage you already have, and some at a particular visual style. This guide compares five AI animation generators, what each one is best at and where it falls short. Plans, credits and model names change quickly, so check each vendor's pricing page before you commit.

Runway is a cloud workspace for generating and editing video, images and audio in one place. Its current video model is Gen-4.5, which Runway promotes for motion quality, prompt adherence and visual fidelity. The platform also offers agent and workflow tools, mobile apps for iPhone and Android, and an API for developers.

Best for: Creators who want to generate a shot and then keep refining it in the same environment, and teams that need an API.

Limitations: There is a lot to learn, and credit costs add up when you iterate. Runway offers a free trial, but check the pricing page for current limits.

Kling AI is an AI video and image generator with a creative studio for image generation, video generation and motion control. It also includes an agent for brainstorming and scripting, and a node-based canvas for working through scripts, storyboards, images and video with multiple rounds of editing. Kling lists advertising, film, music videos and e-commerce among its use cases, and offers an API platform and automation options for producing video at scale.

Best for: Marketers and studios that want to move from script to storyboard to footage in a single workspace.

Limitations: The feature set is broad, so newcomers may need time to find the right workflow. Free credits were not described on the pages I checked, so confirm what is available before relying on it.

Luma focuses on turning still images into video with camera motion. Its workspace can create transitions between frames, extend clips, transform footage into new styles, reframe aspect ratios and add voice, music, captions and lip sync. It runs Luma's own Ray models and also gives access to several third-party models, so you can compare results without switching platforms.

Best for: Designers and illustrators who already have key art, product shots or concept images and want to bring them to life.

Limitations: Paid plans start at around $30 a month at the time of writing, and generation credits can run out quickly if you test many variations.

Pika is a creative platform covering video, images and audio. Its Video Studio can produce anything from a short clip to a multi-shot video of around 30 seconds, and its toolkit includes Extend Video, Motion Transfer, Swap Anything and Color Grade. Character Studio lets you create reusable characters, and separate studios handle music, speech and sound effects. Pika also exposes several video models, including its own Pika 2.5 alongside third-party options.

Best for: Social content creators who want effects, sound and edits in one tool.

Limitations: With so many tools and models, results vary from one to the next. Check the current pricing page for credit details.

Pollo AI is a generator that leans into one look: anime. Its animation generator creates anime-style videos from a text prompt and lets you adjust the video content, output style and size. The page offers eleven Japanese animator-inspired style options, such as Hayao Miyazaki and Makoto Shinkai, and the platform also provides a separate tool that converts existing video into anime.

Best for: Creators who want a consistent anime look for shorts, fan projects, music visuals or social posts without learning a full editing suite.

Limitations: The generator page describes text prompts as its input and does not state clip length, resolution or credit details, so test a few generations and review the terms before planning a project around it. As with any style-focused tool, the look is distinctive, which is a strength for anime projects and a limit for anything else.

Start with the job rather than the brand. If you need to edit and refine footage, Runway is a natural fit. If you want a script-to-video pipeline, look at Kling. If you have strong still images, try Luma. If you want effects and sound for social posts, Pika covers a lot of ground. If anime is the goal, Pollo AI is built for it.

Run the same prompt through two or three tools before paying for anything. Differences in motion, faces and text rendering show up quickly, and they are often more important than the feature list.

AI animation tools have moved from novelty to practical production aid. None of them replaces a skilled animator, but each can speed up storyboards, concepts and short-form content when matched to the right job. Try a few with a real project, compare the output, and keep a human in charge of the final cut. If you want a quick way to experiment with anime styles, you can create anime clips from a prompt and see how the results compare with the other tools on this list.

Are AI animation generators free? Most offer a free trial or limited credits, with paid plans for more generations and higher quality. Terms change often, so read each pricing page.

Can I use AI-generated animation commercially? It depends on the provider's terms and your plan. Check the licensing section before publishing client work.

Do I need animation experience? No, but knowing basic shot composition helps you write better prompts and judge the results.

Which tool is best for beginners? Pick one with a simple prompt-first workflow, test the free tier and move to a more advanced tool as your needs grow.

#10567. ChatGPT has hit roughly 20% of Google Search volume, OpenAI says

According to OpenAI, ChatGPT has managed to grow its search use to around 20% of Google Search’s total volume, a staggering figure.

Google Search has had a massively dominant lead in the search space, with Microsoft’s Bing a distant competitor with a single-digit share at any given time. But, following its push into AI-powered search about 2 years ago, OpenAI says that ChatGPT is catching up in a big way.

Speaking at an OpenAI press event/chat yesterday, ChatGPT consumer product lead Adam Fry confirmed that search volume in ChatGPT is “roughly one-fifth (20%) of the volume of queries of Google.”

There’s a lot of wiggle room there, as the source of Google’s search volume wasn’t revealed. OpenAI’s measurement of what a search query is boils down to when a user inputs a prompt/query that results in a web search – “Every time you ask a question and go search the web, that’s a search.” Again, a lot of wiggle room here.

But, in any case, this would be the biggest rival Google Search has had yet, and certainly explains Google’s massive push to expand AI in Search. Google admits it is facing “lower monetization” with that push, something OpenAI is also surely dealing with in its own efforts. The Verge notes that Fry said that “most people do not seem to mind ads” in ChatGPT and that “we could be doing this more, actually.”

Fry also said that “people really love” ChatGPT’s personalization and understanding.

The A10 Warthog-a-like can fly for 3 to 4 minutes with its single 1,300mAh battery.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A remote-control electronics and DIY hobbyist was surprised by a model airplane they created, powered by a pair of PC air duster engines. The A10 Warthog-like model with a 21-inch wingspan flew “much faster than I had anticipated,” commented RCMakerLab. The video they shared also shows how to build your own fast, agile RC plane powered by the ‘jets’ salvaged from PC air dusters like the 110,000 RPM brushless Wolfbox MF60, which is currently 36% off on Amazon and priced at $31.99.

RCMakerLab’s informative and entertaining video (above) was inspired by some earlier disassembly and tests using engines pulled from PC air dusters. Viewers asked the maker to go beyond pondering the thrust these mini jet engines could generate and actually put them to the test in an RC plane.

About eight minutes of this 10-minute video focus on building the twin-engine RC plane. I found it interesting to see it crafted with wings formed from a folded-over Styrofoam sheet and hot glue, and to see it all put together. The motorized wing flap control seemed ingenious. However, if you want to skip straight to the main attraction, fast-forward to about 8 mins for the installation of the two air blower motors. Then, at 9 min 20s, you can see the RC plane's rapid takeoff. I thought it sounded great; it looked very fast and extremely agile.

Rechargeable handheld air dusters reside in a PC DIY enthusiast sweet spot between canned air and workshop compressors. RCMakerLab’s materials list includes no-brand AliExpress 130,000 RPM blowers at $23 each. Here’s a very similar-performing model on Amazon that's even cheaper, if you prefer.

The video ends with a tease that this successful air duster engine-inspired escapade has inspired RCMakerLab to build a “much larger high-wing model.” The maker asks viewers to let him know “Should I go for it?” in the comments – so stay tuned.

#10562. Peak Design’s new wireless charging stand pulls double duty, literally

The three new charging accessories from Peak Design hit the high notes, like (dual) Qi2 wireless charging and wired fast charging without bulky bricks.

Last year at CES, Peak Design gave us a sneak preview of its upcoming accessory lineup. Obviously, the company is known for its premium travel bags and accessories, and they make some awesome gear. But they have mobile accessories, too, and the latest release looks pretty cool.

All three new products are charging accessories that work with Android. More specifically, they support the right protocols, like Qi2 25W wireless charging and 65W fast wired charging.

In fact, the 2-in-1 Mobile Charging Station supports dual, simultaneous Qi2 charging. In essence, it’s an adjustable charging stand that either lays flat or props up for a more traditional experience. Premium build materials like aluminum are used for the base, top cradle, and stem. There’s a recessed USB-C port for the adapter you’ll need to source yourself, and a secondary USB-C output port for charging a third device.

To get full power from the 2-in-1 Mobile Charging Station, you’ll need at minimum a 75W charging adapter and cable. The box includes a 100W cable, but charging two devices at 25W wirelessly takes a lot of power. Still, it’s pretty cool that it packs two Qi2 pads, not just one paired with a slower coil.

If that’s not your use case, a 65W charger is enough to power one pad at the full 25W. The absolute minimum required power to charge anything is 45W, Peak Design says on its website.

That might be why Peak Design also launched a slim 65W travel charger. It’s a fantastic little charger that slips into a tech pouch or backpack without much trouble. I carry a slim 65W charger wherever I go, and this easily fits the bill for portability.

It has two USB-C ports. Only one is rated for 65W, and if both are in use, you’re either getting 30W/30W or 45W/20W output. Still, I don’t demand much from a travel charger, and 65W is plenty fast enough to charge a MacBook Air or Pixel 11 within the hour.

The other option is a slim battery. Peak Design also launched a Qi2 Kickstand Travel Battery, capable of 25W wireless fast charging as well. It’s rated for 5,000mAh. Because of the kickstand that wraps around and acts as a frame for the charger, it isn’t as slim as some of the batteries we’re seeing recently. Still, I wouldn’t call it “thick” by any means.

It’s solidly built and has a really nice faux leather finish on both sides, accented by status LEDs on the bottom edge. It also sits really nicely on the Pixel 11 I used it on, which is a point of failure for some. This one feels really nice to use.

Peak Design’s new charging accessories don’t come cheap. The new travel 2-in-1 charging stand is available for $99.95, but that’s really not that bad if you value dual fast wireless charging or have a secondary device or partner to use it with. The battery comes at the same price of $99.95, and the slim 65W charger is available for $64.95.

#10561. Epic Games Launcher is coming to SteamOS

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Epic Games confirmed that its Linux launcher will officially support SteamOS and Steam Decks.

Epic Games confirmed that its Linux launcher will officially support SteamOS and Steam Decks.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

In October, Epic Games said that it was “investigating” making Fortnite compatible with Valve’s Linux-based SteamOS. At the time, Epic Games Launcher was in preview, but SteamOS was not specifically listed as a supported distro. Now, Communications Director Jake Jones has confirmed SteamOS compatibility, along with a handful of other distros, to GamingOnLinux. In response to a question about the AppImage being replaced with Debian / Ubuntu .deb downloads, Jones said:

“The new Epic Games Launcher is still in Preview and we plan to support as many Linux distributions as possible, including Fedora, SteamOS, Cachy, Omarchy, and more. We’re testing and evaluating packaging formats for distribution, and we’ll restore AppImage this month.”

That doesn’t necessarily mean Fortnite will come to SteamOS. Easy Anti-Cheat (EAC) Linux v2 support still remains an open question. When it announced its work to bring Fortnite to Linux, the company said that the Steam Deck “does not support the required features for our EAC v2 solution, but we are actively talking with Valve and hope to support it in the future.”

#10558. Sony’s new PlayStation Portal handheld includes ‘additional’ internal storage

Sony is launching a new version of its PlayStation Portal exclusively in Japan next month. The updated model will launch in the midnight black color option we’ve seen before, and will include “additional” internal storage for future-looking features.

It’s the additional internal storage that’s the surprise change to this particular model, which will be locked to Japanese language options and can only be used with a PlayStation account registered in Japan. Sony says the extra storage is for “future functionality” that it will announce once these features are finalized.

Sony is also renaming this particular Portal from PlayStation Portal Remote Player to PlayStation Portal Player. The name change, combined with extra internal storage, seems to suggest that this particular model might get access to streaming apps.

The existing PlayStation Portal doesn’t support streaming apps like YouTube, Disney Plus, or Netflix, and is primarily designed to stream PS5 games from a console or the cloud. The PlayStation Portal isn’t powerful enough to play PlayStation games locally, but extra storage could also let you sync media for offline playback.

This Japan-only PlayStation Portal is launching on December 10th, priced at 39,980 yen (around $253).

#10557. WoW: Forever launch used 70,480 CPU cores and 251 terabytes of RAM

Throwing 70,480 CPU cores at Azeroth so you don't have to stare at a login queue

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Blizzard pulled out all the stops to ensure World of Warcraft: Forever survived a chaotic launch night. What was the company's secret weapon? Splurging an absurd amount of money on infrastructure made up of 3,924 virtual machines with 70,480 CPU cores and a whopping 251,136 GB, or 251 TB, of memory.

Praised by media and players as one of the smoothest launches in Blizzard's history, World of Warcraft: Forever requires significant computing firepower. 3,924 virtual machines work out to 18 cores and 64GB of memory per instance. Or, the company could be using a mixture of 16-core and 32-core nodes with slightly less memory.

To put the infrastructure's scale in consumer terms, we are looking at about 8,810 mainstream processors, or 7,848 high-end gaming systems with 32GB of memory each. In any event, the hardware is greatly improved compared to what World of Warcraft used to run on back in 2009.

*Slaps Roof of WoW: Forever* This baby is running on: 3,924 Virtual Machines 251,136 GB of Memory 70,480 CPU Cores https://t.co/P9PdRBzke7November 5, 2026

In 2009, Blizzard said it used 10 data centers worldwide to host World of Warcraft. At the time, the infrastructure included 13,250 server blades, totaling 75,000 CPU cores and 112.5 TB of memory. By comparison, World of Warcraft: Forever leverages 6% fewer CPU cores but rocks 2.2X more memory. Modern processors are leagues faster than chips from 17 years ago. By today's standards, the bottleneck is memory capacity and bandwidth, which is why the infrastructure can run on fewer cores but has more than double the memory to compensate.

Blizzard does not specify the exact hardware that World of Warcraft: Forever runs on. However, if we were to translate the core specifications to, for example, AMD's EPYC 128-core Bergamo or 96-core Genoa chips, it could be anywhere between 275 and 365 2U server nodes, depending on which processor it uses. Therefore, World of Warcraft: Forever could be running on just eight to ten 42U server racks, a noteworthy improvement over 17 years ago.

Blizzard should have no trouble building the infrastructure for World of Warcraft: Forever. A ballpark estimate for about ten 42U server racks with EPYC chips is at least $15 million, even with today's memory pricing. But then again, since Microsoft owns Blizzard now, the company could just be tapping its parent's existing Azure platform. Technically, it is not free compute, but Blizzard would benefit from cost pricing and priority allocation of Azure's capacity.

World of Warcraft: Forever shows that a high-profile launch does not have to be a nightmare when companies are willing to invest in computing infrastructure. In this case, many would agree Blizzard is putting those 251TB to good use, although Sam Altman may say otherwise.

#10555. Google Photos now lets you turn off background music in Memories

In an update, Google Photos is making it possible to fully disable background music in Memories.

Memories in Google Photos are a fun way to relive some highlighted moments in your photo library, but it can be a little frustrating to see a video pop up only to have the default background music to play over those memories.

Google is now rolling out an option to disable background music entirely.

This new global setting can be found under Photos settings > Preferences > Memories with a “Background music” toggle being added. We’re not seeing this in v7.99 on Android, but Google says that it is on its way to the app.

We are introducing a new setting across all platforms that allows you to disable background music added by Photos to Memories. When turned off, this feature prevents extra music tracks from playing during your Memories. If your Memory includes a video, you’ll still be able to hear its original audio.

Google adds that this is a global setting that applies to all Memories, including the end-of-year recap.

Beyond adding the option to remove background music, Google says that the variety of background music options has also been updated, so you might start hearing new tracks if you do opt to leave the setting turned on.

#10554. A 10-Point Checklist for Legacy Application Modernization

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Legacy applications often support critical business operations, but keeping them running can become increasingly difficult. Outdated infrastructure, growing technical debt, security vulnerabilities, and limited integration capabilities can make these systems expensive to maintain and harder to adapt to changing business needs.

Modernizing them involves more than upgrading technology. You need to understand what the existing application does, identify which components need improvement, and manage the transition without disrupting essential operations.

This 10-point legacy application modernization checklist walks you through the process, from assessing your existing systems and selecting a modernization strategy to migrating data, testing applications, and measuring results.

Legacy application modernization is the process of updating existing software applications, their architecture, infrastructure, or underlying technologies to meet current business and technical requirements.

Depending on the application's condition, modernization may involve moving it to a new platform, improving its code, redesigning its architecture, or replacing it with a new solution.

The goal is to improve performance, security, scalability, maintainability, and the application's ability to support future business requirements.

Modernization isn't necessarily the same as cloud migration. Moving an application from on-premises infrastructure to the cloud changes where it runs, but it may leave its underlying architecture and technical limitations unchanged.

An organization can modernize an application without moving it to the cloud or combine both approaches as part of a larger transformation.

Use this checklist to track the ten essential steps involved in modernizing legacy applications.

1. Define modernization goals: Identify business problems and establish measurable success criteria.

2. Assess existing applications: Review infrastructure, technical debt, code quality, and performance limitations.

3. Map application dependencies: Document critical workflows, integrations, and business rules.

4. Choose a modernization strategy: Select an approach based on business value, cost, complexity, and risk.

5. Develop a roadmap and budget: Prioritize activities, estimate costs, assign responsibilities, and establish milestones.

6. Plan architecture and security: Design the target environment and address security, compliance, and availability requirements.

7. Prepare data migration: Plan data transfer, integration changes, and validation procedures.

8. Modernize incrementally: Validate the approach with a pilot and implement changes in manageable phases.

9. Test and deploy: Verify functionality, performance, security, and data integrity before production rollout.

10. Monitor and optimize: Measure results, support users, and continuously improve the modernized application.

Let's examine what each step involves and how to determine whether you're ready to proceed.

Before selecting technologies or planning development work, identify what the modernization initiative needs to achieve.

Clear business objectives help prioritize investments, guide technical decisions, and establish whether the project delivers meaningful improvements.

Start by examining the problems affecting your application's performance, reliability, or business value.

For example, a legacy order management system might still process transactions reliably but require extensive manual work whenever the business adds a new sales channel.

In this situation, improving integration capabilities may be more valuable than replacing the entire application.

An application's age alone doesn't justify a major technology investment. Whether legacy modernization makes sense for your business depends on the problems the existing system creates, the cost of maintaining it, and whether smaller improvements could resolve those issues.

Once you've identified the problems, translate them into measurable objectives.

If slow performance is the primary concern, establish a target for response times or transaction processing speed. If maintenance expenses are rising, determine what reduction in operating costs would justify the investment.

Relevant key performance indicators (KPIs) may include:

These baseline measurements will help determine whether the changes deliver the expected results.

Completion check: You have a documented business case identifying the problems to address, expected outcomes, measurable KPIs, and stakeholders responsible for approving success.

A legacy application assessment provides a clear picture of your current technology environment.

Without this assessment, teams may underestimate project complexity, overlook critical components, or choose a modernization approach that doesn't address the application's actual limitations.

Begin by creating an inventory of your applications and their supporting technologies.

Document each application's purpose, business owner, primary users, operating environment, programming languages, frameworks, databases, and infrastructure requirements.

Also identify software versions, third-party dependencies, licensing restrictions, and components approaching or past their end-of-support dates.

If your organization operates several legacy applications, use a consistent assessment framework. This makes it easier to compare their business importance, technical condition, and modernization urgency.

Review the application's codebase to identify issues that make maintenance and development difficult.

Look for tightly coupled components, duplicated logic, outdated libraries, insufficient automated tests, and undocumented configurations.

Pay particular attention to areas where small modifications regularly cause unexpected problems elsewhere in the system.

For example, if payment processing, inventory management, and customer records share tightly connected code, modifying one function could require extensive testing across the entire application.

Your infrastructure assessment should also cover hardware limitations, database compatibility, backup capabilities, operating systems, and unsupported dependencies.

Analyze how the application performs during normal operations and periods of increased demand.

Review response times, database queries, resource utilization, transaction volumes, and recurring failures.

Existing monitoring data and incident records can reveal problems that aren't immediately visible during routine use.

Determine whether performance limitations originate from inefficient application code, infrastructure capacity, database design, or external dependencies.

This distinction matters because adding computing resources won't necessarily resolve problems caused by poor application logic.

Completion check: You have an application inventory and technical assessment report documenting major components, technical debt, operational risks, and performance limitations.

Legacy applications rarely operate independently. They exchange data with other systems, support interconnected business processes, and often contain business rules that aren't fully documented.

Understanding these dependencies is essential because changing one component can affect several other applications or workflows.

Document the processes that rely on the application, including the steps users follow, the data they need, and the expected outcomes.

Work with business users, application owners, operations teams, and developers familiar with the system.

Identify processes that cannot tolerate significant interruption, such as payment processing, order fulfillment, customer authentication, or financial reporting.

Don't overlook activities that happen less frequently. Month-end financial reports, annual calculations, and scheduled data exchanges may depend on functionality that isn't exercised during everyday operations.

Map how the application communicates with databases, APIs, third-party services, and other internal systems.

Include scheduled jobs, file transfers, message queues, authentication services, and reporting tools.

Some legacy applications contain years of accumulated business logic.

Rules for pricing, approvals, calculations, or customer eligibility may exist only in source code, database procedures, or undocumented workflows.

Identify these rules before modifying the underlying implementation.

Where possible, create automated tests that capture existing expected behavior. These tests can help verify that the modernized application preserves required functionality.

Completion check: Critical workflows, system dependencies, integrations, and business rules are documented and reviewed by the relevant business and technical owners.

The appropriate modernization strategy depends on the application's technical condition, business importance, budget, and future requirements.

Not every application needs to be rebuilt. Some systems require targeted improvements, while others may be better replaced or retired.

Common legacy application modernization strategies include:

Different applications within the same organization may require different approaches.

Evaluate each option against your technical constraints and business requirements.

Consider implementation effort, expected benefits, ongoing maintenance costs, required expertise, and potential disruption.

For example, replatforming may be suitable for an application that operates reliably but depends on aging infrastructure. Rearchitecting may be necessary when tightly coupled components prevent independent updates or scaling.

A complete rebuild requires particular caution. Recreating undocumented functionality can be expensive, and important business rules may be lost if the existing system hasn't been thoroughly assessed.

The right strategy should solve the identified problems without introducing unnecessary cost or complexity.

Completion check: Each application has a selected modernization strategy, documented rationale, estimated effort, and an approved decision on whether to modernize, retain, replace, or retire it.

Once you've selected a modernization approach, convert it into an actionable plan.

Your roadmap should establish what will change, in what order, who will be responsible, and how progress will be evaluated.

Prioritize modernization work according to business value, technical complexity, urgency, and dependencies.

Applications with unsupported components or significant security risks may require early attention. However, starting with the most complex application isn't always the best choice.

A smaller, lower-risk application can provide an opportunity to validate tools, workflows, and team capabilities before modernizing more critical systems.

Dependencies also affect the sequence. For example, a shared authentication service may need to be updated before applications that rely on it can be migrated.

Prepare a budget covering the full modernization process rather than development alone.

Compare upfront implementation expenses with expected operating costs over time.

A modernization approach with a lower initial cost may still require significant ongoing maintenance.

Allow contingency for unexpected issues, particularly when legacy documentation is incomplete or system dependencies remain uncertain.

Divide the project into phases with defined deliverables and approval requirements.

Assign responsible owners for architecture, development, security, data migration, testing, and business acceptance.

Establish how project risks will be reported and who can approve changes to scope, budget, or deployment schedules.

Keep the roadmap adaptable. Findings from pilot implementations or migration rehearsals may require changes to the original plan.

Completion check: Your modernization roadmap includes prioritized activities, estimated costs, responsible owners, dependencies, milestones, and approval checkpoints.

The target architecture determines how the modernized application will operate, scale, integrate with other systems, and remain maintainable.

Security and compliance requirements should be incorporated into the design from the beginning.

Choose an architecture that fits the application's actual requirements.

Possible approaches include a modular monolith, service-oriented architecture, microservices, or event-driven components.

Microservices aren't automatically the best option. Although they allow independent deployment and scaling of components, they also introduce complexity in communication, monitoring, deployment, and data management.

For smaller applications or tightly integrated business processes, a well-structured modular architecture may be easier to maintain.

Document architectural decisions covering application components, data storage, interfaces, infrastructure requirements, and operational responsibilities.

Identify security weaknesses that should be resolved during modernization.

Review authentication, authorization, encryption, secrets management, logging, and access to sensitive information.

Evaluate third-party dependencies and establish processes for vulnerability scanning, patching, and security testing.

Regulatory and contractual requirements should also influence the target design.

Security controls should be applied throughout development and deployment rather than added shortly before release.

Define how the application will remain available and recover from failures.

Establish recovery time objectives (RTOs) and recovery point objectives (RPOs) based on business requirements.

Plan backup procedures, restoration testing, appropriate redundancy, and monitoring for critical components.

Validate that the target environment can support the required availability and recovery objectives.

Completion check: The target architecture has documented design decisions, security controls, compliance requirements, availability objectives, and recovery procedures.

Data migration is one of the most sensitive stages of legacy application modernization.

Legacy databases may contain inconsistent records, outdated formats, duplicated information, or relationships that newer systems handle differently.

A migration strategy needs to explain how data will be transferred, validated, and made available without compromising business operations.

Begin by reviewing the quality and structure of existing data.

Identify missing values, duplicate records, inconsistent formats, obsolete fields, and relationships that could create migration problems.

Document how existing data structures correspond to the target system.

For example, a legacy customer database might store contact details in a single field, while the new application requires separate fields for email addresses and phone numbers.

The migration process needs defined transformation rules to handle these differences.

Determine which records must be migrated, archived, retained for compliance, or excluded.

Choose a migration approach based on data volume, application availability requirements, and acceptable downtime.

Depending on the system, this may involve a scheduled migration during a maintenance window, staged transfers, or synchronization between old and new environments.

Identify integrations that will need modification.

Where applications exchange information through APIs, files, or messaging systems, verify that the modernized implementation supports the required data formats and business behavior.

If the legacy and modernized applications will operate simultaneously, define how data updates will be synchronized between them.

Test the migration process using representative datasets before transferring production data.

Validate record counts, field values, relationships, and critical business calculations.

For sensitive or business-critical information, use appropriate reconciliation methods, including integrity checks where applicable.

Document how inconsistencies will be identified and resolved.

Also establish what happens if migration fails or the application needs to return to its previous environment.

Completion check: Data mapping rules are approved, migration rehearsals have been completed, and reconciliation procedures can verify data accuracy.

Modernizing an entire application in a single major release can increase project risk, particularly when the system supports critical business operations.

An incremental approach allows teams to validate decisions, identify problems earlier, and limit the impact of failures.

Select a manageable component or workload to validate your modernization approach.

The pilot should test an important assumption, such as whether the target platform supports the application, whether a legacy integration can be replaced, or whether an architectural change improves performance.

Define success criteria before implementation.

A useful pilot should provide evidence that the approach is technically feasible and that the team can manage its operational requirements.

Use the findings to improve estimates, identify missing skills, and refine the broader implementation plan.

Divide the application into manageable migration or development units where its architecture allows.

One approach is the Strangler Fig pattern, which gradually replaces legacy functionality while the remaining parts of the original application continue operating.

For example, an organization might modernize customer notifications first, followed by reporting and then core transaction processing.

This can reduce the risks associated with replacing an entire system at once.

However, incremental modernization also requires careful coordination. Temporary integrations, duplicated components, and parallel systems can introduce complexity.

Each phase should have defined boundaries, validation requirements, and completion criteria.

Establish repeatable processes for developing, testing, and deploying application changes.

Continuous integration and continuous delivery (CI/CD) pipelines can automate builds, execute tests, and promote approved changes through environments.

Use version control, consistent configuration management, and infrastructure as code where appropriate.

Include security and quality checks in the delivery process to reduce the risk of introducing defects during frequent releases.

Completion check: The pilot has validated the selected approach, implementation is divided into manageable phases, and repeatable build, test, and deployment processes are established.

Modernization isn't complete when the new application runs successfully in a development environment.

It must perform the required business functions, communicate correctly with connected systems, and meet operational expectations before production rollout.

Develop a testing plan based on the application's critical workflows and identified risks.

Automate repeatable tests where practical, especially for critical business processes.

Compare results against the performance baselines and acceptance criteria established earlier.

Business users should verify that the modernized application supports their actual workflows.

User acceptance testing (UAT) should include routine operations, exception handling, reporting requirements, and tasks that depend on specialized business knowledge.

Use representative migrated data during testing.

Confirm that critical records, calculations, and reports remain accurate after the transition.

Resolve issues affecting essential operations before approving production deployment.

Prepare a documented deployment plan outlining the sequence of activities, responsible teams, communication channels, and expected service interruptions.

Choose a deployment method appropriate for the application, such as a phased rollout, canary release, or scheduled cutover.

Establish measurable conditions that would stop deployment or trigger a rollback.

Rollback planning must account for data changes made after cutover. Restoring an earlier application version doesn't automatically restore database consistency, especially if new transactions have already been processed.

Rehearse deployment and recovery procedures in a suitable non-production environment.

Completion check: Required tests have passed, business owners have approved acceptance criteria, and deployment, cutover, and recovery procedures have been validated.

The initial deployment marks the beginning of ongoing operation rather than the end of modernization.

The application needs continued monitoring and maintenance to ensure that expected improvements are sustained.

Compare post-modernization results against the baseline established at the beginning of the project.

Track metrics linked to your business objectives, such as application availability, maintenance costs, response times, deployment frequency, security vulnerabilities, and user satisfaction.

Use monitoring tools, logs, traces, and alerts to identify problems that may not be visible through basic availability checks.

Review performance after deployment and at agreed intervals to determine whether the modernization initiative is meeting its objectives.

Technical improvements may introduce changes to established workflows.

Provide relevant training, updated documentation, and support resources for users and operational teams.

Ensure that administrators and developers understand the new architecture, deployment processes, monitoring tools, and incident response procedures.

Collect user feedback to identify usability issues or missing functionality.

Use production data and operational feedback to guide further improvements.

Optimization may involve updating dependencies, improving database queries, adjusting infrastructure capacity, strengthening security controls, or simplifying application components.

Once replacement functionality is stable and approved, begin decommissioning components that are no longer needed.

Before retirement, confirm that dependent applications have been migrated, necessary records are retained, and archival or compliance obligations have been met.

Removing unused infrastructure and software can reduce maintenance costs and unnecessary security exposure.

Completion check: Monitoring and support responsibilities are established, modernization KPIs are reviewed, and unused legacy components have an approved retirement plan.

Organizations without the internal resources to manage legacy modernization may need support from an external software development company.

Depending on the project, a development partner can assist with application assessments, code refactoring, architecture redesign, system integration, data migration, and implementation.

Companies offering custom software development and application modernization services include:

Idea Maker: Provides custom software development and legacy application modernization services, including application assessments, code refactoring, architecture redesign, and cloud migration.

ScienceSoft: Offers custom software development, legacy software modernization, application reengineering, cloud migration, and system integration services.

Itransition: Provides custom application development, legacy application modernization, system integration, software migration, and post-modernization support.

Simform: Offers digital product engineering, software modernization, cloud and platform engineering, and data integration services.

These are examples of providers rather than a ranked comparison.

When evaluating a development partner, consider their experience with your existing technology stack, ability to handle complex integrations, approach to data security, and experience delivering projects with similar requirements.

Ask prospective providers how they assess legacy systems, manage migration risks, preserve critical business logic, and validate application functionality before deployment.

The main steps are defining business goals, assessing existing applications, mapping dependencies, choosing a modernization strategy, developing a roadmap, designing the target architecture, planning data migration, implementing changes, testing and deploying, and continuously optimizing the application.

The exact sequence may vary depending on the application's complexity and business requirements.

Start by evaluating the application's business value, technical condition, dependencies, and future requirements.

Rehosting or replatforming may be sufficient when the application functions well and infrastructure is the primary concern.

Refactoring or rearchitecting may be necessary when code quality or architectural limitations affect maintainability and scalability.

Rebuilding or replacing becomes an option when the existing implementation no longer meets business needs or would be too costly to maintain.

Compare expected benefits, implementation costs, risks, and ongoing maintenance requirements before making a decision.

Common risks include undocumented dependencies, data loss or corruption, unexpected downtime, security vulnerabilities, integration failures, and loss of essential business functionality.

Projects may also experience delays or budget overruns when technical complexity is underestimated.

These risks can be reduced through early assessment, phased implementation, migration rehearsals, comprehensive testing, and validated recovery procedures.

The timeline depends on the application's size, architecture, technical debt, data complexity, integrations, and selected modernization approach.

A limited rehosting or refactoring project generally requires less effort than rebuilding a large application or modernizing several interconnected systems.

A reliable estimate requires an initial technical assessment. A pilot or proof of concept can help validate assumptions about implementation effort and potential challenges.

Measure success against the business and technical objectives established before implementation.

Relevant indicators include application performance, availability, maintenance costs, security improvements, deployment speed, and user satisfaction.

For example, if reducing maintenance effort was the primary goal, lower support costs and faster resolution of application issues may be more meaningful than infrastructure performance alone.

Modernization success should be evaluated over time rather than only immediately after deployment.

Legacy application modernization works best when it follows a structured plan based on business needs and the realities of existing technology.

The ten steps in this checklist cover the key decisions and activities involved, from assessing legacy systems and selecting a modernization strategy to migrating data, validating functionality, and maintaining the resulting application.

Careful dependency mapping, realistic planning, incremental implementation, and thorough testing help reduce risks throughout the transition.

Start by assessing your existing applications and defining what modernization needs to achieve. Those findings will help you identify which systems deserve attention, where to invest resources, and how to approach modernization without introducing unnecessary complexity.

#10551. Google will fix Nest devices that didn’t adjust for Daylight Savings, breaking alarms and more

Google says it will roll out a fix for Nest devices that were impacted by problems with Daylight Savings adjustments due to changes in Canada.

A number of Nest Hub and Nest Thermostat users on Reddit noticed this week that their devices were showing the wrong time following the Daylight Savings time change.

This isn’t just a display bug, with real impacts including late alarms, delayed or broken automations, and altered schedules, among other impacts. The problem is specific to certain parts of Canada, user reports show, as changes in the country left some provinces skipping Daylight Savings changes while others kept the status quo. Google previously issued guidance on how to handle these changes on Android phones.

On Nest devices, though, adjusting time zones isn’t quite as easy, so this was a bigger headache.

Google is working on a fix, though, the company confirmed to Android Authority. In a statement, Google says that it is aware of the problem and working to resolve it.

Google is aware of an issue affecting a small subset of devices in some provinces in Canada where daylight savings is no longer observed. We apologize for any inconvenience and are actively working on a fix.

Google also replied to some affected users to confirm a fix is coming but, as of November 4, the fix does not appear to be in place.

#10549. Pocket-sized Ukrainian receiver intercepts Russian strike drone video feeds up to six miles away

This could help Ukrainian operatives see what Russian drone operators are seeing.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

CODE380, a radio manufacturer based in Kyiv, just built a new software-defined radio (SDR) that intercepts analog video signals that Russian drones transmit to their operators, making it easier for troops to find and intercept them. According to Defense News, the D·Vision SDR can sweep radio frequencies from 500 MHz to 8.7 GHz and convert any intercepted analog radio signal into a video feed. It has a detection range of up to six miles for signals on the 540 MHz to 6 GHz band, and up to two to three miles for higher frequencies.

Although this isn’t the first passive Ukraine-made drone detector, it’s one of the lightest models produced so far, weighing just 10 ounces or 290 grams — by comparison, an iPhone 18 Pro Max weighs about 8.78 ounces or 249 grams. Its competitors include the Vydun 7.5, which weighs around 2 pounds or 900 grams and could intercept signals at up to 7.5 GHz; the 23-ounce (650-gram) Chuyka 3.0, which can detect signals at up to 8.8 GHz; and the SENSE-4, which weighs 21 ounces or 600 grams and could detect drone signals of up to 8.2 GHz.

All these receivers cover the 4.1 to 4.5 GHz band that Russian Molniya strike drones began using in June of this year to transmit video back to their operators, Presidential advisor Serhii “Flash” Beskrestnov said. However, he also warned against relying on them for protection alone. “Most detectors don’t see these frequencies, or see them poorly,” he said in a social media post. “I’m warning you so that trust in a detector doesn’t cost you your life.” These receivers are often designed to work in tandem with other sensors, like radar, acoustic sensors, and drone interceptors, as part of an anti-drone defense system.

Most of these units are designed to intercept analog signals, meaning they won't be useful for intercepting video signals once Russia figures out how to use digital signals. Still, the D·Vision SDR is claimed to have digital UAV detection, although it’s only tuned towards DJI drones for now. It’s unclear what types of radios Russian drones use, though, so the company would have to recover downed units to inspect their hardware and build appropriate countermeasures. It will also be ineffective against fiber optic drones, which are both jam- and intercept-proof as they use a physical connection to communicate with their home base.

#10548. Microsoft says it now sucks less to use a Windows PC with 8GB of RAM

Using a computer with 8GB of RAM in 2026 is rough, to say the least, but as RAMageddon rages on, Microsoft says it is making improvements to using a Windows PC with less memory available.

In a blog post this week, Microsoft says that Windows PCs with 8GB of RAM are now 20% more responsive and 40% faster (in multitasking) in lab testing.

These are just a few of the updates Microsoft says it has made, with the company still working to get more out of memory compression and work with third-party developers to improve memory usage.

With RAMageddon’s effects only set to get worse in 2027 and problems thought to continue through 2030, this is all good news for end users. It’s also good seeing as Apple’s MacBook Neo triggered an avalanche of new 8GB Windows laptops designed to rival the lower price point.

#10545. Homelabber uses second-gen Optane memory sticks to run a 2.8-trillion-parameter AI model on CPU alone

It’s slow, it’s loud, but it runs on old Optane.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A homelab builder on Reddit has run Moonshot AI’s 2.8-trillion-parameter Kimi K3 AI model on discontinued second-gen Optane PMem 200 memory, using a 40-core 3rd-gen Xeon Scalable CPU and not a GPU for inference. The build runs hot, and the builder’s advice is that you “treat active DIMM cooling as mandatory,” with dual 12W blower fans aimed at the modules to get temperatures under control. The project, a follow-up to a separate user’s first-gen Optane build earlier this year, runs at a sluggish 0.85 tokens per second even with 1-bit quantization.

Kimi K3 is absolutely not built for home use, in our estimation, with Moonshot AI recommending data center inference engines to run it, and even the smallest community quantization needs at least 610GB of RAM. Using secondhand Optane, instead, is one of the few ways to reach that capacity at home. It doesn’t help that DRAM prices have skyrocketed, and the builder says the Optane memory costs “a fraction” of the equivalent.

Intel launched its Optane Persistent Memory 200 Series (PMem 200) in 2020 for its 3rd-gen Xeon Scalable CPUs in 128GB, 256GB, and 512GB capacities. On two-socket Xeon platforms, it runs at a higher effective MT/s rate than the first generation (PMem 100) that was used on the first project, and it can deliver an average of up to 32% more memory bandwidth. Optane can run in either Memory Mode or App Direct Mode; both builds had platforms allowing for each.

Memory Mode has the Optane act as system memory with DRAM as its cache, the method used in the first build, while App Direct exposes Optane as a separate, persistent region that the software maps directly, the mode used in the second. This can give “more control over where the model’s tensors live,” according to the first build’s creator, u/APFrisco, who planned to try it.

Performance does take a hit by going with Optane over DRAM, and with the higher 3-bit quantization, the speed went down even more to 0.75 tokens per second. One commenter points out that this is very slow, with a 50k-token task estimated to take 13 hours at about 1 token per second, but another notes that overnight batches don’t care as much about speed. Both speeds compare to the first build’s roughly 4 tokens per second, although in that case the builder had an RTX 3060 handling part of the model in addition to Memory Mode, a 2-bit quantization, and the smaller, earlier 1-trillion-parameter K2.5 model.

The latest builder also had to deal with heat output, with the Optane DIMMs getting “extremely hot” with sustained inference, creating a situation the builder says can endanger neighboring DRAM. That heat is consistent with Intel’s own specs, as the manufacturer rates the modules with forced airflow of 2.4 to 2.7 meters per second for cooling, which is what you expect to find in data centers. You might not normally have this level of airflow pointed directly at your memory sticks.

The expected operating conditions put each Optane module at 12W or 15W, regardless of capacity. If the build used four sticks, that would mean up to 60W of heat placed right next to the system memory. Throttling on these occurs at 83 degrees Celsius, with a thermal trip shutdown at 85 degrees, compared to the 85 degrees Celsius and up to 95 degrees in an extended range that typical DDR4 server memory is rated for. A lot of heat has to be removed from the system to keep things running.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The performance numbers given are pre-tuning — this means without tuning thread counts or NUMA affinity — so there is room for improvement, and one commenter also asked about using Memory Mode with DRAM caching for the most-used experts. The builder plans to add GPUs and to also load parts of the model into DRAM as a faster tier alongside the Optane, with “more benchmarks to come.”

#10542. PC's with 64GB of RAM plummet 18% as memory prices soar

AI gets the petabytes, while PCs get the scraps.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The latest CPU-Z hardware survey data shows 32GB and 64GB memory configurations down 2.5% and 18% year on year, respectively. The driving factor is no mystery: High-capacity memory has become a luxury in modern PC builds. The AI rush is still gobbling up the global DRAM supply, so prices for the best RAM options across DDR4 and DDR5 have gone through the roof.

If you are not familiar with CPU-Z, it is a free program that gives you a detailed breakdown of the hardware inside your system. It only tracks systems where it is actually installed and running, so it does not report monthly retail sales figures. Do not take the data as sales figures in any sense. They represent CPU-Z's active user base.

32GB and 16GB are common configurations that have grown over the years. According to the gathered data, 32GB configurations lead CPU-Z's user base with a 37.2% share, with 16GB configurations close behind at 33%. Not a surprise, really, since applications and games have grown more memory-intensive. 16GB has been the baseline for a long time now, and 32GB is now the sweet spot for many people, even gamers.

On the flip side, 8GB configurations have been steadily declining. They make up only 11% of the surveyed systems. 8GB systems are rare nowadays. You may still find them in legacy systems, basic office machines, or extremely budget-limited builds where performance takes a backseat. Meanwhile, higher-end configurations, including 64GB and 24GB, are still in the minority at 7.3% and 2.3%, respectively.

Fresh stats from CPU-Z! 🔎 For the first time ever, RAM capacity on PCs has declined year-over-year : -2.5% for 32 GB and -18% for 64 GB! https://t.co/ya0soA55bRNovember 6, 2026

For the first time, the CPU-Z developer recorded a noticeable decline in higher-capacity configurations. Specifically, 32GB configurations fell 2.5% year over year, while 64GB options dropped a significant 18%. This is likely due to the steep rise in memory prices, which has forced consumers to reconsider the cost-benefit of larger capacities.

As the price per gigabyte goes up, buyers are opting for more affordable capacities. Lower-capacity memory kits have seen a somewhat revival. The 16GB configuration, often considered the minimum, has rebounded by 12.9%. Similarly, 8GB configurations, which were previously in sharp decline, grew 5.5%.

Interestingly, 24GB configurations also increased by 19.7% and have become a compromise for consumers who need more than 16GB of memory but do not want to spend a lot of money on a premium 32GB memory kit.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The current distribution between DDR4 and DDR5 memory types reveals that DDR4 remains the dominant memory standard at 54.5%. In contrast, DDR5, despite being the latest memory technology, is still in its early adoption phase at 32.9%. DDR4 has been the de facto standard for almost a decade now, so it will take time for DDR5 to overtake it.

Nonetheless, the noticeable 6% growth in DDR4 and 2.9% decline in DDR5 suggests budget-conscious consumers are sticking with platforms that still embrace DDR4, such as AMD's Zen 3-powered Ryzen 5000 (codenamed Vermeer) or Intel's 13th Generation Raptor Lake processors. CPU-Z's processor charts show a significant increase in Zen 3 chips, including the Ryzen 7 5700X, Ryzen 5 5600, and Ryzen 5 5500, with figures up to 42.8%, 36.1%, and 67.3%, respectively.

Although both DDR4 and DDR5 have increased in price, the former remains slightly more affordable. Companies are supporting the effort too, such as AMD rereleasing the Ryzen 7 5800X3D, vendors restarting DDR4 and DDR4 motherboard production, or Intel preparing rewarmed Raptor Lake Next chips for 2027.

#10540. Three months in, Galaxy Z Fold 8’s cameras aren’t enough, and Fold 9 might not fix it [Gallery]

I’ve been enamored with my Galaxy Z Fold 8 since I started using it roughly three months ago, but its one big downside has been the cameras, so I’m not exactly thrilled to hear that the Galaxy Z Fold 9 might now make any progress here.

In my review of the Galaxy Z Fold 8, I called the cameras “the biggest mistake in this whole package,” and that opinion hasn’t changed after three months. The duo of 50MP sensors get the job done, but not as well as I want for such an expensive smartphone.

What I’ve found as I’ve continued to use this device that is that I don’t want to use these cameras. I’ll use them for cases where quality isn’t as important, like snapping pictures of my meals for my nutrition app, scanning documents, and things like that, but I’m always reaching for the phone in my other pocket for any other snaps. And that’s a privilege that not everyone has, nor should they be required to do so on their pricey foldable.

The downsides I’m most frequently running into include a lack of any quality zoom range – quality falls off a cliff beyond 2x – and problems with framing. The short design of the Galaxy Z Fold 8 means that, often, I’m capturing my finger at the bottom of the shot unintentionally.

The results through the main lens are perfectly fine, and I’m actually rather enjoying Samsung’s saturated colors as the Fall foliage cranks up, but I’m still constantly underwhelmed.

Foldable cameras have always been lesser compared to standard smartphones, but the Galaxy Z Fold 8’s lack of a third lens just makes that a tiny bit worse.

And yet, it sounds like Samsung is happy with the status quo. Some leakers are claiming that the Galaxy Z Fold 9 won’t make any changes to the camera sensors. A 50MP main and a 50MP ultra-wide. Fold 9 UItra might get a new main sensor, but no major changes to the hardware either.

To say that’s disappointing is an understatement, but if I can make one plea to Samsung, it’s to at least change the camera layout next time around. “Core identity” excuses aside, constantly needing to readjust my grip on the phone just to avoid taking a picture of my finger is frustrating, and having a new layout also opens the door to improvements on the table wobble and the whole Qi2 debacle. If we’re not getting new sensors, at least let’s get some physical improvements to the layout.
