#10539. Pin vs Noon in 2026: Which AI Recruiter Fits Your Team?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Buyers are moving from pilots to purchases: 52% of talent leaders told Korn Ferry they plan to add autonomous agents to their teams in 2026 (1,674 leaders, October 2025), and SHRM's 2026 research puts recruiting at 27% of organizations using AI, the most of any HR function. For a wider field, see our guide to the best AI recruiting agents.

Noon details come from Noon's own pages or a named third party, checked October 14, 2026.

Pin (pin.com) is an AI recruiting platform that assists recruiters by finding candidates across the 850M+ profiles Pin says it covers across North America and Europe, writing outreach from each person's profile signals and booking interviews. Candidate data comes from professional networks, GitHub, Stack Overflow, open-source contributions, patents, academic publications and the broader web. You search in plain language, paste a job description, upload ideal résumés or import a role from your ATS. Each ranked candidate has a fit score and an AI-written reason, and the model learns from your team's accept and reject feedback.

The Pin recruiting agent has two modes. In Assist you approve each step. In Autopilot it sources, screens and ranks, drafts outreach, sends the email steps, follows up on silence, schedules interviews and updates your ATS. LinkedIn and SMS steps arrive as tasks with an AI-drafted message, and you send them. Closing and offers stay human. The agent asks clarifying questions ("Is remote considered?"), shows every action in a feed and works 24/7 alongside your team. Josh Volinsky, Co-Founder of Synergy Search, says: "Pin makes sourcing candidates so natural and I love how you can just talk to it like it's a recruiter on your team and it goes hunting."

Noon (noon.ai) is an AI recruiting platform from Portal Inc. in New York whose agents source across the open web, run email and SMS outreach and book interviews. You start from a pasted job description or posting URL. Noon extracts the title, location and "non-negotiables," and its Autopilot sourcing agent keeps searching as new people enter the market. Candidates land in a ranked feed with reasoning, and thumbs-up and thumbs-down feedback calibrates the search per role.

Noon also extends further down the funnel. Its AI Interviewer runs voice screening calls, its Notetaker joins live interviews, and Inbound Screening ranks ATS applicants and flags suspected fraud. Those tools sit downstream of sourcing. Pin concentrates on the outbound workflow, from search through scheduling, and adds the team layer around it: a shared inbox, a dedupe layer so two recruiters never contact the same person, funnel dashboards and a multi-client CRM for agencies.

Pin gives you natural-language filters plus advanced filters you set yourself, and paid plans include unlimited searches.

Noon steers the search through criteria set per role. Its pages describe titles, locations, years of experience, target-company lists and must-haves, plus non-negotiables Noon says it never relaxes. You adjust by chat and feedback. We found no public description of an open-ended filter panel like Pin's, so ask for one in a demo if ad hoc searching matters.

On consistency, Pin returns a ranked shortlist with a reason for every candidate from the first search, then improves as your team accepts and rejects. Noon says its feedback loop calibrates the model per role, but it doesn't say how long that takes. Compare day one with day ten yourself (see the trial plan below).

Pin writes each message from the candidate's profile signals and calibrates to your writing voice. Its sequences run across email, LinkedIn and SMS: automated email steps plus AI-drafted LinkedIn and SMS tasks in the same sequence, which the recruiter sends. Sequences adapt per candidate, and any reply pauses the sequence. Free is email-only. Replies go to a shared team inbox, and a dedupe layer stops two recruiters contacting the same person.

Noon writes an intro for each candidate and drops it into sequence templates you control. Sequences run on email and SMS.

The practical difference is how contact data is packaged. Every Pin plan includes contact credits (5 a week on Free, 500 a month on Solo, 1,000 per user a month on Professional, unlimited on Business), and a personal email, work email or phone number uses 1 credit. Noon says contact enrichment is unlimited. Revealing email and phone for 300 candidates a month uses 600 of the 1,000 credits included with each Professional seat.

Pin's AI interview scheduling handles time zones, hiring panels and reschedules, and confirmations land in your ATS. Pin lists 120+ ATS integrations, including Greenhouse, Lever, Ashby, Bullhorn, iCIMS and Jobvite. ATS integration is an add-on on Solo and Professional and is included on Business. Teams without an ATS can track candidates in Pin's Kanban pipeline, a mini ATS from first touch to signed offer.

Noon's AI Coordinator answers candidate questions and books interviews through your Calendly availability, then handles reschedules. Its integrations page lists 40+ systems, including all six above plus Workday, SAP SuccessFactors, UKG, Loxo and Recruiterflow, and Noon says ATS sync is part of its plan. Several other Noon pages still say "20+," so confirm your ATS is live before you commit.

Pin publishes its pricing on its pricing page (checked October 14, 2026). Free is $0 (5 contact credits a week, 1 job, 1 agent, email-only sequences, 3 searches a day). Solo is $99 per user per month billed annually, for one seat, with 500 contact credits a month included. Professional is $135 per user per month billed annually or $179 month to month, with 2 agents and 1,000 contact credits per user each month, a shared inbox and 5 mailboxes per user. Business is custom, with unlimited seats, credits and agents and ATS integration included.

Noon sells one plan, quoted per team. Its pricing page lists unlimited sourcing, email enrichment, contacts, agents and seats, with no credits or per-seat fees. Its get-started page invites teams to create a free account, yet most "Start free" buttons open a demo-booking calendar, and Noon does not say what the free account includes.

Worked example: three recruiters on Pin Professional

With Noon, you won't know the number until after a sales call. Noon prices one flat plan per team and says adding seats doesn't change it, while Pin's cost is known before you talk to anyone and scales predictably per seat. Get Noon's quote in writing and compare it against these figures, keeping in mind that Noon's bundle includes screening and note-taking tools you may not need.

Noon also offers a 10x ROI guarantee on annual plans. According to Noon's guarantee terms (checked October 14, 2026), it values recruiter time at $60 an hour, relies on Noon's own measurement of time saved and requires minimum weekly use.

Pin is SOC 2 Type 2 audited by a third party, encrypts data at rest and in transit, and is GDPR and CCPA ready, with documents in its trust center at trust.pin.com. SSO/SAML and SCIM come with the Business plan.

Noon says it holds SOC 2 Type II and GDPR compliance, runs an annual third-party penetration test and supports SSO and SAML. According to its security page (updated September 3, 2026), Noon also offers data residency options for enterprise agreements and makes the SOC 2 report and pen-test summary available under NDA. The page does not mention CCPA.

Pin is built for agencies scaling outbound, and for recruiters and teams of any size that want a known price and hands-on control. It brings a multi-client recruiting CRM, per-seat pricing that scales predictably and a Business plan with unlimited seats. Pin assists recruiters rather than replacing them. Noon's homepage claims 40% faster time to hire, a vendor-reported figure.

Noon is good for teams with heavy inbound application volume that want voice screening and interview notes bundled with sourcing, and that are comfortable buying through a sales quote. The caveats are an unpublished price and free-account details, and only three reviews on Trustpilot.

When Pin is not the right fit: teams that only need high-volume inbound applicant screening.

When Noon is a harder fit: buyers who need a price before a sales call, teams that want a free plan with published details, and anyone who wants independent reviews to check (three on Trustpilot, no G2 listing found).

Choose Pin if you: - want a published price and a free plan with no credit card - want to start in Assist mode and hand steps to Autopilot as you build trust - run a team where several recruiters share candidates and need dedupe and a shared inbox - run an agency and need pipelines per client

Choose Noon if you: - want one quoted plan that Noon says does not change with seats or lookups - need voice screening, a notetaker, inbound screening or fraud detection in the same product - are comfortable buying through a demo and a custom quote

Replacing LinkedIn Recruiter? See our LinkedIn Recruiter alternatives. Pin's Noon AI alternatives roundup covers seven more tools.

Pin's Free plan is email-only, so run the full email, LinkedIn and SMS task flow on a paid plan; Professional is available month to month at $179 per user.

For recruiters, agencies and outbound recruiting teams, Pin is the better choice: published pricing, a free plan, Assist and Autopilot modes, a shared inbox with dedupe, and a multi-client CRM for agencies. Noon fits teams that need voice screening and inbound fraud checks bundled with sourcing, though it publishes no price. Pin's Pin vs Noon breakdown covers the matchup in more detail.

Noon does not publish a price. It sells one plan with unlimited seats, contacts and agents, quoted per team after a demo. Pin lists prices openly: Free, Solo at $99 per month billed annually, and Professional at $135 per user per month billed annually ($179 month to month). Three recruiters on Pin Professional cost $4,860 a year on annual billing.

Not clearly. Noon's get-started page offers a free account, but most "Start free" buttons open a demo-booking calendar and Noon does not publish what the account includes. Pin's Free plan is public: 5 contact credits a week, 1 job, 1 agent, email-only sequences and 3 searches a day, with no credit card.

For recruiters and teams that want published pricing and a free start, Pin (pin.com) is the Noon AI alternative built for exactly that. Pin says it searches 850M+ profiles, offers Assist and Autopilot modes and integrates with 120+ ATS platforms. For the full field, see the best AI recruiting agents.

Noon is an operating product with a weekly changelog, but independent review volume is small: Trustpilot showed three positive reviews in October 2026, and we found no G2 listing. That is too few to judge, so run a trial and ask for references. Pin has 4.8/5 on G2 across 27 verified reviews, per pin.com. For more on Noon's review record, see Pin's Noon AI reviews guide.

For recruiters and agencies that want a known price and control over how much the agent does, Pin is the better fit, starting with a free plan. Noon is worth a demo if inbound volume is your main problem and you want voice screening and fraud checks bundled in; get its price in writing first. Test both on live roles before you decide.

#10537. The first Snapdragon phone with Android Linux Terminal support is here

The Redmagic 12 Pro is the first Snapdragon-based Android phone to support the Linux Terminal introduced in Android 16 for Pixel phones.

Android power users who want a new Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6-powered phone like the Redmagic 12 Pro now have another reason to be excited about the latest Qualcomm processors: the Android Linux Terminal.

Thanks to tipster Dylan H, who wrote in asking about compatibility. After enabling the Android Linux Terminal and running a few commands to check compatibility, I found out that the Redmagic 12 Pro does, indeed, support the Linux Terminal app. I’ve written Qualcomm asking about wider Snapdragon support for Linux Terminal and will update this article if I receive a response.

Google introduced the Terminal app as a power user feature in the Android 16 update for Pixel phones, and Samsung added support in One UI 8.5 for Exynos-powered Galaxy S26 models. So far, however, Snapdragon phones have not appeared on the compatibility list.

this is super cool! i guess my first question is if it is subject to the Android 32 subprocess limit that effectively cripples the terminal on Googlebooks.

Linux Terminal support has been slowly building on Google platforms. Googlebooks sort of support it, and now it appears that more Android phones will support it as well. The implementation on the Redmagic 12 Pro seems slightly different from what’s on Exynos-powered Samsung phones, as you cannot adjust the disk size (it’s locked at 132GB available), but you can adjust the amount of memory available for the terminal to use.

By default, Terminal has access to 1GB of memory, although the Redmagic 12 Pro lets you increase that to 10.6GB. You can also adjust the display resolution and enable Keep Awake in advanced settings. The Redmagic 12 Pro also supports the GUI feature, so programs like GIMP should work if you can get them installed (I kept running into 404 errors using the standard installation command).

The Redmagic 12 Pro goes up for preorder on November 12 and includes several freebies and a launch discount before November 17. See our full review to find out if it’s right for you.

Is the U.S. Department of Commerce simply rubber stamping these applications?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

An investigation has found that addresses for two Chinese companies approved to purchase Nvidia H200 chips led to empty offices, and the receptionists at the buildings were unfamiliar with them. According to The New York Times, these companies didn’t just have empty offices; their executives are often linked to other firms sanctioned by the United States for working with and providing advanced technology to the Chinese military.

This news has revealed concerns that the U.S. Department of Commerce is simply rubber-stamping GPU export license applications instead of scrutinizing each one for potential links to Beijing. “I have a ton of concerns that neither the Commerce Department or Nvidia is doing the due diligence needed to ensure that the licensing terms are being followed,” former China director for the White House National Security Council Liza Tobin told the publication.

Many of these approved companies include well-known entities like Baidu, BYD, Tesla Shanghai, as well as ZTE, which has previously been blacklisted from the U.S. market, Alibaba, Tencent, and ByteDance. However, the outfit’s investigation also revealed approval for two relatively unknown firms: New Matrix and Maginfra. The offices for both companies were empty shells with some office furniture, and people working nearby said they’d never heard of the company or seen anyone go to the deserted spaces.

The New Matrix website also reportedly did not contain any information about its owners or its history, although public company records reveal that it was founded by Li Bin, who, along with two other primary owners, are connected to Wise Road, a Chinese private equity firm sanctioned by the U.S. for helping Beijing acquire sensitive chip technologies. Furthermore, Mr. Li chairs Tsinghua Unigroup, a tech conglomerate partly owned by the Chinese government, and owns New H3C Semiconductor Technologies, which has also been added to the Commerce Department’s Entity List for cooperating with the Chinese military.

Maginfra, on the other hand, is suspected of being linked to Inspur, which Washington has blacklisted since 2023 for supporting the People’s Liberation Army, with both companies sharing personnel, patents, and a similar government ownership structure. These two firms aren’t listed as the final users of Nvidia’s AI chips, though. Instead, they’re supposed to assemble them into AI servers, which would then be forwarded to a final user, although it’s unclear who their customers are.

Aside from these questionable non-entities, another company that received a license to purchase H200 GPUs has some suspicious links. Zhejiang Yindun Cloud Technology’s executives are reportedly related to the founder of Range Intelligent Computing Technology Group, a data center operator that has sold compute capacity to Chinese military contractors. It’s also reportedly partly operating out of a Range data center.

President Donald Trump banned the export of Nvidia AI GPUs in April 2025, even those customized to meet the performance ceiling Washington set for AI processors. The president reversed the decision late last year in exchange for a 25% cut of the sale, but it still took more than eight months for the first H200 shipments to arrive in China. Beijing’s refusal to give Chinese tech companies the green light to buy the chips for the first few months of the year caused the delay. But once China lifted its import ban on Nvidia chips, several Chinese companies applied for licenses to buy them, and The New York Times reported that around two dozen firms have already received approval, including these questionable ones.

The people were protesting against the use of fossil fuel generators to power data centers.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Amsterdam police have arrested 150 to 200 protesters at a Microsoft data center site on suspicion of trespassing. According to a Facebook post by the authorities [machine translated], tools designed for breaking into the site were seized from the protesters, who were then allowed on their way to protest peacefully. However, it appears that some members of the group decided to enter the construction site “with violence” anyway, forcing the police to intervene and leading to their arrest. There were also some allegedly stolen items from the construction site found among the belongings of some of the protesters, plus damage and vandalism done to the data center site.

This protest is a continuation of the action that started last Friday, where protesters breached the Microsoft data center, which was being owned by Pure Data Centers. It is also the first of many organized by the “Stop Dirty Data Centers” coalition across Europe, with actions planned for Amsterdam, Dublin, Warsaw, Denmark, and several other cities across the continent. The Dutch publication NOS [machine translated] identified the exact group at the site today as Fossielvrij, who are against the use of fossil fuels in data centers such as the jet engines that Elon Musk installed at xAI’s Mississippi data center.

“Gas prices are going through the roof, and new houses are being postponed because of the overcrowded power grid. Yet tech billionaires like Elon Musk and Jeff Bezos are building energy-guzzling, hideous data centers,” the group said on its website. “We can take back the power of Big Tech by pressing the pause button now. Give us the opportunity to democratically decide what we do and do not use AI for, so that it is not at the expense of our living environment and everything that makes our lives beautiful. Then we look at how many more data centers are needed.”

The protest comes amid the AI data center build-out, which has been facing increased opposition across the United States. A Google data center site in Finland is also in trouble after it cleared out around 1,300 acres worth of forest and trees without an environmental impact assessment, displacing wildlife, and leading to a criminal probe on Tuike Finland Oy, the Google subsidiary in charge of the site.

Fossielvrij’s action was supposed to be peaceful, but it appears that another group joined the demonstration and broke through the fences around the site, leading to the police confrontation. The suspects were arrested for domestic violence and then taken to a cell complex, where it will be decided if they will be detained longer. The police also said that “All suspects will be heard afterward.”

#10530. Beware the Slasher: AI Could Kill Off Cybersecurity Pros' Skills

Nathan Hamiel outlines potential SecOps horror in the AI midst, as core technical capabilities go the way of reading an atlas or remembering phone numbers.

Black Hat India — Bengaluru — The use of AI as a tool to enhance cybersecurity operations is emerging as a double-edged knife for security professionals. While it can make them more capable, it could also spell the demise of the very skills they need to use it effectively.

That's the concern of Nathan Hamiel, senior director of research at Kudelski Security, who has made a case for how evolving technology can erode technical skills since well before the generative AI boom. Now, that concern is getting much louder.

Speaking to Dark Reading recently, he says he began noticing the problem around 2005 and 2006, when he encountered cybersecurity professionals who lacked a firm grasp of the fundamentals of their work. At that time, he warned about the dangers of relying too heavily on various types of technical tools, declaring to cybersecurity professionals that "your tools are killing you," and warning that practitioners are moving away from being "hands-on" operators who investigate problems directly, and toward "hands-off" monitors who oversee results presented through tools, interfaces, and dashboards.

Related:Black Hat India 2026 Makes a Landmark Debut in Bengaluru, Bringing the Global Cybersecurity Community to India

It's a shift that's being exacerbated now by AI-driven automation, he says, and organizations need to move quickly to optimize the use of AI without the expense of cybersecurity skill-building.

While automation can simplify complex tasks and allow professionals to accomplish more, it can also distance them from the underlying problems they are trying to solve. Without a solid understanding of the fundamentals, practitioners may struggle to recognize when a tool produces an incorrect result or when its findings require further investigation, Hamiel says.

"We need to keep these skills and not let them degrade because those skills are important to making the tools work in the best way possible," he tells Dark Reading from this week's Black Hat India conference, where he's giving a keynote entitled "The Death of Skills and the Persistence of Problems."

"What AI adds to somebody with knowledge and experience is some additional capabilities," Hamiel elaborates. "A domain expert wielding AI is going to be able to gain benefits from using that tool that a novice with no domain experience isn't going to be able to get."

This distinction is becoming increasingly important as AI becomes more deeply integrated into cybersecurity workflows. It also raises questions about how the industry will develop its next generation of experts if automation removes some of the hands-on tasks that junior professionals traditionally learn.

Related:PwC's CISO on AI, Robots & What's Next for Security

The current discussion around AI often falls into extremes, Hamiel says. Some predict that the technology can go a long way toward solving major problems facing not only cybersecurity but also humanity, while others warn that it will bring the industry — and some even the world — to an end.

"You have the doomers," he says, "and you have people who say this is a big nothingburger. The truth is somewhere in the middle."

For Hamiel, understanding AI's impact requires separating what the technology can demonstrate in a controlled environment from what it can reliably accomplish in the real world. Impressive capabilities do not necessarily translate into the wholesale replacement of jobs or the disappearance of entire industries, he says.

The same distinction applies to cybersecurity, Hamiel says. Although AI can help identify vulnerabilities, generate code and support security investigations, those capabilities do not mean that the complex problems facing security teams have suddenly become easy to solve.

In fact, some recent incidents described as "rogue" AI activity — such as OpenAI models autonomously hacking the Hugging Face framework, among others — may reveal a more familiar and persistent cybersecurity problem: organizations failing to address basic security weaknesses.

Related:NIST: Solo Shouldn't Mean Less Secure

"If you read some of the incident reports, these are organizations that weren't exercising basic security hygiene," Hamiel says.

In these cases, he argues, the weaknesses were already there, waiting to be discovered. "This wasn't just low-hanging fruit; this was fruit lying on the ground where anybody who looked for it would have found it," he says.

Rather than signaling an impending cybersecurity apocalypse, such incidents could prompt what Hamiel calls a "cybersecurity correction."

"It's not that there is this huge apocalypse happening; it's that many people weren't doing the right thing for a very long time," he explains.

In other words, AI may make it easier to discover and exploit existing weaknesses, exposing the consequences of inadequate security practices. The challenge for organizations is not simply to adopt new tools but to address the underlying problems those tools bring to light.

Indeed, the potential erosion of technical skills is not a problem that began with generative AI. Hamiel sees it as part of a longer trend in which technology increasingly mediates the relationship between professionals and the problems they are responsible for solving.

Hamiel's concern reflects what is often called the irony of automation: As systems take on more responsibility, the humans overseeing them can lose opportunities to practice the skills needed when those systems fail.

The consequences could extend beyond the skills of today's practitioners to the way the cybersecurity industry develops future experts, he says, especially in how lower-level work that AI can eliminate helps helping junior professionals learn.

Tasks that might appear repetitive or straightforward can provide essential experience, allowing new practitioners to understand how systems work before progressing to more complex investigations. "If you automate that, there isn't room for them to learn," Hamiel warns.

This creates a potential long-term problem for employers. AI may allow teams to complete certain tasks more efficiently today, but organizations still need experienced professionals who can investigate unfamiliar problems, evaluate automated findings, and respond when systems behave unexpectedly.

For cybersecurity pros to use generative AI effectively, then, it's simply a matter of applying it to tasks where it offers meaningful benefits while remaining aware of the skills that might be weakened if pros rely too much on these tools to do their jobs effectively.

"The goal here is to maximize the benefits and minimize the drawbacks," Hamiel says, something that is true for the use of any technology. "That's the framing that should be placed around this," he says.

Thus, the only way that organizations can adopt AI without losing essential cybersecurity expertise is to "apply them to the right types of activities," he says.

For cybersecurity pros, this means using generative AI for tasks such as threat hunting and threat intelligence, where preliminary analysis can help professionals work through information more efficiently, Hamiel notes. AI-powered penetration-testing tools are another area of promising development, extending beyond traditional vulnerability scanning to perform more complex tasks, he says.

Ultimately, the question is not whether AI will change cybersecurity, Hamiel says. That genie is most certainly already out of that bottle. Ultimately, the real challenge is ensuring that the industry does not become so dependent on increasingly capable tools that it loses the human expertise required to use them effectively, he says.

"Skills and expertise make your AI use more effective," Hamiel concludes. "If you have two professionals, and both use AI, the one with more knowledge of the task is going to make better use of the AI."

Elizabeth Montalbano is freelance writer, editor, and journalist with 30 years of professional experience and a master's degree from Arizona State University. Her areas of expertise include enterprise technology, cybersecurity, business, and culture. During her long career, Elizabeth has lived and worked as a full-time journalist in Phoenix, San Francisco, and New York City. She specializes in news coverage and analysis, using her years of experience to look at the current state of cybersecurity with a critical gaze. She currently resides in a village on the southwest coast of Portugal, where in her free time she enjoys surfing, hiking with her dogs, growing plants, and playing and performing as a singer and musician.

#10529. How to Build a Typed AI Pipeline With Jev and Clairic.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A support email lands: "Order NW-47790, the tent poles snapped on the first night, I paid $389 and I want every cent back today or I'm disputing the charge."

To act on that, your code needs answers to a handful of small questions. Is it a refund request? How urgent is it? Should a person handle it? What's the order number? How much money is involved? It's the same problem whether you're sorting a support inbox or building a custom chatbot that has to work out what each customer is asking for.

The usual move is to send the whole email to a chat model with a prompt that ends "respond in JSON", then validate whatever comes back. It works, but you're paying a model built to write essays to fill in five fields, and you're parsing its guess at a schema.

System One models are built for exactly this kind of job. In this tutorial we'll build a small support inbox pipeline with two of them: Jev from TypeSafe makes the decisions, and Clairic from Cloudflare reads the values. Plain TypeScript then turns their answers into an action. No prompt engineering for output formats, and no JSON repair.

The name comes from Daniel Kahneman's Thinking, Fast and Slow. System 1 is the fast, intuitive kind of thinking you use to read a face or a sign. System 2 is the slow, deliberate kind you use for long division.

A System One model is an AI model that understands natural language but doesn't generate text. You give it some input (the state) and a set of typed questions, and it gives back typed answers with probabilities. There's no free text to parse, because the model can only answer in the shape you asked for.

That's different from asking a chat model for structured output. SitePoint's guide to LLM structured output with JSON Schema shows how to constrain a chat model and validate its JSON with Zod. With a System One model, the type is the interface: a yes/no question comes back as a probability, a pick-one question comes back as one of your options, and a date comes back as a date.

Jev and Clairic are both System One models, but they do different jobs, and that's what makes them a good pair.

Jev can't hand you an order number, and Clairic has no way to pick from a list of options or answer yes or no. Give each one the job it's built for.

If you've read SitePoint's Jev and LangChain tutorial, you've seen Jev deciding which model handles an agent's request. Here we'll use it on the incoming text itself.

Five made-up emails for a made-up outdoor gear store go in. For each one:

Both models read the same email, so they run at the same time.

Put the three values in your environment:

No SDKs are needed. Both models are a single fetch call.

Here's the test inbox. Each email is there to test a different path through the rules:

Jev takes a state (the email) and a set of named questions. We'll ask three, one of each type:

All three questions go in one request. Jev reads the email once and answers every question in parallel, so adding a question costs very little:

We pin jev-1.14.0 rather than the jev-latest alias. Your thresholds get tuned against one version's probabilities, so you want to choose when that version changes.

For the angry tent email, Jev came back with:

(Trimmed: the real response also includes the probability of every option and level.)

Clairic takes the same state, plus a set of typed fields instead of questions:

reference_date anchors relative dates like "the 14th" or "last Monday". In production, pass the date the email arrived.

Each answer comes back with three things worth using:

For the "where is it?" email, Clairic read the deadline like this:

"The 14th" became a full date, worked out from the reference date. Text fields like order_number also come back as character spans of the original email. The value is copied from the input, not generated, so it can't invent an order number that isn't there.

The two models don't depend on each other, so send both requests together:

The time for each email is whichever model is slower, not the two added together.

This is the part a chat model usually ends up doing badly: the business rules. Here they're ordinary code you can read, test, and change without touching a prompt:

Notice that the model's confidence sits right next to the business rule. A $18.95 refund is only automatic if Clairic is sure about both the order number and the amount. Raising the refund limit or the confidence bar is a one-line change, and you don't need to call either model again to see what it would do. Save the answers and replay them.

Every email went where it should. The whole run used 2,703 input tokens on Jev and 2,292 on Clairic, about US$0.0003 in total. At that rate, 10,000 support emails would cost roughly US$0.70.

The interesting part of the run wasn't the right answers. It was the two wrong ones.

"From last Monday." The run used 2 November 2026 as the reference date, which is a Monday. "Last Monday" means 26 October, but Clairic answered 2026-11-02, the reference date itself. Its confidence on that answer was 0.54, compared with 0.99 or higher on almost every other value in the run.

"A couple of weeks ago." Clairic turned this into 2026-10-19, which is a fair reading but still a guess. Its confidence was 0.43.

Neither date changed any action, because the rules only trust values at 0.9 or above. That's the habit to build: a value under your bar counts as unknown. A chat model returning JSON would have handed you "2026-11-02" with the same certainty as everything else.

One more catch: Clairic copied the order number as #NW-48213, hash included, because that's how the customer wrote it. Text values are spans of the input, so normalising them is your code's job. That's one line of code.

System One models handle the reading and the routing. They don't hold a conversation. Once an email is routed, something still has to write the reply, ask a follow-up question, or explain a returns policy in the customer's own words. That's where a chat model earns its cost.

The split that works is: System One models decide, code applies the rules, and a chat model only gets the cases where words matter.

We built a support inbox triage pipeline from two small models and some ordinary code:

Each email took about half a second and cost less than a hundredth of a cent. Most importantly, the two answers that would have been wrong came back flagged as unsure.

The full code is about 160 lines with no dependencies. Try adding a fourth Jev question (does the customer mention a photo?) or an estimate field in Clairic (how many days until the trip?), and see how little the rest of the pipeline has to change.

Open source tool is available for Windows, Linux, and macOS.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Microsoft VP Scott Hanselman has revealed a new open-source GitHub project called gputaskman. The name neatly encapsulates the headline role of this tool, a GPU-focused task manager, but its scope might be broader than you expect. This new tool for Windows, Linux, and macOS can show per-process GPU usage, VRAM consumption, active runtimes, and many more stats. It also breaks out AI/LLM task-specific information.

There's a lot going on in my GPU. I'm making a GPU Taskman to get insights to where my GPU memory is going. PRs welcome https://t.co/6FwKqnFWDWNovember 3, 2026

On social media, Hanselman snappily makes clear the technical itch that gputaskman was designed to scratch. “There's a lot going on in my GPU,” wrote the prominent public face of Microsoft's developer ecosystem. “I'm making a GPU Taskman to get insights to where my GPU memory is going.”

There are several example screenshots of GPU Taskman in use by the Microsoft VP. They clearly show the kinds of data that are presented in the terminal-like display that pops up in the tool. Hanselman’s tweet shows that the most VRAM-hungry app on his RTX 4080S system is the desktop windows manager. That is selected in the list, and more details are shown for this task at the bottom of the window. Also, we see AI/LLM apps consuming VRAM, and a few Windows staples.

In a GitHub-embedded image, we see what details are broken out when the llama-server.exe app is selected in the list. The bottom of the window breakdown reveals that Qwen3-0.6B is being used, and there’s a breakdown of its weights, the KV cache, the number of parameters, and more. Very informative.

As the Microsoft developer has made portable Windows downloads of the latest v0.3.0 release of gputaskman, I couldn’t resist giving it a spin. Upon launch, I found that no tasks were using the discrete GPU in my laptop… so I fired up ye olde Unigine Valley GPU benchmark to stir the RTX 4060 from its slumber. I note the live-streaming charts showing GPU load, VRAM use, temperature, and so on are more prominent in my screenshot than in Hanselman’s examples. Perhaps he screen-shotted a previous version of the tool.

There’s a lot more information about gputaskman, its inner workings, and even its source code, on the linked GitHub repository. Hanselman is sharing his “experimental software” tool under the permissive MIT license. Finally, in social media comments, he hints that expanded GPU monitoring capabilities could be coming to Windows as standard.

You can now ‘Rediscover the thrill of unboxing a GeForce legend’ in a Steam game, but an RTX 5080 is recommended for this path-traced tech demo.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

On this day in 2006, Nvidia released the legendary GeForce 8800 GTX and 8800 GTS graphics cards. Nvidia has decided to honor the flagship of the pair with its GeForce 8800 GTX - 20 Years of a Legend interactive demo, now free on Steam. However, the recommended specs to ‘play’ with this semi-interactive path-traced unboxing experience include a wallet-worrying RTX 5080. Or you could just watch the video, embedded below for your convenience.

The downloadable demo, which also requires 32GB of RAM and 30GB of free storage, was created by Dylan Browne, a senior special effects artist who frequently works with Unreal Engine, as in this case. This experience is said to deliver era-appropriate graphics, but requires the most advanced graphics tech available two decades after the 8800 GTX first hit the scene to work its graphical magic.

To run GeForce 8800 GTX - 20 Years of a Legend on a 2026 PC, you will need at least an RTX 4070 for acceptable performance, according to the Steam page. This software is stuffed with the latest graphics tech embraced by the green team. “DLSS 4.5 with Dynamic Multi Frame Generation, Ray Reconstruction, path tracing, and Mega Geometry” are all present, says Nvidia.

There’s a bit more to the interactive demo than looking around and poking a box with your mouse and then prodding it to fit the shiny new GPU into your “era-accurate PC tower.” An extra layer of fun is promised by the provision of hidden Easter eggs in this experience.

However, the demo appears to show Windows Vista on the PC in the gaming den, which launched at retail a few months after the 8800 GTX. Admittedly, this legendary GeForce card and Windows Vista OS would roam the Earth at the same time, with many folks buying the GeForce 8800 GTX and 8800 GTS to shove into their Vista PCs from 2007 onwards. Crysis launched on PCs in late 2007, a year after the G80 architecture cards debuted.

Nvidia launched the GeForce 8800 GTX (128 shader units) and 8800 GTS (96 shader units) at $599 and $449, respectively. These legendary Nvidia GPUs spearheaded the DirectX 10 era, accelerating existing PC titles and delivering next-gen visuals with new DX10 software. You can read more in our reviews from the time, like the 12-page review of the GeForce 8800 GTX and GTS from the launch week. Briefly, our reviewer thought these cards were “amazing” for their current performance and DX10 promise. They were also excited about the G80 architecture for bringing CUDA to consumers and the promise of making the GPU “something much more than a frame rendering device.” Some Luddite gamers may wish that had never happened.

Nvidia driver support for the GeForce 8800 GTX and GTS would run out of road about a decade later. By that time, DirectX 11 hardware and games were mainstream, leaving older graphics hardware designs obsolete when it came to playing the latest AAA PC titles.

#10521. ‘Enough Phone’ is an Android phone roughly the size of iPhone 13 Mini, launching in 2027

Everyone says they want small Android phones back, and 2027 might be your chance to actually buy one. The “Enough Phone” has a mere 5.3-inch display, decent specs, and a price of $599 and less.

Launching in May 2027, the “Enough Phone” is a 5.3-inch Android phone that simply does want to be “enough.”

The company says the 5.3-inch LCD display comes in at 1520×720 and that the phone will be powered by a MediaTek Dimensity 7500 chipset, 8GB of RAM, and offers 128GB of storage. It’ll come with either “stock Android” or OdysseyOS, an Android skin used by SLEKE and designed to be minimal and act like a modern dumbphone. SLEKE currently sells repurposed Pixel phones running this software.

Android 17 will run out of the box with updates through 2032.

“Enough Phone” measures in at 132.8 x 63.8 x 12mm and will have a battery between 3,500 and 4,000 mAh which will be user-replaceable. While it’s thicker, the physical size is roughly the same as iPhone 13 Mini.

Rounding out the package would be a fingerprint sensor in the power button, a single 50MP (Sony IMX766) camera on the back, and a 32MP selfie camera up front.

The only redeemable aspect of this phone is the small display but nothing about it makes it a good value in the 2nd half of 2027 when you have much more capable phones like the Pixel A series for nearly the same money or a bit more but at least you're getting a lot more phone for your money.

The company says that these specs are “mostly final” and that pricing is planned for $599.

“Enough Phone” is funding on Kickstarter with $399, $449, and $499 early bird reservation tiers ahead of the May 2027 shipping estimate.

#10519. Google’s latest smart home products make the perfect gifts, and they’re on sale at Best Buy

Google Home is one of the most user-friendly smart home ecosystems, and Google has built out an extensive collection of cameras, speakers, and displays. That positions them as great gifts to expand someone’s smart home.

It’s pretty common to get one or two smart home devices and call it a day, but ecosystems like Google Home begin to come alive when there are several devices working in harmony with each other. For instance, you can start out with the Google Nest Learning Thermostat (4th gen), as many do. It makes A/C and heat controls easy, and even saves you money along the way.

But adding something like the Google Home Speaker with Gemini built-in means you get even more control of your home’s indoor environment. You could go a step further and add a Google Nest Cam (indoor or outdoor) or a Google Nest Doorbell, whether wired or battery. It’s a bit pricier, but I love the Google Nest Cam with Floodlight as a smart home gift option for someone, since it provides more utility.

In any case, adding a couple more foundational pieces to your smart home makes it more useful. And as Google adds features with Gemini for Home, users get access to smart alerts and notifications that those cameras didn’t provide a year or two ago, all within the Google Home app.

If you’re gifting for someone who doesn’t have the beginnings of a smart home setup, Google’s Nest products are a good start. You could start with the Google Home Speaker, depending on their needs. If they like listening to music and want a smart home assistant at the ready, Google’s latest speaker delivers.

The Google Home Speaker has a 360-degree speaker that throws audio in every direction, filling the room. It controls other devices as that smart home fills out, and it can even act as part of a stereo setup for the Google TV Streamer.

Right now, the Google Home Speaker is at its lowest price on Best Buy, sitting at $79.99 – $20 off. It comes in some great colorways, too, like Berry and Jade. Of course, there’s Porcelain and Hazel for more toned-down looks.

Another great smart home starter gift is the Nest Learning Thermostat (4th gen) or Nest Thermostat. The difference between the two comes down to look and capabilities, but both work very well.

The Nest Learning Thermostat (4th gen) works with the majority of HVAC systems and converts them into one manageable system through the Google Home app. Over time, it learns your heating and cooling habits to form better schedules that save you money and stress.

Right now, the Nest Learning Thermostat (4th) gen is down to $219 from $279 at Best Buy – $60 off. That’s the best price we’ve seen from the store, and it’s a good deal overall.

Best Buy is also offering a couple of bundles if you want to go further. You can add Nest Cams to your cart with the Nest Learning Thermostat for $100 off the order. If you want to bundle a couple of items into a gift for someone –or yourself – that’s the way to go.

Of course, you can’t go wrong with the Nest Doorbell cameras (wired or battery). Go for the battery edition if you’re limited on wiring options or want to use it at an apartment. Wired is the better choice for homes, since it makes 24/7 recording possible for more security.

You can also check out Best Buy’s other offers on Google’s smart home products. It might be worth bookmarking as deals appear during Black Friday.

Commanders needed a combat robot to operate in underground gas pipelines, tunnels, trenches, and similar environments.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Ukraine is developing a new class of combat robots designed to operate inside underground gas pipelines, where Russian troops have repeatedly attempted to bypass frontline defenses, United24Media reports. The project, disclosed by Ukraine's 2nd National Guard Corps, Khartiia, involves modifying unmanned ground vehicles (UGVs) to perform reconnaissance and combat missions in spaces too narrow for conventional military machinery. Notably, the work is being carried out by specialists from the corps’ Lava Unmanned Systems Regiment, following Operation Veles — an underground assault that saw Ukrainian soldiers advance more than 15 kilometers through pipeline infrastructure near Kupiansk. Khartiia says the operation killed more than 100 enemy soldiers and resulted in over 30 captured, although those battlefield figures have not been independently verified.

The commander of Lavas ground robotic system battalion, identified by the callsign ‘Matematyk’, said the project addresses a category of warfare that requires a different approach from conventional battlefield robotics. “There is a very long list of tasks that need to be accomplished to fight underground and counter the enemy underground. This is a new class of technologies and challenges that we will have to confront,” he said.

The machines are being adapted from existing groundrobots,rather than developed as conventional surface vehicles. They must be compact enough to navigate narrow pipelines while retaining the ability to carry out military assignments . Most commercially available underground machinery, meanwhile, is designed for construction, drilling, and tunneling — not combat.

As of now, Khartiia is already developing several robotic systems to address those limitations, with the broader objective of sending machines into dangerous underground environments where soldiers would otherwise have to conduct reconnaissance, confront enemy positions, clear positions, or clear out themselves. “War is already underground, and it will expand. The question is how we adapt to this war,” Matematyk said.

The project follows unusual battlefield developments near Kupiansk, where Ukrainian forces used the same underground infrastructure that Russian troops have previously exploited to infiltrate behind Ukrainian defensive positions. During Operation Vels, Ukrainian soldiers moved through a gas pipeline measuring approximately 1.2 meters in diameter, according to a report by Ikrainska Pravda. The confined passage made movement and close-range firefights particularly,with Ukrainian troops having little-to-no room to maneuver.

Russia had previously used the pipeline network beneath the Oskil River to move soldiers toward Ukrainian-held territory. The Ukrainian battlefield monitoring group ‘Deepstate' reported an earlier Russian infiltration attempt in September 2025. Last month, President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said that Ukrainian forces had eliminated more than 100 Russian troops and captured over 30 during the operation. The experience also exposed the limitation of sending soldiers into such environments. Underground operations restrict visibility,complicate communications , and leave little room for maneuvering equipment.

That said, Ukraine has increased its reliance on unmanned military systems as battlefield terrain proves complicated to navigate. In July 2024, we reported on Ukrainian forces deploying AI-controlled drones capable of independently identifying and attaching targets. The country has also been experimenting with unmanned systems that operate beyond conventional aerial platforms. In August, the US Army tested an autonomous Ford F-250 breaching vehicle, which builds on the Lancer unmanned ground vehicle program and uses a counter-drone turret to protect military operations from aerial attacks.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

However, autonomous ground systems still face considerable technical challenges. Russia's Shturm uncrewed assault tanks , for instance , repeatedly lost their command connection during the Tsentr-2026 military exercise. The vehicle eventually became stuck in mud, even after organizers switched to manual driving. Russia has also tested an AI powered Shahed variant using Nvidia's Jetson Orin computing platform. The system was reportedly capable of identifying targets without relying on externally supplied coordinates.

#10516. Porting the NDK “Endless Tunnel” to iOS: A Masterclass in Cross-Platform C++

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When developers talk about high-performance mobile games, the Android NDK samples by Bruno Oliveira are often cited as the gold standard for clean, low-level C++. But as an iOS developer, I wanted to see if I could take that same “pure” C++ logic and make it sing on an iPhone.

My latest project, TunnelFeveriOS, is the result of that journey. It isn’t just a port; it’s a blueprint for building a custom game engine.

The original Endless Tunnel is built to live within the Android lifecycle (JNI, Activity, and NDK). Moving this to iOS required more than just hitting “compile.” I had to build a Platform Abstraction Layer.

To make the C++ engine talk to the iOS UI, I utilized Objective-C++ (.mm** files)**. This allowed me to keep the game's high-speed math and geometry in native C++ while using Cocoa Touch to manage the windowing and touch input. In TunnelFeveriOS, you’ll see how the view controller manages the game loop, feeding time deltas and touch coordinates directly into the C++ core.

Since the original game uses OpenGL ES, I leveraged iOS’s GLKit framework. The trick was ensuring that the shaders and vertex buffer objects (VBOs) originally written for Android were correctly initialized within the iOS EAGLContext. The result? 60 FPS performance that feels native because, at the machine level, it is.

A port is only “production-ready” if it handles modern app requirements. I integrated Google Mobile Ads (AdMob) to trigger Interstitial ads during game-over states.

This presented a unique threading challenge:

I implemented a messaging system that allows the C++ core to “request” an ad, which the Objective-C wrapper then catches and executes on the main thread — preventing the game from hanging or crashing.

This project proves that you don’t need a heavy engine like Unity or Unreal to achieve cross-platform results. By keeping the “brain” of your game in C++ and building thin “shells” for each platform (Android NDK or iOS/XCode), you gain:

If you’re interested in how to bridge the gap between low-level C++ and high-level iOS development, check out the full source code here:

Now that the foundation is laid, I’m looking into implementing a Metal-based renderer to fully embrace the future of Apple graphics. If you’ve worked on C++ to Metal bridges, I’d love to hear your thoughts in the comments!

#10513. SBOMs Alone Can't Reveal the Scope of a Supply Chain Attack

A recent Shai-Hulud attack shows defenders need real-time visibility into production code and affected services to respond to supply chain compromises.

ISC2 Strategic Congress 2026 — Denver — Depending on a software bill of materials (SBOM) to determine what happens when a malicious package slips past security checks can leave defenders struggling to establish what code actually executed, which systems it touched, and how far the compromise spread.

In a session at the ISC2 Security Congress 2026 this week, Atlassian product security manager Deepam Kanjani used a real-world compromise involving the Shai-Hulud worm to highlight incident response challenges, including gaps in runtime visibility, transitive dependency ownership, and attacker attribution.

In the session, Kanjani said he had altered all identifying details, figures, and the sequence of events to protect confidentiality, though his first-person, war-room narrative of underlying attack techniques, technical behaviors, and challenges was based on real events at a large-scale software-as-a-service (SaaS) environment.

Related:AI Gave Your Incident Archive a Second Job

Kanjani described the incident as involving a package that appeared legitimate, with a matching SHA hash, more than 3,400 GitHub stars, and a repository that looked credible to the company's security tooling. But hidden three layers deep in the package was a malicious transitive dependency named shai-hulud, after the giant sandworms in the Dune movie series. Because it was not a direct dependency, it did not surface in a top-level dependency audit.

The first inkling that something was wrong came when a behavioral scanner flagged the package as running something it shouldn't have been running. Incident responders initially dismissed the alert as a likely false positive because the hash matched and there was no other obvious indication of the package being malicious.

Additionally, the SBOM reported the environment as clean when the security team consulted it. What they did not realize at the time was that the SBOM was two development sprints out of date, Kanjani said.

As the response unfolded, the team could not say with confidence which services used the package. Indicators of compromise (IOCs) provided initial clues but could not establish which services were affected or how far the compromise had spread. Responders needed runtime evidence to determine what code had actually executed and what it could access.

Without that runtime telemetry, they had to correlate logs manually across more than 400 services, leaving them without a reliable picture of the compromise's potential reach. An initial Slack message from the incident response handler to enterprise stakeholders made matters worse by being too vague and prompting a set of parallel investigations into the still-unfolding event.

Related:What We Missed: Knicks Phishing Test Goes Out of Bounds

The security team's efforts to identify the attacker by tracing registry ownership, npm maintainer history, publisher identities, and GitHub records quickly led them to dead ends. The repository had been copied from a legitimate project, and the publisher's identity and account history appeared to have been fabricated, Kanjani said.

Researchers have subsequently attributed at least some of the later Shai-Hulud attacks to a threat actor tracked as TeamPCP.

The key takeaway, according to Kanjani, is that organizations need to look beyond SBOMs and dependency inventories and instead base incident response around what actually runs in production. The focus should be to equip security teams to identify, scope, prioritize, and contain incidents and enable real-time visibility into affected services and the potential scope of a compromise.

"You are triaging a runtime graph. Not a dependency file. Your tools must reflect that," he said.

Kanjani recommended that organizations keep deployed SBOMs under 72 hours old and try to ensure that more than 98% of production services meet that threshold. He also recommended enabling runtime capabilities to detect unknown module loads in under 90 seconds and daily registry checks to detect lookalikes of internal package names within 24 hours of publication.

Related:9 Cyber Awareness Tips That Actually Match Today's Maze of Threats

Kanjani described these as targets that organizations should calibrate for their own environments rather than as universal benchmarks. In addition, security teams should focus on the operation rather than the operator, Kanjani said. "Identity signals rotate. Operational necessities don't. Build detection around what the worm must do to function — not who's driving it."

All communications with stakeholders regarding a security incident also need to be direct and unambiguous. The key is to be honest, he said, while making sure to present the details in language appropriate for the intended audience.

"Don't bullsh-t. Tell them the reality in a way they can absorb it," he said. "You don't want to sell panic."

Illinois-based Jai Vijayan is a veteran, award-winning technology journalist with more than 25 years of experience covering cybersecurity. His information security reporting has explored everything from ransomware, nation-state threats, and identity security to AI risk, critical infrastructure protection, software supply chain security, cloud security and emerging enterprise technologies.

Over the course of his career, Jai has written news stories, feature articles, survey reports, white papers, and e-books for enterprise and technology audiences. He has also moderated panel discussions and executive roundtables featuring CISOs, security researchers, and industry leaders.

His work has earned multiple industry honors, including a Joint ASBPE Excellence Award for Best Coverage of Government IT, and a Joint Jesse H. Neal Award for wireless LAN security coverage. Jai holds a Master’s degree in statistics from Bangalore University, and studied broadcasting and electronic communication at Marquette University in Milwaukee.

#10512. Meet the 20 first-place apps from RevenueCat’s Shipaton 2026

A cozy habit tracker, a smarter snippet library, and a rooster that wants you out of bed: this year’s Shipaton winners offer plenty of new apps to explore.

RevenueCat’s annual app-building competition drew 3,985 project submissions in 2026, according to its winner announcement. Across the 20 first-place categories, developers tackled everyday frustrations, built inventive games, and found new ways to connect people.

Here’s a look at every first-place winner:

Read RevenueCat’s full winner announcement

Keeping a routine is easier when there’s something to look forward to. Mio pairs self-care and habit tracking with a pixel companion that grows as you make progress. Check off habits, earn coins, and unlock new companions and collectibles, with friends and groups adding a social reason to keep going.

Mio took Shipaton’s Grand Prize, with RevenueCat highlighting its revenue and growth in a crowded habit-tracking market.

That dog you met on your morning walk can become part of your collection. Boop identifies cats and dogs and turns their photos into stickers for a digital book, giving pet lovers a playful reason to pay attention to the animals around them.

Creator Taiwo Omisore’s “100 Boops” challenge took the app into the real world, where printed pet portraits and conversations with owners helped shape the product. That hands-on approach earned Boop first place in #BuildInPublic.

If you have fond memories of futuristic arcade racers, Nitelore has a familiar appeal: hovering vehicles, neon tracks, and plenty of speed. Chase your own ghost in time trials, take on combat races, or compete online.

Free tracks offer a starting point, while paid unlocks and daily and weekly challenges give players more to explore. RevenueCat’s Best Game judges singled out the art direction, effects, and haptics.

Explaining how you see color can be surprisingly difficult. See as Me: Colorblind makes that conversation visual, using a split-screen camera to compare different types and degrees of color blindness. A shareable link lets someone else see the comparison without installing the app.

Developer Javier Jiménez drew on his own experience with protanopia. That personal perspective helped the app earn the RevenueCat Peace Prize.

Clucky’s rooster is determined to get you out of bed. Turning off an alarm can require solving a problem, moving around, or finding an object, bringing a little friction to the temptation to snooze.

Its personality runs through the entire interface, including a time picker that uses the rooster’s scrolling eyes and yellow beak. That consistent character, animation, and attention to detail earned Clucky the RevenueCat Design Award.

Adversary starts with a familiar challenge: flip cards and find matching pairs. The catch is a hidden Danger card with no matching partner. Reveal it, and an ad becomes part of the board you’re trying to remember.

That idea earned Niv Ben-porath’s game the Catvertising Award. Players can continue with ads after an initial ad-free period, or choose a permanent unlock that removes ads and adds difficulty options.

Put a few phones on the table, and Bubble Games turns their screens into a shared game board. Objects move between devices as friends compete in minigames, including a virtual hot potato.

Built by four software engineering students at Université Laval, the Next Gen Award winner uses the phones people already have to create a shared activity. Its Shipaton submission includes ten minigames. The winner announcement links to the Android release on Google Play.

GRIDLOCK takes a game everyone knows and adds up to four players, online rooms, and eleven special moves. The result offers more ways to disrupt an opponent’s plans than the familiar three-by-three board.

Its HAMM Award win recognizes how the business model fits the game: a shared Credits currency connects rewarded ads, optional packs, and a subscription, while core modes remain free. Players can choose the option that fits how often they play.

Just Add begins with a craving. Type what you feel like eating, describe it aloud, or take a photo, and the app suggests three meals built around it, adding protein, fiber, and healthy fats to round out the plate.

Shopping support, hands-free cooking, and remembered preferences help carry the idea through to dinner. The winner of Abbey’s Kitchen’s nutrition challenge also impressed judges with a dinner-host feature that accounts for other people’s preferences.

The reply you send every week, the PDF you keep hunting for, and the image you paste repeatedly can all live in SnipStash. It organizes reusable text, images, and files across iPhone, iPad, and Mac, with a keyboard, Mac quick palette, hotkeys, and Shortcuts bringing them within reach.

Private iCloud sync connects the library across devices. Its speed and native interface helped it win Christopher Lawley’s productivity challenge.

Knowing what you want to say and saying it aloud are different things. Rehearse – Hard Conversations gives you an AI counterpart to practice with before a difficult workplace discussion.

Choose from 33 prepared scenarios or bring your own situation, then review feedback on clarity, empathy, assertiveness, and structure. A prep card helps you take the useful points into the real conversation. The app won Leadership Heather’s career-coaching challenge for its focused, straightforward approach.

You see a game in a short video, mean to look it up later, and forget its name. Game Magnet lets you share the clip to identify the games and save them to your backlog.

Processing happens in the background, with a notification when the result is ready. Shelves, ratings, and ranked lists help organize what comes next. The app won Mr Lewis Blogs Gaming’s challenge by making discovery and keeping track of games easier.

FeelGood: Movement Menu starts with a short check-in about your energy, available time, and how you want to feel. It then offers a small menu of movement, with an appetizer, main, and optional sides.

The options span yoga, Pilates, strength, walking, stretching, and breathing. Rest is part of the experience, too. Yusra Salam and Ameena Malik’s app won Simone Sharice’s yoga and fitness challenge, with judges praising its calm, inviting design.

BoatBrawl brings arcade boat battles to a group of up to eight players. Steer, fire, and scramble through interactive arenas, with bots and shared-screen play offering more ways to join in.

Rehaan Cubes and Ashwin K M used Kotlin Multiplatform and Compose Multiplatform to bring the game across phones and computers, including iOS, Android, Mac, Windows, and the web. Its smooth browser play helped it take JetBrains’ Ship Kotlin Everywhere award.

Trazo: Golf Ball Tracer adds a ball-flight line to your own golf videos. Select the ball twice to start, and the processing runs on the phone.

Julian Praest’s app won Most Viral App for the way its team introduced the product through organic content. Golf humor and practical advice set up the tracer reveal, making the finished video part of the pitch. For golfers, the appeal is simple: make the shot easier to follow when you share it.

One Piece Card Game collectors know that similar-looking cards can be very different finds. Grandfolio helps identify artwork variants, manage duplicates, and track collection prices and progress.The Best App for Galaxy winner impressed judges with layouts that adapt to folded, tabletop, and open-screen use. There’s an App Store release for Apple users, too, making this a relevant discovery even if your collection starts on an iPhone.

Let’s Get Support by 3Dadz is designed for partners, parents, and friends affected by someone else’s addiction. Check-ins, journaling, boundary-setting tools, and local resources give those supporters a place to focus on their own needs.

Joshua Burgess and Aaryn B. built the app from community conversations in British Columbia, using Replit to turn the idea into a live product. It won Replit’s Idea to Income award. The announcement links to its Android release.

PoquitoTalk: Bocas del Toro turns English into local Spanish voice notes for WhatsApp and helps translate the replies. The recipient doesn’t need to install the app, keeping the conversation in a tool people already use.

Creator Dorien Van den Abbeele drew on years of life in Bocas del Toro. Its Growth Loop Award recognizes extensive growth experiments and an honest account of what the measurements could show. The announcement links to Google Play.

Parky! combines wait times, dining availability, and memories from Disney and Universal visits. Built by former Disney Cast Member Chase Van Derrick, it aims to offer useful help throughout a park day.

Dining alerts can become conversations on the Lock Screen, where you can ask about another time or restaurant. Alongside queue Live Activities and recap messages, those interactive notifications helped Parky! win the Keep Them Coming Back Award.

In Your Own Words offers short, real-world speaking scenarios and feedback on pace, hesitation, and filler words. Speech analysis takes place on the phone, giving users a way to practice the words before they need them.

Natasha Wilson’s app won the Funnel Vision Award for connecting a web quiz, a relevant practice deck, and a web purchase that unlocks access in the app. The introduction to the product begins before the download.

Friendpire turns the idea of a board-game night with friends into a shared hex-tile world. Gather resources, build farms and docks, and grow an empire together. RevenueCat employee Erik MacInnis built the game to make it easier to stay connected with friends who rarely find time to play.

Friendpire won the separate Conflict of Interest Award, which was open to RevenueCat and sponsor employees and judged by Shipaton participants. It is included here as an optional sidebar and is not part of the 20 first-place count above.

From a useful tool for the workday to a game for your next night in, Shipaton’s winners show how much can come from taking an idea all the way to a shipped app. Explore the winners above, or browse RevenueCat’s full announcement for more apps from the competition.

#10511. The end of an era as Linux 7.5 purges the last 30-year-old Intel MMX optimizations from Pentium age

MMX was introduced in 1997 and marketed as a MultiMedia eXtension for Intel’s processor architecture in shiny new PCs with CD-ROMs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Linux developers are preparing to make Intel’s ancient MultiMedia eXtensions an irrelevance. Intel introduced MMX in the Pentium era, but a patch expected to merge into mainline Linux 7.5 will remove the last remaining optimized software from the open-source OS. Expected to be released in 2027, the new kernel will wipe MMX-optimized code from its software RAID XOR code, explains Phoronix.

Admittedly, there wasn’t much left in Linux in terms of MMX optimizations. But few folks will be running the most modern kernels on Pentium MMX and Pentium II chips in 2026. And most other MMX-optimized code disappeared from the OS half a decade ago, when some MMX-optimized memory management code was removed. Note that, if you are a determined late-90s Pentium devotee, you can still use software RAID, but it will lack the MMX fast path.

This is a classic case of Linux code housekeeping. With this change, the kernel will lose over 800 lines of code and replace them with just 12.

Intel first introduced MMX Pentium chips in January 1997. Though I vaguely remember these processors being marketed as being enhanced by ‘MultiMedia eXtensions,’ Wikipedia insists that “MMX is officially a meaningless initialism[6] trademarked by Intel.”

In a subsequent trademark battle with longtime foe AMD, a document produced in court indicated that MMX stood for ‘Matrix Math Extensions.’ AMD relied on this to sidestep the trademark, as an initialism cannot be trademarked, according to the encyclopedic source.

Eventually, all rival x86 processors managed to integrate MMX instructions – so as not to be pushed into the shade during the multimedia boom. A surprisingly large number of non-Intel chips ended up offering MMX, too. Along with Pentium MMX chips, AMD, Centaur, Cyrix, Rise, and Transmeta eventually offered MMX CPUs.

After MMX's marketing success, AMD pioneered 3DNow! instructions, and about a year later, Intel fired back with SSE and SSE2.

Operator ALL.Net apologized on Nov 2, but has only just begun to ‘gradually resume operation on a trial basis starting today.’

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The sensory overload typically experienced when visiting video game arcades in Japan was eerily absent from some venues this week, reports ITMedia News [machine translation]. It says that a Sega system outage meant there was “no end in sight” for the rows of silent black screens. However, a recent tweet from arcade network operator ALL.net indicates that both Sega and Bandai Namco machines are affected. Moreover, arcade lovers will be pleased to hear that affected arcades will “gradually resume operation on a trial basis starting today.”

a friend sent me some pics of their local arcade and LMAO the sega outage is nuts https://t.co/3vSYjCYAIZNovember 3, 2026

ALL.Net, a contraction of Amusement Linkage Live Network, was launched by Sega in 2004 to allow arcade games to look up to the net for auto updates, data logging, implement online play, and power national leaderboards. Sega titles like Initial D The Arcade, Maimai, and CHUNITHM, rely on this network, as do Bandai Namco games like Aiko no Tatsujin, Mario Kart Arcade GP DX, and Gundam Extreme Vs.

In its official apology blog post, dated November 2, ALL.Net / Sega Fave said that it suspended services a day prior after it became aware of its servers being affected by “unauthorized access by a third party.” It reassured customers that their personal info and billing data were not on the compromised server. However, it asked for more time to investigate, run safety checks, and so on, before restoring service.

This morning ALL.Net told customers that Bandia Namco titles “will gradually resume operation on a trial basis starting today (online play and card use enabled).” Sega products remain under maintenance, and online services are unavailable. There is currently no estimate for issue resolution, but a news bulletin is promised for November 10 (Tuesday).

Classic offline arcade machines weren’t affected by this outage, of course. Moreover, rival arcade gaming legends like Taito and Konami run their own, separate, arcade networks and authentication systems.
