#10505. Google’s AI search fumbles leave schools in the lurch

Good morning, and happy Ocarina of Time day to all who celebrate. Oh, and speaking of celebrating: congratulations to the real winners of the midterms, YouTube and Twitch streamers.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

It’s hard to overstate how reliant US school districts are on Google. Almost every school board in the country purchases Chromebooks for their students, many of whom are able to take them home and treat them as personal laptops. IT and procurement managers like them because they’re relatively cheap and offer zero-touch enrollment, which lowers upfront and ongoing maintenance costs. Teachers also like them because Google Classroom is relatively intuitive and ChromeOS is optimized for most other web-based education platforms.

But this reliance on Google’s tech stack comes at a cost: school boards are forced to comply with the company’s capricious terms of service, in particular when it comes to the creeping proliferation of AI across its products.

Things came to a head earlier this week when the New York Times reported that changes to the Google Search experience in schools will require opting into Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and, perhaps most importantly, AI Overviews. The move actually comes after Google was pressured to make student AI usage opt-in. Google’s justification: search and AI are now “deeply integrated” in the tech stack, so disabling one means disabling the other.

Google’s alternative to AI Overviews is a new Guided Learning feature, which replaces search’s primary function — solving a problem — with a step-by-step guide that helps a student understand said problem.

Former FTC Chair Lina Khan took to X to decry the move: “Google is using its search monopoly to coerce schools into adopting AI,” she said. “This highlights the danger of allowing an unregulated monopolist to control an essential service. Multiple courts have found Google liable for violating antitrust laws, and this latest move shows it remains unchastened. As the public increasingly pushes back against the use of AI in schools, Google is making clear it will punish those who don’t fall in line.”

I have a feeling Google will need to walk back this decision in some form. As 9to5’s Ben Schoon points out, it’s comically easy to disable AI Overviews in the current version of Google Search, and it doesn’t seem to have a detrimental impact on the subsequent results. But Google is likely worried that if it is forced to carve out versions of the search experience for specific user bases, revenue from its burgeoning AI Overviews ad business may be impacted.

Even if Google sticks to its proverbial guns, the move exposes a rift between the public, particularly parents, and the tech giants pushing AI on their users. Furthermore, the AI boom has had a direct impact on educators’ ability to purchase new hardware and upgrade old Chromebook fleets, since component prices have increased by as much as 5x over the past year. Some lower-income districts are even recalling Chromebooks from students, reverting to a shared laptop pool to save money.

For a much less complicated relationship with Google Search, try its Ocarina of Time Easter egg. Search for “Zelda” or “Ocarina of Time,” then tap the Master Sword to travel back to a 1998-style results page, complete with Link’s sound effects. It’s a trip down memory lane to a time when, as even Google acknowledges, things were a whole lot simpler.

Was AI on the ballot? According to Ben Thompson at Stratechery, it was in a big way. Democrats and Republicans both rejected data centers, but Democrats want to actually pass some regulation reining in the hyperscalers’ and frontier labs’ ambitions.

First we were short on compute, but that ramped; now we are short on memory, but that will ramp; we were coming close to being short on power, but that will be rectified. The final constraint is political, and that may be the most imposing constraint of all. Not only is it harder to solve, but it actually interferes with solving the other constraints. Many of the theories about AI assume that it becomes a commodity; I think it’s just as likely we are looking at scarcity for much longer than we expected, which will have ripple effects on everything.

Is it possible to pass broad AI regulation that will slow down an industry that has been allowed to run unchecked since Trump’s reelection in 2024? And is it in the best interest of these companies to support it, even if it means a short-term slowdown at a time when the two biggest AI labs, Anthropic and OpenAI, are about to IPO?

Some fascinating developments in the $6.3 billion Binance hack: turns out it was yet another case of social engineering. A Binance engineer was lured into a fake job interview, before which he downloaded malware that let hackers breach his computer and, eventually, others on the network.

After my discussion with Will last week about the death of browsers, it came as no surprise to learn that Brave is being sued by the New York Times for copyright infringement. Brave is accused of scraping paywalled material from sites like nytimes.com and selling access to it through a search API to AI companies, which use it as training data. Yes, it’s difficult to earn money as a browser maker (see Arc for another compelling example), but this is particularly egregious, even for a company familiar with shady business practices.

You can also support us by becoming a 9to5Google Pro member, starting at just $5/month. You get bonus podcast episodes, access to our members-only Discord server, and lots more.

Thanks for reading, and have a great day. I’ll talk to you on Tuesday.

#10504. How Product Teams Can Use AI User Research to Test Assumptions Before Sprint Planning

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Sprint planning is on Thursday, and the top of the backlog rests on three things nobody has checked. Users want the feature, they will find it, and they will understand what it does when they do. Each belief is plausible, and each has already been written into a ticket.

AI user research uses language models to simulate or analyze user conversations, so a team can put a question to a modeled audience in minutes instead of recruiting people for weeks. Used well, it gives a product team a cheap first check on backlog assumptions. Used badly, it produces confident-sounding agreement. This guide covers a two-day routine that gets the benefit and avoids the trap.

An assumption is worth testing when the plan depends on it and a wrong answer would change the ticket. "Users like clean interfaces" fails that test. "Analysts re-apply the same filters every session" passes, because if it is false the saved-filters feature shrinks or disappears.

The timing matters because planning leaves little room for research. The Scrum Guide says Sprint Planning is timeboxed to a maximum of eight hours for a one-month Sprint, and the first question the team answers is why the Sprint is valuable. Checks run before that meeting inform the answer. Checks run during it eat the time meant for deciding.

Write each risky item as a short card that names the belief, the consequence of being wrong, and the decision it feeds. A card forces the team to say what would change its mind, which most tickets never do. Here is an example:

Aim for three to five cards. A team that tries to test the whole backlog will test none of it, and the cards on the riskiest items give the best return.

AI user research comes in two forms. The first analyzes data you already hold: clustering support tickets and summarizing interview transcripts. It works on real evidence, so its main risk is a sloppy summary.

The second form interviews simulated people. AI user research of this kind builds a panel of modeled personas matched to your target profile, asks them your questions, and reports the themes. It needs no recruiting and returns results quickly, which fits a two-day window. Its output comes from a model of users and not from users, and that distinction should appear in every planning document that cites it.

A simulated audience helps most when you want to sharpen a question, and least when you need proof. Nielsen Norman Group's 2024 review of synthetic users found them useful for generating hypotheses and preparing interview guides. It warned against using them for final decisions or concept validation, and for niche populations, because AI tends to please and "every idea is often seen as a good one."

Spend Tuesday collecting answers and Wednesday reading them skeptically. On Tuesday, put each card's question to the modeled audience in neutral wording, and ask what would make them not use the feature. A question phrased as "Would saved filters help you?" invites a yes, while "Walk me through setting up your weekly report" does not.

On Wednesday, compare the answers with what you already know from support tickets and analytics. Mark each assumption as supported, contradicted, or unclear. Treat a unanimous answer with suspicion, because real audiences rarely agree on anything. Also be wary of lists in which the modeled users "seem to care about everything," which is how Nielsen Norman Group describes their weak prioritization.

Whatever stays unclear or high risk goes to real people. Jakob Nielsen's classic analysis found that five users find about 85% of usability problems, assuming comparable users, and he recommended several small rounds over one large one. An AI check is a good way to decide what those five people should be asked.

Each mark maps to a different decision. A contradicted assumption means the ticket is cut or reduced to its smallest useful version. A supported assumption that rests only on simulated evidence is not yet safe, so schedule a real-user check as the first task in the sprint, ahead of the build work that depends on it. An unclear assumption should not receive full scope.

The cost of skipping this step is real. A 2019 analysis of 615 product subscriptions by a product analytics vendor found that about 12% of features generate 80% of daily usage volume, and 80% of features are rarely or never used. That is one vendor's customer base, and the figures cover features that customers chose to track, so treat them as a rough guide. They still suggest that most of a backlog is a bet.

AI user research cannot replace talking to real users. Nielsen Norman Group found that synthetic users gave overly optimistic accounts of their own behavior, such as claiming to finish every course in a study, and that they cannot supply real past experience. It also called presenting synthetic findings as real-user findings "an unethical way to share what you've learned."

Two habits keep a team honest. Label every piece of evidence in the planning document as modeled, observed, or reported, and require that any feature built on modeled evidence has a real-user check scheduled. The review also warned that teams starting with synthetic research may get comfortable and skip the real kind, so put the real check on the calendar before the first AI result arrives.

Can AI user research replace interviews with real users? No. It is a cheap first pass that helps you write better questions and spot weak assumptions. Anything that drives a significant build still needs real people.

How many assumptions should a team test before planning? Three to five. Pick the ones where a wrong answer would cut or reshape a ticket.

How do I stop simulated users from just agreeing with me? Use neutral, open questions, ask what would make them avoid the feature, and treat unanimous answers as a warning sign.

What should I do with results I cannot verify? Mark them as modeled, give them less weight than observed evidence, and schedule a real-user check before committing full scope.

Sources: Scrum Guide, Sprint Planning; Jakob Nielsen, "Why You Only Need to Test with 5 Users" (March 18, 2000); Nielsen Norman Group, "Synthetic Users: If, When, and How to Use AI-Generated 'Research'" (June 21, 2024); 2019 Feature Adoption Report (615 product subscriptions, vendor-reported).

#10502. External Teams Chats Anchor 15-Minute Malware Delivery

Mail bombing is getting a new look with a wide-scale, ongoing social engineering campaign that might be tied to the Black Basta ransomware gang.

Threat actors are abusing Microsoft Teams' external chat functionality at scale, using IT-support impersonation and email bombing tactics to trick employees into granting remote access. Attackers target multiple users within short, five-minute windows of time, and once the victim bites, the attack chain reaches payload execution in under 15 minutes.

In a blog post on Oct. 26, Arctic Wolf detailed the campaign and what it described as a "recurring social engineering pattern" across more than 400 incidents in the wild. Threat actors use Microsoft Teams' external chat creation capability, which enables outside parties to contact employees.

"A consistent pattern of attacker behavior has emerged across these incidents: the use of the same throwaway tenants, the same 'IT Support (Internal)' display names, the same remote-access tools, and increasingly, the same payload domains," Arctic Wolf's Shubham Dubey wrote in the blog post.

Related:LLMjacking Emerges as Thriving Cybercriminal Enterprise

In a typical example of the attack, the victim is "mail bombed," or inundated with a flood of email subscriptions or newsletters, to establish a sense of urgency. Then, an external Teams user claiming to be IT support contacts the victim and offers to address the issue. The impersonator persuades the employee to launch a remote-access session via legitimate tooling like Quick Assist (most common), AnyDesk, ScreenConnect, Atera, Zoho Assist, NetSupport, and LogMeIn.

Once the victim grants access, the attacker conducts follow-on activity such as executing malware, stealing credentials, performing reconnaissance, or possibly deploying ransomware. Dubey explained that a typical order for this is a whoami/systeminfo-based recon, a PowerShell-based payload, a command-and-control (2) beacon, lateral movement to gain access to credentials through Kerberos abuse methods like Kerberoasting and GoldenPAC, and a second, commercial-grade remote access tool, "which provides an independent control channel that survives remediation of the original implant."

Spam or mail bombing is not a new tactic. Threat actors have been flooding inboxes as a pretense for social engineering for some time now. Microsoft researchers identified the use of this technique by Black Basta in mid-2024, with later examples seen in 3AM, Cactus, Chaos, and the GoGRPC campaigns. But what stands out about Arctic Wolf's research is that it shows this technique operating at scale, with recurring infrastructure, naming conventions, and attacker behavior seen across hundreds of incidents.

Related:TiDB Breach Exposes Flawed Cloud Security Assumptions

Justin Moore, director of adversary operations at Arctic Wolf, tells Dark Reading roughly 15% of cases progressed beyond the initial stage into a successful compromise, which, given the volume of the campaign, constitutes a success.

As for who is behind recent attack waves, Moore could not make definitive attributions but says, "Black Basta-associated activity remains one of the most significant drivers of Teams chat abuse."

On the infrastructure front, Arctic Wolf noted a move from throwaway onmicrosoft.com tenants toward attacker-controlled domains, especially ".top" domains, and provided data on their prevalence. The vendor stated that approximately half of observed cases involving paid domains used .top domains.

For naming, attackers would reach out with "(Internal)" appended to the display name to trick the victim that a message is coming from within the company, even though Teams labels the sender as "external" simultaneously. The names will also frequently use friendly sounding names (Arctic Wolf offered Liam, Scott McGhee, and Matthew as examples) after labels like "IT Support" and "IT Service Desk."

Other markers observed included naming conventions specific to the target's locale ("Supporte TI" for French- and Spanish-speaking organizations), and the use of 100 different emojis in attacker display names.

Related:Marvell, Microsoft & Oracle Work to Standardize Data Center Hardware Security

Arctic Wolf noted that prior to engaging with victims, the attackers initiate a one-on-one chat to avoid group conversations, which Microsoft records in its telemetry as the ChatCreated event. Arctic Wolf assesses this to be the primary detection point for defenders setting alerts.

The vendor also recommended setting an allowlist for trusted external organizations, blocking chats with unmanaged or Teams consumer accounts, disabling the option that lets unmanaged users start conversations with your users, blocking federation with trial-only tenants, and hardening your remote access path.

In a nod to Cybersecurity Awareness Month, Arctic Wolf pointed out that it may also be wise to alert staff to the social engineering aspects behind these campaigns, including outlining exactly how IT reaches out, what these contacts look like (and what they do not look like), and to train users how to read the external sender tag in Teams to identify possible red flags. Microsoft Teams also includes end-user reporting in the application itself.

That said, the technique could yet progress further.

"We have also recently observed campaigns combining Teams chat abuse with ClickFix-style social engineering, suggesting that the technique continues to evolve and is being integrated with other intrusion methods," Moore says.

A Microsoft spokesperson tells Dark Reading that the company helps organizations reduce the risk of phishing and social engineering in Teams through layered, built-in protections that, for example, can let administrators block external Teams users. The spokesperson adds that, next month, "Teams is introducing AI-powered phishing detection that uses conversation context to identify phishing and social engineering in higher-risk messaging scenarios, particularly first-contact chats with external users."

Alex is an award-winning writer, journalist, and podcast host based in Boston. After cutting his teeth writing for independent gaming publications as a teenager, he graduated from Emerson College in 2016 with a Bachelor of Science in journalism. He has previously been published on VentureFizz, Search Security, Nintendo World Report, and elsewhere.

At Dark Reading, he covers a variety of cybersecurity topics, including the cybercrime ecosystem, open source security, and the intersection between AI and threat actors. In his spare time, Alex hosts the weekly Nintendo podcast, "Talk Nintendo Podcast," and works on personal writing projects, including two previously self-published science fiction novels.

He has received numerous awards, including TechTarget's Writer of the Year in 2022 as well as more than 10 Azbee awards for his reporting between 2022 and today.

#10501. Logitech G502 X Lightspeed Review: No lights, same price

This is a great gaming mouse in terms of performance, but it's currently the same price as the prettier G502 X Plus.

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Logitech's G502 X Lightspeed has been around for awhile — the mouse was introduced in 2022 alongside the fancier Logitech G502 X Plus (and the wired G502 X), but since it’s receiving a firmware update that upgrades its Hero 25K sensor to a Hero 44K sensor, this seemed like a good time to take a look at it. The G502X Lightspeed is available in black and white colorways and currently retails for $159.99, which is the same price as the G502 X Plus — however, it can (and should) normally be found for less than this.

The Logitech G502 X Lightspeed is a right-handed gaming mouse with an ergonomically contoured shape. It's the same size and shape as the G502 X Plus, with large primary mouse buttons, textured rubber side panels for grip, and a smooth matte chassis with glossy accents. It comes in black and white colorways (our review unit was black) and has no lighting aside from a small indicator light at the front of the mouse to indicate connection and battery life.

Like the G502 X Plus, the G502 X Lightspeed is definitely on the larger side. It measures 5.17 inches (131.4mm) long by 3.12 inches (79.2mm) wide, and is 1.62 inches (41.1mm) high at its tallest point. It's larger than the Razer Basilisk V3 Pro and V4 Pro (5.11 x 2.96 x 1.67 inches / 130 x 75.4 x 42.5mm) — not by a lot, but it definitely feels like a much larger mouse. It weighs about 3.6 ounces (102g), which makes it lighter than the Basilisk V3 Pro, but slightly heavier than the Basilisk V4 Pro.

The G502 X Lightspeed has 11 programmable buttons, and 13 programmable controls if you include scroll up/down. There are the left and right primary mouse buttons, as well as two side buttons along the left edge of the left mouse button. On the left side of the mouse there are the two standard thumb buttons, plus a "sniper" button that's magnetically attached and can be flipped so that it's not in your way. The sniper button can also be "deleted," and Logitech includes a delete cover in the box that makes the button unpressable (and also has the same soft, rubbery texture as the rest of the thumb rest).

The scroll wheel is a 4-way tilt wheel, so it has three programmable "buttons" (left click, right click, and center click), as well as scroll up/down. Above the scroll wheel are two buttons: One is a mechanical scroll wheel switch that switches between notched, tactile scroll and free scroll — that’s not programmable, as it's a physical switch — and the other is a profile cycle button that can be remapped.

On the bottom of the mouse you'll find UHMWPE mouse feet — at the top and bottom, around the sensor, and under the thumb rest. There's a power switch, as well as a magnetically-attached dummy puck that can be swapped out for the charging puck included in the Logitech PowerPlay 2. Under the puck is a slot for storing the mouse's wireless dongle.

In the box, the G502 X Lightspeed comes with a 6-foot (1.8m) USB-C-to-USB-A cable, a USB-A dongle, a USB-C converter, and a sniper button delete cover.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

5.17 x 3.12 x 1.62 inches / 131.4 x 79.2 x 41.1 mm

The Logitech G502 X Lightspeed ships with Logitech's Hero 25K optical sensor, which has a max resolution of 25,600 DPI and a maximum speed of 400 IPS, and can handle up to 40 G's of force. However, both the G502X Lightspeed and the G502 X Plus are receiving a firmware update that will upgrade the Hero 25K optical sensor to a Hero 44K optical sensor, with a maximum resolution of 44,000 DPI and a maximum speed of up to 678 IPS. It's not clear when this upgrade will roll out, however, and I wasn't able to upgrade my review unit in G Hub as of this writing.

Upgrades aside, the G502 X Lightspeed's sensor is very good. It tracks on just about every surface you might possibly use while gaming, and is speedy, sensitive, and very accurate. The mouse supports a polling rate of up to 1,000 Hz, which is still pretty standard for most gamers — however, if you're someone who's convinced that the extra 0.125 milliseconds of lag is the reason for your losses, this is not a mouse with an 8,000 Hz or even a 4,000 Hz polling rate.

The G502 X Lightspeed features Logitech's Lightforce hybrid optical-mechanical switches in its primary mouse buttons, which combine the feel of mechanical switches with the speed and reliability of optical ones. The G502 X Lightspeed's switches do have a nice, tactile click with a crisp sound that's definitely on the louder side. The side buttons also feel and sound good — not quite as tactile as the primary buttons, but with a very crisp click sound.

The mouse features a dual-mode tilt wheel with a mechanical switch that lets you move between a notched, tactile scroll and a smooth, speedy free-scroll. Logitech's implementation of this is different from Razer's — Logitech's is mechanical, while Razer's is software-based. This means the modes are more distinct, but I'm not really sure if that's a good thing. While I liked the free-scroll mode of the G502 X Lightspeed better than I liked the free-scroll mode on Razer's mice (such as the Basilisk V4 Pro and the Naga V3 Pro), the tactile scroll was stiff, clunky, and very loud — with a lot of rattling. The scroll wheel only has two modes, so there's no middle ground — and while I'm fine to use free-scroll for most things, there are definitely games where I need a notched scroll wheel.

The G502 X Lightspeed is configurable via Logitech's universal peripheral software, G Hub. You can use G Hub to change the mouse's DPI settings (DPI steps and DPI shift speed — the DPI switch that occurs when you hit the DPI shift button) and remap keys. You can also use G Hub to update the mouse's firmware, so this is presumably where the Hero 44K sensor upgrade will come through.

The G502 X Lightspeed connects to your PC via 2.4GHz wireless; it can also utilize a wired connection, though the included USB-C cable is rubber and won't get you that smooth, drag-free wired mouse movement you can get with flexible paracord-covered mouse cables. The mouse does not include Bluetooth.

Logitech rates the G502 X Lightspeed at 140 hours of battery life, which is about 10 hours longer than the G502 X Plus (with no lighting). This is pretty impressive, even if there are similar wireless mice that last longer (the recently-launched Razer Basilisk V4 Pro gets up to 200 hours, for example). This should give you at least two weeks before you need to recharge, even with intense use. The G502X Lightspeed is also compatible with the PowerPlay 2, Logitech's wireless charging mousepad (sold separately).

The G502 X Lightspeed is a solid gaming mouse for most gamers — its contoured, ergonomic shape is comfortable, though it does feel bigger than similarly shaped mice, such as the Razer Basilisk V4 Pro. It has enough extra well-placed buttons for the average gamer (though, not enough for the average MMO player), and its 140-hour battery life is higher than average. Plus, assuming it actually gets this firmware update, it will have a competitive sensor — though it's not going to be quite as good as the actual Hero 2 sensor. But this mouse is currently priced at $159.99, which is the same price as the fancier Logitech G502 X Plus — and it's definitely supposed to be the more budget version of the two. Unless you find it at a great sale price, I'd pick up the G502 X Plus or even Razer's Basilisk V3 Pro 35K at a similar price.

#10498. Motorola Signature 27 goes official with true flagship specs and a $1,599 price tag

Motorola is officially back in the flagship space, with its new Signature 27 phone available for pre-order starting today, but it’ll cost a pretty penny.

Motorola Signature 27 is, in every sense of the word, a true and proper flagship smartphone, with specs that are more or less unmatched in the US and hold up even in international markets.

The full specs on Motorola Signature 27 include:

Add it all up, this is a true flagship like we’ve never really seen from Motorola, especially in the US.

Motorola also touts that Signature 27 is compatible with GrapheneOS, has an “ArcticMesh” vapor chamber cooling system with diamond-infused thermal gel, and reiterates just how bright that display is. The Tandem OLED technology in use is also mentioned to be 34% more energy efficient and have a 121% longer lifespan compared to traditional OLED panels. The speakers are also tuned by Bang & Olufsen, a name we’ve not seen in the Android space since LG was still in the game.

On the software side, Android 17 is available out of the box with the promise of 7 years of OS upgrades and security updates – no promises on how often those will be delivered, though. Motorola is also the first brand outside of Pixel to fully embrace Gemini Intelligence, with Gboard Rambler, “Create My Widget,” app automation, and Sign-To-Text ASL translation all supported on Signature 27.

Battery life should be excellent with a 7,000 mAh cell inside that Motorola says charges at 90W (wired) and uses silicon-carbon battery tech. Wireless charging speeds top out at 50W on Motorola’s charger and it sounds like it maxes out at 15W on other devices based on the company’s communication with us. There appears to be no Qi2 support either, sadly, even though Motorola supports the tech elsewhere in its lineup.

The cameras are not holding back either. The 50MP main shooter uses a Sony Lytia L910 sensor with LOFIC for improved color, brightness, and video, with two other 50MP sensors for the ultrawide and front-facing cameras. There’s also a 200MP periscope telephoto camera that’s only 3x optical quality, but goes all the way to 100x hybrid zoom. Motorola has also equipped the device with a dedicated EVS action sensor that is specifically used to improve clarity and sharpness on fast-moving objects while using an “Action Shot” mode.

Truly, a full flagship across the board, but oh boy it is not cheap.

Motorola Signature 27 is launching in the US for $1,599 on November 19, with pre-orders open today. That’s for the 512GB/12GB variant that’s also the only one coming to the US, with colors including “Coal Smoke” and “Capulet Olive.” There’s no availability coming to Canada at this time, but US buyers can get a free Moto Watch Ultra (the one with Wear OS) with their pre-order from Motorola.com for a “limited time.” That same offer also includes a free year of the “motocare” extended warranty. There’s no word of carrier launches yet, but Motorola says US units are “universally unlocked.”

#10497. Diamond Foundry creates world's first 200mm single-crystal diamond wafer

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

It has taken 25 years to get here and 300mm diamond wafers are in its sights for 2030.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Diamonds have exceptional cooling capabilities due to their excellent thermal conductivity, with the material estimated to be at least five times more conductive than copper. Because of this, Diamond Foundry, a firm focusing on lab-grown diamonds, announced the world’s first 200mm single-crystal diamond (SCD) wafer. The 200mm SCD is about the same wafer size that semiconductor fabs use for older processes, with the company boasting on its website that “Single-crystal diamond has entered the wafer-scale era.” This is part of the effort to manufacture SCD at scale so that it can be used for semiconductor cooling, especially now that increasing power of AI chips has also led to enormous thermal problems.

What makes Diamond Foundry’s 200mm SCD wafer different from other lab-grown diamonds is that it’s created as a high-quality single-crystal piece instead of a polycrystalline gem. Although the former is difficult to manufacture, it is what allows diamond to become an extreme efficient thermal conductor while simultaneously retaining its electrical insulating properties — this efficiency should help cut down on hotspots, making SCDs an excellent solution for AI chip cooling.

The company also showed in a graph that it is now thought possible to create a D300 SCD wafer before 2030, matching the size of the wafers used in leading node processes. However, it should be noted that the sample shown by the company remains unpolished, meaning it still needs to go through some processes to make it suitable for chipmakers.

Although the company was founded in 2012, it also acknowledged the work of scientists who have been chipping away at the scale problem of SCDs since the early 2000s. “This is one of those milestones that once seemed almost impossible. Hundreds of DF engineers, scientists, and technicians contributed to us getting here. It is the accumulated result of countless experiments, failures, inventions, rebuilt machines, and insights over more than a decade,” Diamond Foundry CEO Martin Roscheisen said.

“Stefan and Martin pursued this goal for 25 years. There are very few examples in technology of someone sticking to a really hard scientific challenge with that level of perseverance. They kept going when the prevailing view was that single‑crystal diamond simply could not be made at this scale. Today, the perseverance shows as the first-ever D200mm single‑crystal diamond. And best of all, it makes D300mm predictable as well.”

Diamond Foundry isn’t the only company that has identified the potential of lab-grown diamonds for semiconductor cooling. Akash Systems secured $18.2 million in CHIPS Act funding in 2024 to produce Gallium Nitride fused with synthetic diamond, which it claims will reduce temperatures by 20 degrees C. The De Beers Group, which is known for its diamond mines in Africa, is also building a copper-plated diamond composite material aimed at improving cooling efficiency for AI, high-performance computing, and GaN RF devices. Even Frore Systems, which is known for its solid-state cooler, is diving into diamond-infused watercooling cold plates for Nvidia’s next-generation AI GPUs.

#10495. Moto Buds Ultra 27 and Watch Fit 27 help complete the company’s new premium ecosystem

The duo of Motorola accessories will see a North American release in the coming months, featuring Motorola Qira integration.

If you’re planning on upgrading to the extremely ultra-level Motorola Signature 27 flagship phone this year, I have good news for you: you’ll have plenty of time to save up for Motorola’s accompanying accessories after you buy the $1,600 phone. Motorola isn’t announcing a specific release date or price for either the Moto Buds Ultra 27 or Moto Watch Fit 27, but here’s what you can look forward to when they eventually come out.

Starting with the Moto Buds Ultra 27, which Motorola calls its “most feature-packed pair of earbuds yet.” These sport Bose-branded ANC and SoundDesign tuning, plus six high-SNR microphones with two dedicated Voice Pickup Units in what’s now become a conventional wireless earbud design with tips. Motorola says it created the microphones not just for great sound quality, but also for content creation, which means they’re clearly confident in the quality of these mics.

The buds deliver around 8 hours of continuous playback on a single charge, and the included case adds another 37 hours of use. They’ve also got built-in Motorola Qira support, plus AI Live Translate, head gestures, dual connection and audio share (so you can pair them with two devices, or pair two sets of buds with one device), plus a wear detection auto-pause feature.

They also sport IP57 protection, support Google’s Find Hub, and feature 11mm dynamic drivers for crisp audio. Moto Buds Ultra 27 will debut sometime in the near future in two colorways: Pantone Reflecting Pond (dark blue), and Pantone Temple Gray.

The Moto Watch Fit 27 is the antithesis of the recently launched Motorola Watch Ultra, focusing more on daily health than anything else. That means nearly two weeks of battery life on a single charge (instead of a day or two as Wear OS watches have), plus a Polar-powered health engine that’s designed to get your health metrics right the first time.

The square design features a rotating crown and a customizable physical button below the crown, IP68 and 5ATM protection, Bluetooth connection, built-in Motorola Qira support, all in an aluminum frame. A 1.85-inch gOLED display sports ultra-thin 2mm bezels and Gorilla Glass 3 on top. The display supports always-on visibility, and the built-in Qira assistant gives you hands-free command of the watch’s abilities.

As for health metrics, this one supports SPO2 monitoring, stress insights, heart rate powered by Polar, menstrual cycle tracking, auto activity detection, step and distance counter, over 100 tracked workouts, and dual-frequency GPS for accurate tracking. The Polar engine also supports holistic tracking, including sleep, calories, inactivity, and active workouts. Moto Watch Fit 27 launches sometime in the future in three colorways: Pantone Stratified Sea, Pantone Pigeon, and Pantone Ballerina.

#10493. AI-Powered Pentesting for DevSecOps: 7 Checks Developers Should Make First

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Security testing used to happen once or twice a year, usually after a release had already shipped. That rhythm does not fit teams that deploy daily. AI-assisted tools now promise testing that runs continuously, chains steps together on its own, and writes up what it found.

For developers, the interesting question is not whether these tools are impressive. It is what has to be true in your pipeline before you let an automated attacker loose on your own systems. This article covers seven checks to make first, each with a pass condition, and one short code example you can run. Some checks, such as written authorization and cost tracking, usually belong to a security lead or manager. For those, the developer's job is to confirm they exist, not to own them.

Testing systems you do not have permission to test is not a gray area. Make sure someone with authority has approved the targets and the time window, and keep that approval with your records. Run the first tests against staging with test data. Production comes later, once you know how the tool behaves and what load it creates.

Pass: The approval is on file, and the SOC knows the window and source IPs.

An automated tester should never decide for itself what it is allowed to touch. Define scope in a file that your pipeline can check: which hosts are in bounds, when testing may run, and whether anything destructive is allowed.

SitePoint's article on security patterns for autonomous agents uses an open-source AI penetration-testing agent as its case study. It covers sandboxed execution, tool permissions scoped to each role, approval gates for high-risk actions, and audit logging. Scope enforcement belongs in that same family of controls. Here is a minimal check that refuses any target outside the allowlist or time window:

Call this before every scan job, and log the result. A tool that is out of bounds should fail loudly instead of quietly continuing.

Pass: An out-of-scope target fails the job, and egress outside the allowlist is blocked.

Findings are only useful if they reach the people who can fix them. Most teams already have a place where security results show up in pull requests or a security tab. SitePoint's walkthrough on automating DevSecOps static analysis with GitHub Actions shows how results written in the SARIF format appear as inline annotations and can block a merge when severity crosses a threshold. If your pentest tool can export SARIF or a similar format, send its findings to the same place.

Pass: The tool exports SARIF or similar, and results show up in PRs or the security tab.

An AI tool can produce a long list of findings. To judge whether they are complete and sensible, you need a reference for what good web security testing covers. The OWASP Web Security Testing Guide is a free, widely used checklist of test categories for web applications. It covers web applications only, so APIs and network services exposed to the internet need their own coverage. Map the tool's findings against it, and note which categories it never touched.

Pass: You know which OWASP WSTG categories you expect the tool to cover.

Vendors describe AI pentesting in similar terms: agents plan attack steps, try them, and verify what works. For a view of how a security vendor explains the approach, CyCognito's overview of AI penetration testing walks through a workflow from scoping to autonomous testing, validation and reporting, and notes that human review stays part of the process. All vendor claims need testing in a demo against your own systems before you rely on them.

Ask the same questions of any tool you evaluate: What does it test beyond known checks? How does it confirm a finding is real? What does it send to external services?

Pass: The vendor has clear answers, including where the target list comes from.

Automated findings need review. A person should confirm that a reported path is exploitable, judge its business impact, and decide the fix order. Make sure this is set up before the first run. Without it, a team can end up with a long backlog of unverified findings and little trust in the tool.

Pass: There is a named reviewer and time set aside each cycle.

Running agents continuously can add up, especially when they call large models. Make sure you can track what each run costs and how many real issues it finds, so you can compare it with other approaches. You also need to be able to rerun the specific test that found a problem after a fix ships and keep the result. A finding is only closed when the retest passes.

Pass: Both are possible before you commit to running the tool continuously.

Does AI pentesting replace manual penetration testing?

Not entirely. Automated testing can run more often and cover more ground, but people are still needed for scoping, complex attack paths and judging impact.

In staging, with test data, inside a written scope and time window.

What should I do with findings from the tool?

Send them to the same place as your other security results, have the named reviewer check them, and retest after fixes.

AI-assisted pentesting can give development teams faster and more frequent security feedback, but only when it runs inside clear limits. Before the first run, check that targets and the window are approved, scope is enforced by your pipeline, findings will land where developers work, you have a baseline to judge the output, you know what the tool tests and sends out, someone owns the review, and you can measure cost and retest. The scope check above is small, but it makes sure the tool stays inside the lines you set.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has admitted to having an affair with a woman who is allegedly a Chinese spy. His apology came in a letter to Nvidia staff, which you can read in full below. Nvidia's board of directors released a statement late on Sunday to address the bombshell report that U.S. intelligence believes Huang had an affair with a Chinese spy. The board says it has no reason to believe sensitive corporate information or U.S. national security was compromised, but the company intends to examine all available information to determine what happened.

The BoD claims it is fully confident in Jensen Huang and his leadership team. Huang, who has been married to his wife, Lori Huang, for 41 years, says he fully supports the investigation.

"The Board prioritizes U.S. national and economic security, which is paramount to the interests of Nvidia and its shareholders. […] Jensen fully supports the review and has committed to cooperating with it."

The company has been on the radar for smuggling restricted hardware to China since at least 2025: Megaspeed has been accused of smuggling $2 billion worth of AI chips into China via its subsidiary Speedmatrix.

To my colleagues at NVIDIA, I owe my family and all of you an apology.

Nvidia has strenuously rejected suggestions of improper dealings, saying that its CEO regularly meets thousands of customers and that these interactions do not involve sensitive corporate information or national security secrets. Chinese officials have also dismissed the espionage allegations as unfounded.

The suspected Chinese intelligence operation targeting Jensen has not been independently confirmed as a fact. The relationship is alleged to follow the typical "honeypot" strategy, wherein foreign intelligence agencies use operatives to ensnare targets into romantic relationships to steal information and influence their decision-making.

Huang has been an outspoken critic of those opposed to limiting the supply of AI GPUs to China. Huang recently expressed his opinion that the label 'China hawk' is not a badge of honor Americans should wear; it is really 'a badge of shame.' Huang also said it "doesn’t have to be all us or them. It could be us and them," drawing fire from others in the administration and other tech companies, including the CEO of Palantir.

#10490. 3 Frameworks to Protect CISOs & Orgs Against AI Liability

Cyber leaders can get ahead of AI liability with rigorous documentation, transparency, and smart contract negotiations with AI vendors.

ISC2 Strategic Congress 2026 — Denver — Many chief information security officers (CISOs) currently find themselves in the deep weeds of AI governance. But the question they might not be asking is: How do you defend decisions around AI deployments when litigators, regulators, and customers come looking for someone to assume liability for a compromise?

Cyber legal expert Daniel B. Garrie, CEO of Law and Forensics, is striving to answer that question with distinct frameworks that both help CISOs determine whether a specific AI feature is sufficiently safe to run, as well as generate an evidentiary trail of paperwork that can be used to defend their actions, should something go horribly awry down the road.

While the need for such frameworks is particularly in the limelight in the age of agentic AI, the work began back in June 2024, when JAMS AI Rules were established to create a framework arbitrators can use to settle AI disputes between parties. The rules themselves don't explain liability or apply to every litigator. But Garrie, who helped write the rules, says they are helpful for establishing guidelines for technical evidence handling.

Related:AI Gave Your Incident Archive a Second Job

Speaking from the stage at ISC2 Strategic Congress 2026 this week, he stressed the importance of addressing incident-response needs at the contract stage and outlined frameworks for three best practices in particular that are useful for quantifying AI liability: calculating an AI deployment risk score; documenting board decisions around risk appetites; and determining which data specific should be preserved from an incident.

In the aftermath of an AI compromise, the basic forensic evidence as to what happened can be difficult to get. Garrie stresses that baking these considerations in at the contract stage with AI vendors is crucial, as is having cybersecurity leadership involved as early as possible during those negotiations, with an eye to creating provisions that allow CISOs to establish precisely what happened in an incident, including access to logs and preservation requests.

“Can the AI vendor provide you with a snapshot of the model at the time of an incident?” Garrie suggested CISOs ask. “And how fast can that be made available?”

He added that important considerations for contracts also include risk allocation that needs to be established as well, and urged CISOs to have a clear picture of vendor AI use permissions, data restrictions, warranties, insurance, and liability caps. Cyber leaders should also have a handle on the vendor's operational controls, access, and security responsibilities. Finally, an understanding of how disputes will be handled is necessary for CISOs, including ensuring compliance with governing laws, the forum for disputes, emergency relief scope, and more.

Related:What We Missed: Knicks Phishing Test Goes Out of Bounds

Getting a handle on contract negotiations with AI vendors early could be the difference between getting the evidence you need to prove your company wasn't negligent and not, Gerrie stressed.

"Once the contract is signed, that's it," Garrie warned. "They aren't going to give you anything that isn't specifically outlined. You'll have to wait for a renewal."

Garrie has developed a simple five-factor rubric that helps CISOs determine the risks and exposures related to the deployment of any given AI tool. Across five dimensions, including "potential harm," "data and rights," "autonomy," "evidence gap," and "vendor dependence," the framework helps provide a score that, according to Garrie, "makes assumptions visible."

The final score determines whether the AI tool falls within "standard review," "conditional approval," "restricted pilot," or "hold release" thresholds, each of which indicates a specific level of risk.

Related:9 Cyber Awareness Tips That Actually Match Today's Maze of Threats

Creating a paper trail of specific board decisions can be a critical piece of evidence in disputing liability, Garrie explained. Weak or vague reporting isn't the CISO's friend.

Garrie's framework for preserving board decisions includes granular details like the scope and time frame of the board decision; the exposure associated with the deployment (and evidence provided of that risk); the conditions of the deployment and the requirements for human approval; the accountable parties who authorize the work and review evidence (and a record of oversight including the final decision; challenges that were raised during the decision-making process; as well as the owner of the tool and associated due dates.

This one is about not just creating a static record of the decision, but about generating ongoing records that are defensible against AI liability, he added.

The purpose of this tool, Garrie said, is not just to contain the compromise, but to work to preserve the state of the AI system at the time of the incident. He also advised CISOs to get legal involved immediately.

The tool helps identify and preserve this kind of data, beyond the basic security logs. Garrie's framework requires evidence on the system state at the time of the incident, the interaction records, data and access information, and a governance record, as well as data integrity and chain of custody details.

Over the next month, Garrie challenged CISOs to put his frameworks to the test on one AI deployment, improve one board report, and run one AI tabletop to practice coordinating actions around vendor evidence preservation and notifications.

The bottom line, according to Garrie, is that regulators, litigators, and customers ultimately are only going to care about the evidence you can produce to prove the organization considered all the risks and acted responsibly to mitigate them.

"They don't care that you're using the NIST guidelines," he warned. Determination of liability is going to come down to what the CISO and the organization can prove they did to protect their systems against AI compromise.

#10482. AAWireless expands beyond Android Auto adapters with new cooled Qi2 car charger

AAWireless says to use your car’s 12V outlet with the included adapter for “best performance,” but you can also use existing USB-C connections so long as they deliver enough power.

To be clear, there’s no wireless adapter built into the charger (as cool as that would be), but you can use this alongside an AAWireless adapter while driving, the company specifically mentions.

#10481. Retrying a send is harder than sending

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every integration that moves something out of your system has the same weak point, and it is

not the happy path. It is the second attempt.

Your code posts a document to a provider. The connection drops before the response arrives. You now hold no job id, no confirmation and no idea whether anything left the building. The timeout told you about your network. It told you nothing about theirs.

So you retry, and you either send the thing twice or you never send it at all. Pick one, because

without help from the API you cannot have both.

The first instinct is a client-side guard. Hash the payload, keep a table of what you have already

That works until the duplicate attempt comes from a different process, a different container, or a

queue worker that started after a deploy. Your table is local state defending against a distributed

The second instinct is to ask the provider whether the job exists. That means listing recent jobs

and matching on something, usually the recipient and a timestamp window. It is a search, and

searches have false positives. Two legitimate sends to the same recipient a minute apart look exactly like one send and one retry.

The third instinct is to make the operation naturally idempotent. That works beautifully for a database upsert and fails completely for anything that leaves your control. A document that has been transmitted cannot be un-transmitted by a second call.

An idempotency key, generated by the caller, attached to the request, and honoured by the

The contract is simple. The caller invents a unique string per logical operation. Every attempt

carries the same string. The first request to arrive does the work and records the result against that key. Every later request carrying that string gets the recorded result back instead of doing the work again.

The important part is where the state lives. It is on the server, which is the only place that knows

whether the job exists. Your retry loop stops needing to be clever. It just needs to reuse the key.

Stripe popularised this pattern and the reason it spread is that the alternative is unbounded.

Without it, every caller writes their own deduplication, badly, and the failure only shows up under load.

Document and messaging APIs are where it matters most, because the side effect is visible to

someone outside your company. Fax for Agents is a current example of the pattern taken

seriously. It requires an Idempotency-Key on every mutating call, so a retry hands back the

same job instead of creating a second one.

Idempotency solves the duplicate. It does not solve the other half, which is that you still do not know what happened.

Most integrations model this as a boolean. Sent, or not sent. That is wrong, and you discover it

the first time a transmission half completes.

A useful state machine for a send has more room in it. Fax for Agents publishes six states:

queued, active, partial, failed, unknown and delivered. Two of those are the interesting ones.

unknown means the provider has not said. Not failed, not delivered. The correct response to

unknown is to wait. A retry loop that treats unknown as failure needs rewriting, because that is

how you produce duplicates even with idempotency in place.

partial means some of it arrived. Resending the whole document would deliver the first pages

twice, so partial is a state that must never be retransmitted automatically. It needs a person, or a

And delivered should be the narrowest word in the vocabulary. In that API it is written only when

the provider's evidence for the attempt supports it, never when the request was merely

accepted. The gap between "we accepted your job" and "it arrived" is where most false

Four questions, answerable from the documentation before you write a line of code.

Does it accept a caller-supplied idempotency string on every mutating call, and for how long

How many terminal states does a job have? If the answer is two, the API is hiding something

Is there a state meaning "we do not know yet", and does the documentation say what you should do when you see it?

When a webhook arrives, can you verify it came from them? Signed delivery with an event id is

the difference between a webhook handler and an open endpoint.

None of that is exotic. It is the difference between an integration that degrades and one that

#10479. Security researcher had to jailbreak their car just to update the clock

By reverse engineering the 2014 Renault Latitude’s firmware and exploiting some vulnerabilities, the researcher fixed their daylight savings time mismatch issue.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A security researcher has revealed how they were forced to update the time on their car infotainment system using reverse engineering techniques. Of course, invasive tech surgery shouldn’t be needed for this kind of basic routine daylight saving time tweaking task. However, Rasmus Moorats’ 2014 Renault Latitude wouldn’t allow him to adjust the time because the original navigation map SD card was missing.

Moorats had been warned. When he bought the used car, the previous owner noted that the SD card would be required to change the time on the in-car display. “I didn't really find that believable,” recalls the security researcher. They seem to have believed it was a skill issue of the prior owner and, because the maps it contained were chronically out of date, they decided to confidently decide they didn’t need the SD card...

The time to adjust non-connected clocks to daylight saving time inevitably came, and Moorats decided to adjust the in-car time display, which had become an hour out of sync. Two settings menus were available via the in-dash system. One allowed the adjustment of Bluetooth and Phonebook settings – clearly no use here. But the second settings menu wouldn’t produce any submenu. Instead, an error message "No maps detected!" popped up every time it was prodded.

With a certain set of skills under his belt, Moorats decided to “just RE it,” where RE = reverse engineer. He found out that the head unit ran a customized Linux-based TomTom operating system on a Samsung SoC. In brief, he extracted and analyzed the firmware, built a QEMU environment for the device, reversed the scripting engine, and uncovered a number of vulnerabilities affecting the system.

The breakthrough came in a brainwave, which seems to stem from experience with kernel exploit techniques during his day job. “Instead of trying to get the userland to execute our script, why don't I get the kernel to execute our script instead?” pondered Moorats. The approach worked, rewarding Moorats with root-level access to the infotainment system.

The 2014 Renault Latitude owner subsequently discovered that the date & time settings were in /mnt/flash/settings.dat in a custom binary format. Interestingly, the system “doesn't really have a concept of time zones at all,” observed Moorats in the conclusion of his blog post. “It gets the time from GPS and stores the local offset as a number of minutes. Mine was set to 180. I changed it to 120.”

The open-source project reportedly violates Synaptics' patents, but its creator begs to differ.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A hobbyist building on open-source driver for DisplayLink hardware received a cease-and-desist from Synaptics, resulting in a lengthy written argument and a threat to sue from the company. According to Phoronix, the Vino driver was “clean-room reverse-engineered” and partly developed using AI and written in Rust. However, even though the person behind the open-source driver, Mike Lothian, did not break the copyright of the code behind the driver, Synaptics argued that his work violates its patent on the DisplayLink technology.

The story began in July 2026, when Lothian reached out to Łukasz Spintzyk, a software engineer working at Synaptics, to help solve a few bugs he’s been encountering. However, Michal Lukaszek, the Strategic Engineering Partnerships Lead at the company, replied a couple of weeks later, saying that the company is concerned that Lothian is attempting to implement the DisplayLink protocol itself, which is a violation of its patents. This resulted in a cease-and-desist letter from Synaptics’ legal department asking him to take down the Vino GitHub repo, among other things.

Synaptics claims that Lothian’s work infringes on two patents: GB2484736 and GB2485613. The former, called Image generation, is about sending screen updates over a general-purpose data network like USB or Ethernet using a specific architecture and compression method, while the latter, called Display control device, details hardware that works with compressed data and only decompresses it as needed. Lothian responded, asking for complete details as to how his work infringes on its patents, but received no response, prompting him to follow up three weeks later.

This was when Synaptics decided to escalate, telling him that it would be billing Lothian “to recoup the cost and effort required to create such documentation of your infringing acts.” Andrew Bateman, the company’s Assistant General Counsel, also added, “By open sourcing our DisplayLink technology for whatever personal reasons you may have as a ‘hobbyist,’ you will be single-handedly eviscerating DisplayLink's competitive advantage in this crowded market by educating our competitors and other third parties as to how we implement our proprietary technology, which we have gone to great lengths and great expense to prevent.”

“If you'd like to notify the media or otherwise publicize our response, I encourage you to do so. We welcome any and all free publicity to let the world (in particular, our competitors and our shareholders) know that Synaptics aggressively defends and protects its DisplayLink IP from all threats of infringement.”

Lothian eventually took the GitHub repo containing the Vino driver offline, but it seems that the fight isn’t over yet. The hobbyist claims that Synaptics’ patents are invalid, saying, “In both cases the independent claims as filed contained no network or transport limitation. The claims were granted only after being amended to add delivery of the display data over a ‘general-purpose data network’ (and, for GB2485613, a ‘transport protocol’ with an ‘unpackaging’ step). That added feature was not new.”

He further argues that even though the company says that DisplayLink isn’t part of the Open Invention Network, Synaptics itself is an OIN licensee. Some say that an OIN license requires that any subsidiary acquired by the original licensee and all its patents would also fall under the company’s OIN license, although Synaptics apparently disagrees with this.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Despite the escalation, Lothian says that he remains open to discussing a sensible resolution. But unless the two parties come to an agreement, it looks like they’re getting ready for a legal face-off. You can see the complete email thread between Lothian and Synaptics in this Linux Kernel Mailing List page and the attached email thread (PDF).

Lothian said that he used a Claude subscription to help out with his cleanroom reverse-engineering efforts, revealing the headache that advanced AI tools pose for tech giants. A user has created a free Adobe Creative Suite clone in Rust using Claude, and was also reportedly working on an open-source office suite and Autodesk tools. Aside from that, we’ve also seen a lot of reverse-engineered games running on platforms they’re not designed for, forcing Anthropic to enforce some limitations on Opus 5.5. There was also concern that the widespread use of AI in chipmaking could result in the inadvertent spread of a copied design or an infringed patent across thousands of chips before anyone catches it.

#10471. How to Build an AI-Assisted Workflow That Does Not Leak

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A generative AI tool can draft a client email, summarise a forty-message thread or refactor a function in seconds. That speed is real, and it explains how fast these tools have become part of the ordinary working day, used to summarise threads, draft replies and reformat data in a single paste. The same ease is the problem. It moves sensitive material into systems that were never scoped to hold it, quietly and at scale.

Here is the uncomfortable part for anyone running a lean team. The risk in that workflow is almost never the model being wrong. It is that nobody decided what should go into the tool in the first place, and nobody is accountable for where the data ends up. AI made the drafting instant. It did nothing on its own to make delegation safe.

This article is about the control that closes that gap. It is not a product and it is not a policy document. It is a person, placed deliberately in the workflow, making the one call that no model can make for you.

Samsung learned this in public. In 2023, engineers in its semiconductor division were given access to ChatGPT to help debug source code. Within weeks, staff had pasted proprietary code and internal meeting notes into the tool, and Samsung banned generative AI across the company soon after. The story ran everywhere, from Bloomberg to The Register. Nothing malfunctioned. People simply moved sensitive material into a system that was never scoped to hold it, and the material was gone.

Two things make this failure mode easy to hit, and both are structural rather than careless.

First, most of these tools run in the browser. The data loss prevention software a security team relies on was built to watch files, endpoints and network egress, and it often cannot see a paste action into a web app at all. Worse, much of this activity happens through personal accounts that sit outside the controls the organisation does have. The leak does not trip an alarm because nothing was downloaded and nothing was emailed. Text was typed into a box.

Second, consumer-grade accounts can retain what you give them. OpenAI's own documentation states that content from consumer ChatGPT may be used to improve its models unless the user turns that setting off. Paste a client list or a contract clause from the wrong account, and that material may now sit inside a training pipeline you do not control and cannot claw back.

For a developer or a founder, the lesson is not "do not use AI". It is that the point of failure sits one layer above the model, in how the work is handed off and who decided what was safe to hand over.

If any of the data moving through that workflow identifies a person, a client, a candidate, an employee or an investor, then GDPR treats the tools and people touching it as processors acting on your behalf. That sounds abstract until you translate it into design constraints, which is what a technical team can actually work with.

Under Article 28 of the GDPR, anyone who processes personal data for you must do so under a binding contract that sets out what is processed, for what purpose and for how long, and everyone handling that data must be under a confidentiality commitment. An AI vendor you have never papered is a processor you have not accounted for. A free account used quietly by one engineer is a sub-processor nobody signed off. The obligation does not disappear because the tool was convenient.

Location is the second constraint, and it is the one most teams miss. A person, contractor or tool processing your data outside the EU means personal data is being transferred outside the EU, which requires a specific safeguard such as the Standard Contractual Clauses on top of the Article 28 contract. Keep the procesing inside the EU and that entire category of transfer risk falls away before you have to manage it. For UK teams the position is the same: the Information Commissioner's Office sets out the identical core terms for a processor contract, documented instructions, confidentiality, security measures, rules on sub-processors, and deletion or return of data at the end of the engagement.

There is now a second regulatory layer as well. The EU AI Act, which entered into force in 2024, adds obligations on how AI systems are deployed and governed, on top of the data-protection duties GDPR already imposes. The detail is still settling, but the direction is clear: unmanaged, undocumented AI use is getting harder to defend, not easier. None of this is exotic. It is the same governance you would apply to any third party with access to your systems. AI simply introduced a new processor that most workflows never wrote down.

"Human in the loop" usually gets discussed as a quality measure: a person checks the output so errors do not ship. That is true, and it undersells the point badly. The human is also the only part of the workflow that can make a judgment about what should never enter the tool at all.

A model cannot decide that a board deck stays out of a consumer chatbot. A browser extension cannot know that one folder holds client data governed by a contract that forbids sub-processing without written consent. Those are judgment calls, and judgment is precisely the thing you cannot automate. The person doing the delegating is your policy enforcement point, sitting at the exact moment the decision is made. They decide what gets pasted, what gets redacted first, what runs only inside an enterprise account with training switched off, and what a human handles end to end because the risk is simply not worth the minutes saved.

This reframes the division of labour that actually holds up. The AI owns the mechanical layer: summarising a long thread, turning rough notes into a first draft, extracting action items, reformatting data. The human owns the judgment layer: what matters, how it is framed, and, above all, where the data is allowed to go. Remove the human and you have not automated the work. You have removed the only control that was reading the situation and deciding what was safe.

It is worth being specific about what that judgment looks like in practice, because it is a skill and not a job title. Someone exercising it knows which inputs are off limits without having to ask, keeps sensitive material inside controlled systems by default, and treats the question "should this go into the tool at all" as part of the work rather than an afterthought. Whether that person is an engineer, an operations lead or an executive assistant, the control is the same: someone with enough context to know what is sensitive, and enough standing to say no. That habit is what separates a workflow that is genuinely governed from one that merely has a policy document nobody reads.

The practical version, for a founder or a small technical team that wants the speed without the exposure:

Most teams can put the first four in place in about a week. The last two are harder, because they are not configuration changes. They are about whether the workflow still holds when the people in it change, and that is the part most teams discover they got wrong only after someone has already left.

The speed of AI is not in question. What AI cannot supply is the judgment about what should enter it, and the accountability for where the output goes. Treat the human in the loop as a security control, design the workflow around that control, and you keep the nine-second draft without the quiet data leak. Remove the human, and the only thing you have really automated is the leak.

#10468. LG WOLED TVs suffer from 'edge rot' pixel death

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

User reports of the problem keep mounting, as well, including from RTings staff and our own.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

When shopping for an OLED TV, most prospective owners are concerned about burn-in, a problem that's been mostly mitigated, save perhaps for scenarios that involve leaving a TV on 24/7. This has been demonstrated by RTing's famous long-running test, but the site has found a new problem that seemingly pertains to pre-2023 LG WOLED panels: edge rot, where pixels progressively go dark around the edges, similar to a mold pattern.

RTings says that 14 out of the 16 LG televisions in its burn-in test have developed this problem in varying degrees, so 87.5% of a small test sample are affected. Those sets were all made between 2020 and 2022. The site says that several staff members saw the issue pop up on their personal televisions. Some searches indicate similar reports from users everywhere, and coincidentally, I learned about this just last week as a close friend sent photos of his 2019 LG TV.

The site says that the television sets bearing Samsung QDOLED panels in its burn-in test do not exhibit these symptoms, though it notes that it only has three such units in its test run. Additionally, since the test ran between 2022 and 2025, RTings says it can't assess whether LG's newer-generation WOLED panels have this issue, and notes that the manufacturing process for said panels reportedly improved in 2024.

Needless to say, most of the television sets in the study are going to be out of warranty. LG does have five-year panel warranty coverage, but only since 2021, and only covering higher-end units like the G, Z, and M series. It's still worth looking into this, in case you own a set from those lines and within the covered timeframe.

RTings asked for statements on the matter from both LG and Samsung, and interestingly, both firms discussed moisture directly or indirectly. LG stated that the issue "may occur in a very small number of OLED products under prolonged exposure to high-temperature, high-humidity, or other harsh environmental conditions."

It's not likely that RTing's test environment is especially humid, though, and the flurry of user reports with TVs sitting in their own living rooms doesn't lend much credence to LG's argument.

Meanwhile, Samsung is more specific, saying that it's implemented its proprietary Thin Film Encapsulation (TFE), a method for blocking ingress at the panel's edge. The firm says TFE "[eliminates] the root cause of border degradation and [ensures] long-term edge-to-edge uniformity where traditional structures falter."

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

RTings says that according to conversations with industry experts, "contamination during manufacturing, defects in the encapsulation of some OLED displays, or degradation of that encapsulation over time" may be at the source of the problem. The site's investigation also notes that some of its test units are affected by edge delamination.

The publication also believes the pixel-death problem may be related to power-on hours, while edge delamination could be dependent on the unit's age. It's unlikely that all user reports come in from people leaving their TVs on 24/7 or close to it, though. A sample size of one means little, but the TV is used sparingly at my friend's home, only around 30 hours per week — over nearly seven years, he's only seen the pixel refresh function come up twice.

For whatever it's worth, he notes that his set's dark spots mostly reside on the side closest to a portion of the wall with very minor humidity ingress from outside the building. The set is resting on furniture, not wall-mounted, though, so the relation between humidity and the edge rot is inconclusive at best.
