#10466. DroidDeck review: This could change how you game on your phone [Video]

The gaming landscape has changed drastically over the past year to the point where you’re able to effectively turn a capable phone into a fully fledged Steam Deck with projects like DroidDeck.

If you’ve been living under a rock for a bit, all you need to know is that it’s now technically possible to run full PC games directly on a high-end Android smartphone without relying on cloud streaming or rooting your device.

In effect, this turns a recent, compatible (more on that in a second) flagship phone into a de facto Steam Deck. I am still trying to wrap my head around how well it actually works, but we’ll try to give you a fair summation here with all the pros and cons.

A quick preface: I am a sucker for mobile gaming (I easily have over 30 hours clocked in Balatro across my devices), though the most taxing things I usually run are retro emulators. Being able to play full x86 PC titles locally on the device you already carry everywhere is a completely different beast – and why I am so excited by this project as a Steam Deck aficionado.

DroidDeck is a project that allows you to launch Valve’s Steam Big Picture interface natively on many recent Snapdragon-powered Android phones. It comes complete with the all-important Proton translation that has been so game-changing for Linux gaming, and it also includes custom graphics drivers for mobile devices.

What makes DroidDeck so approachable is that you do not need to mess around with terminal commands or complex emulation configurations. No piracy or ROMs required. It boots directly into the familiar Steam interface, allowing you to download and store titles locally on your internal storage, completely legally.

It’s very simple to get started; you simply grab the APK file from the official DroidDeck GitHub page. Install, sign in to your Steam account, and you’re 95% of the way to gaming.

During the initial DroidDeck setup, there is one critical step you cannot skip: select the appropriate Proton translation layer and enable the custom Mesa Turnip drivers in the application settings. This unlocks proper 3D acceleration on Snapdragon Adreno GPUs. If you boot up a game and cannot figure out why it is failing to launch, check that driver setting first. It was an issue I initially ran into, but I found out it was essential after some digging around in forums and Reddit posts.

Once you log into your Steam account, your library works exactly like it does on a Steam Deck.

You get full access to the store, automatic cloud saves plus backups, and even Steam Workshop support. When I booted up Brotato, all of my modded Workshop content carried over seamlessly. Better yet, because the games are installed locally, the entire experience works fully offline. Services like Nvidia GeForce NOW are great when you have a rock-solid connection, but because you’re running locally rather than streaming, DroidDeck makes PC gaming on your phone viable for a commute or a flight where you just want to play games unhindered.

Some of this may sound vaguely familiar if you have heard of another similar project called GameNative. It differs by acting as its own custom Android launcher rather than booting directly into Valve’s official Steam Big Picture interface. It is also built on top of existing open-source tools like Pluvia and Winlator; GameNative feels much more like a traditional mobile app that pulls in your libraries from Steam, Epic Games, GOG, and Amazon. Although you can add games from those platforms on DroidDeck if you really want to.

It has been around a bit longer and automatically applies community-shared configurations to get games running out of the box, though it relies on separate background containers per title rather than giving you that unified, console-like Steam Deck environment that makes DroidDeck feel like a better implementation strictly for gaming, in my opinion.

Just a quick cursory glance at Reddit of people using both, and it seems most people are having a better time with this implementation.

There is some good news and bad news with compatibility.

According to the official GitHub documentation, DroidDeck requires an Adreno 730 GPU or newer. That is the graphics chip paired with the Snapdragon 8 Gen 1, a processor that is roughly four years old now, meaning millions of phones out in the wild technically meet the minimum spec. Sadly, that list does not include any recent Google Pixel device. You could try to run on the beta track, but it’s not recommended.

Minimum specs and enjoyable specs are two very different things. Because DroidDeck is actively translating x86 PC instructions to run on ARM architecture, there is plenty of overhead. For a smooth experience right out of the gate, I recommend starting at a processor one generation newer than the Snapdragon 8 Gen 1.

If you want to pick up an older, cheaper flagship specifically to tinker with this, the OnePlus 12 is in a great sweet spot because it’s cheaper, has a great screen, and plenty of power. Make sure whatever device you use has at least 12GB of RAM (16GB is even better) and fast UFS 4.0 storage to help with in-game asset loading.

For my testing, I have been using a Galaxy S25 Ultra with DroidDeck installed for about 4 or 5 weeks so far. Talking about phones for my day job puts me in the privileged position of having last year’s flagship sitting around as a backup device, and its hardware is ideal for this. Modern flagship displays arguably outshine dedicated handhelds anyway. The 120Hz anti-glare AMOLED panel on the S25 Ultra is gorgeous and not much smaller than the Steam Deck OLED screen, meaning the Steam Big Picture UI scales cleanly with zero tiny, illegible text.

I wish I could say every single title in my library runs flawlessly, but that wouldn’t be completely honest. Since every game handles translation differently, performance varies wildly depending on the age and complexity of the game in question.

Older 3D classics and 2D indies are rock solid. I set my resolution to 1080p and jumped into Portal 2 for what is probably my sixth playthrough. It ran locked at 60fps, which is still mind-boggling to witness natively on a phone. Meanwhile, Burnout Paradise Remastered hit a fairly stable 60fps at 1080p with some dips into the high 40s, and a personal favorite, Dave the Diver, was solid but sometimes struggled to hit 45fps when lots of things were going on on-screen.

Dipping down to 720p is absolutely necessary for heavier titles, though let’s be honest, 720p (or 800p) is native for a Steam Deck anyway, so you’re not losing out if you want portability.

I was desperate to get Batman: Arkham Knight running nicely since I have been replaying it lately, and it landed right in the “not perfect, but just about playable” category. Even after tweaking the settings at 720p, it hovered around 30-40fps before it tanked hard when the skybox opened up and more assets were loaded in.

Cyberpunk 2077 failed to launch for me, which I expected, but I have no doubt in the future it will be running with some added work.

Beyond raw framerates, there are a few practical caveats you will run into immediately, starting with internal storage limits. Unless you bought a 512GB or 1TB phone, you cannot realistically keep three or four 50GB+ PC games installed alongside your daily apps and camera roll. I need space for photos and videos of my dog, so giving that up isn’t something I’m willing to do on my “main” phone. A backup or dedicated gaming handheld is fine, though, but with storage prices ballooning, it might be a costly side arm.

Thermal throttling is another unavoidable hurdle on a wafer-thin device with no active cooling. When a Snapdragon chip does this much heavy lifting, the chassis gets scorching hot fast, and performance eventually drops as the system throttles to protect itself.

Because of that excessive heat, a dedicated controller is practically mandatory just to keep from scalding your hands. All that thermal output also translates to rapid battery drain, meaning a demanding 3D game can easily wipe out a full charge in under an hour. Gaming phones with built-in cooling fans do exist, though I certainly wouldn’t recommend buying one just to squeeze out an extra twenty minutes of playtime here.

Even with the heat and storage hurdles, we are in the very early days of this project. Think of it this way: right now is the absolute worst that DroidDeck will ever be.

I don’t doubt we would have seen something like this without recent AI development progress, but talented devs have accelerated the work by tenfold in recent months. To go from a standing start to being able to run PC games on your phone almost natively is a heck of an achievement no matter which project you prefer.

As drivers mature and translation layers become more efficient, the ceiling for native PC gaming on Android is massive. At a time when dedicated gaming hardware and handheld PCs are ballooning in price, you no longer need to spend a fortune to play your Steam backlog on the go if you already own a powerful smartphone.

DroidDeck is completely free to download and tinker with, so you have nothing to lose by giving it a shot. Just make sure you have a spare controller handy before you boot it up.

#10462. How to Learn Java with AI: A Practical Guide for Beginners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Java has been one of the most in-demand programming languages for over two decades. It powers Android apps, banking systems, enterprise software, and huge backend platforms. The good news: learning it has never been easier. With AI tools like Claude, ChatGPT, and GitHub Copilot, you now have a patient tutor available at any hour.

The catch is that AI can help you learn faster or help you avoid learning at all. This guide shows you how to use it the right way.

Traditionally, learning Java meant reading a textbook, watching videos, and getting stuck on a compiler error for two hours with nobody to ask. AI removes much of that friction:

Instant explanations. Ask "what is the difference between an abstract class and an interface?" and get an answer pitched at your level.

Personalized pace. Ask for the same concept explained three different ways until it clicks.

Error decoding. Paste a confusing stack trace and get a plain-English explanation of what went wrong.

Unlimited practice. Generate fresh exercises on any topic, any time.

But AI is a tutor, not a replacement for practice. You still have to write the code yourself.

Before writing code, you need the JDK (Java Development Kit) and an IDE such as IntelliJ IDEA, Eclipse, or VS Code.

"I'm a complete beginner on Windows. Walk me through installing the JDK and IntelliJ IDEA, and help me run my first Hello World program."

If something goes wrong (and it often does with PATH variables), paste the exact error message and ask what it means.

Use AI to build understanding with analogies and examples.

"Explain Java loops like I'm a beginner. Give me one real-world analogy, then three short examples that get progressively harder."

Then close the chat and write your own loop from memory. That second step is where learning actually happens.

OOP is where many beginners struggle, and where AI shines as a tutor. Concepts like classes, objects, inheritance, polymorphism, encapsulation, and abstraction are easier to grasp when you can ask follow-up questions.

"Teach me inheritance in Java using a Vehicle, Car, and Bike example. Then quiz me with 5 questions, one at a time, and wait for my answer before giving feedback."

Asking the AI to quiz you, rather than just explain, turns passive reading into active recall.

Once you've written a program, ask AI to review it. This is one of the most valuable habits you can build.

"Review my Java code. Point out bugs, style issues, and anything that isn't idiomatic. Explain why each suggestion matters, but don't rewrite the whole thing for me."

That last sentence is important. You want feedback and understanding, not a finished solution you copy and paste.

When your code throws a NullPointerException or ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException, resist the urge to say "fix this." Instead, try:

"Here's my code and the error. Don't give me the answer yet. Ask me guiding questions so I can find the bug myself."

Debugging is a core professional skill. Using AI as a Socratic coach builds that skill instead of bypassing it.

Tutorials only take you so far. Projects are where Java sticks. Ask AI to suggest projects matched to your level:

Beginner: Number guessing game, to-do list in the console, simple calculator

Intermediate: Library management system, bank account simulator, student record manager

Advanced: REST API with Spring Boot, inventory system with a database, a chat application

"I know Java basics and OOP. Suggest a project I can finish in a weekend, break it into milestones, and don't write the code for me."

Copy-pasting without understanding. If you can't explain a line of code, you haven't learned it. Ask the AI to explain it line by line.

Trusting AI blindly. AI can produce code that looks right but is wrong, outdated, or insecure. Always run it, test it, and verify against official documentation.

Skipping the struggle. A little frustration is productive. Try for 15 to 20 minutes before asking for help.

Never writing code from scratch. Regularly practice without AI so you know what you can actually do on your own, especially before job interviews.

AI is a fantastic assistant, but it can't tell you what you don't know you need to learn. A structured course gives you a proven roadmap, so you cover the right topics in the right order, while AI fills in the gaps, answers your questions, and keeps you moving.

The best way to learn Java with AI is to treat it like a good mentor: ask it to explain, challenge, and review, but do the typing yourself. Learners who combine consistent practice with smart AI use progress faster than ever before.

#10458. AMD admits GPU driver bug is writing data continuously, wearing out SSDs

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Radeon drivers may be quietly racking up thousands of gigabytes in SSD writes

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

An SSD can spend most of its day doing very little, particularly when a PC is sitting idle. That may not be the case for some AMD Radeon systems, however, with reports suggesting that AMD’s External Events Utility (EEU), which is installed alongside Radeon drivers, may be writing data to the system driver continuously, even when the computer is barely being used. The issue drew attention again on November 8, when Reddit user ‘darklinkpower’ highlighted reports of the unusual drive activity.

One Radeon owner claimed the problem had been quietly eating into their drive's reported health for more than a year, writing: "I just found out that the driver eats up 1% of my SSD health every month for 16 months." Another user reported sustained writes of approximately 5MB/s while their PC was idle. Another one claimed their SSD health had dropped by about 1% every month for 16 months. AMD has acknowledged the problem. In an October 21 response on its community forum, a company representative confirmed that they were investigating. “Yes. We're looking into it with the aim to resolve it in a future driver release." He said. Another representative, AMAD Vik, subsequently offered a more encouraging update on Reddit, apologizing and saying, “This is hopefully fixed in the next one."

Notably, the EEU is not an entirely unfamiliar culprit. Last November, a similar issue involving AMD chipset drivers arose, with a Redditor claiming he had discovered that the software repeatedly updated a log file whenever on-screen elements were moved or resized. That incident also prompted questions about whether stopping the service would affect FreeSync. The latest reports are a different proposition, though, with some users now seeing writes that barely let up, even when their PCs are doing nothing.

The numbers are worth looking at. At 5 MB/s, an SSD would receive about 18GB of host writes per hour. Over a full day, that's 432GB, and a month of uninterrupted activity could push the figures close to 13TB. That, however, does not necessarily mean being written to the NAND flash itself. SSD controllers use caching and wear-leveling, and other techniques, to manage incoming data. How much of that reported activity thus translates into actual flash wear is still unknown. The user's claim of losing 1% of drive health each month also hasn't been independently confirmed.

Moreover, there doesn't appear to be clear patterns across Radeon driver versions. Reports include systems running Adrenalin 26.19.2, as well as older releases, suggesting the problem may not be limited to the latest update. AMD's October 30 release notes for Adrenalin 26.20.2 don't mention the issue either. For now, it's difficult to say which driver versions, hardware combinations, or system configurations are responsible.

That said, there are two reported workarounds. The first involves opening the Windows Services application by pressing Win + R, entering services.msc, and locating AMD EEU. Users can then stop the service or change its startup type to Disabled. That appears to stop excessive writeups, but isn’t necessarily a clean solution. The second workaround involves disabling Resizable Bar (ReBAR), which another user reported had stopped the disk activity without disabling the EEU. This, however, isn’t ideal either, as ReBAR can improve gaming performance by allowing the PC to access the GPU’S memory more efficiently.

For now, checking whether the problem actually exists on your PC is probably the most sensible first step. Open Windows Resource Monitor by pressing Win + R, typing resmon, and selecting the Disk tab. Sort by write activity and look for sustained writes from atieclxx.exe or atiesrxx.exe. AMD has indicated that a fix is in the works, but hasn’t published a confirmed release date. Until then, Radeon owners may want to monitor their storage activity rather than immediately disabling driver services or changing BIOS settings.

#10454. Top 4 MCP Servers for Marketers: Features, Use Cases, and Limitations

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You no longer need to build a dashboard every time you want to investigate a drop in traffic or compare campaign results. With servers built on the Model Context Protocol (MCP), you can ask an AI assistant a question and let it retrieve the relevant data directly from your marketing tools.

But connecting an assistant to Google Analytics is not the same as connecting it to HubSpot or Ahrefs. Each server exposes different data, capabilities and levels of access. Some are built for quick analysis inside one platform, while others support reporting across several channels. This guide compares four servers, what they can do, how they are set up and where their limits matter. Details such as plans and tool lists change often, so confirm them against each vendor's documentation before you commit.

The summary below reflects each vendor's documentation at the time of writing.

Google publishes an official, open-source Google Analytics MCP server for GA4. It connects AI assistants to the Admin and Data APIs, so it can list accounts and properties, run standard, realtime, funnel and conversion reports, and inspect custom dimensions and metrics. That makes it useful for investigating traffic changes, landing page performance and engagement trends.

Example prompt: "Which landing pages lost the most engaged sessions this month compared with last month?" The assistant can pull the relevant reports and help you spot changes, and you can then check whether the decline comes from specific channels, countries or devices.

Setup: The server runs locally. Google's documentation lists Python 3.10 or later, pipx or uvx, a Google Cloud project with the Analytics Admin and Data APIs enabled, application default credentials with the read-only Analytics scope, and at least Viewer access on the property you want to query.

Access: Google describes the server as available for read requests only, so it cannot change your Analytics configuration.

Limitations: It covers GA4 only, so it cannot combine website metrics with CRM records or advertising costs on its own. Running it locally also means you maintain the configuration yourself.

Best for: Web analysts and technical marketers who regularly investigate GA4 performance.

Marketing performance usually spans several platforms. Google Ads generates clicks, GA4 tracks site activity and a CRM records leads. Seeing the whole picture means bringing those metrics together.

Coupler is a no-code data integration platform that offers MCP access to prepared business data. It lists more than 400 supported data sources, including Google Ads, Facebook Ads, GA4, HubSpot, LinkedIn Ads and Shopify, and lets teams combine and transform data before an assistant queries it.

Example prompt: "What was our cost per lead across Google Ads, Meta Ads and LinkedIn last quarter?" For paid search specifically, Coulper describes a Google Ads MCP server that lets you ask about campaigns and compare them with other connected channels.

Setup: Coupler is hosted, so there is nothing to install locally. You connect your platforms, configure data flows, choose refresh schedules and connect a compatible AI assistant. According to the vendor, queries run against the prepared datasets, which is useful for recurring reports because teams can reuse refreshed data instead of preparing exports by hand.

Best for: Agencies and teams that need consistent reporting across advertising, website analytics, ecommerce and CRM platforms.

HubSpot's remote MCP server became generally available on April 13, 2026. It connects compatible AI clients to CRM data such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities, and can also read marketing objects such as campaigns, landing pages and blog posts. That helps marketers investigate lead quality, pipeline performance and lifecycle trends without reviewing records one by one.

Example prompt: "Which deals created last quarter originated from webinar leads, and how many closed?" The assistant can retrieve the records and examine conversion patterns, provided the attribution data exists.

Setup: HubSpot hosts the server. In your account you create an MCP auth app under Development, then connect an MCP client that supports OAuth 2.1 with PKCE to the HubSpot endpoint. See HubSpot's remote MCP server documentation for current steps.

Access: Unlike a purely analytical server, HubSpot supports selected create and update actions on records such as contacts, companies, deals, tickets and activities. Other objects, including marketing content, are read-only.

Limitations: Available operations differ by object type, and incomplete lead sources or inconsistent lifecycle stages will weaken any analysis.

Best for: B2B marketers and revenue operations teams that manage customers and pipeline in HubSpot.

Ahrefs offers a hosted MCP server that brings its SEO data into compatible assistants. It supports conversational research into keywords, backlinks, competing pages and organic visibility, so you can follow up with questions instead of exporting report after report.

Example prompt: "Which websites link to both of our main competitors but don't link to us?" The assistant can retrieve backlink data and help you identify outreach targets, which you can then narrow by Domain Rating or organic traffic.

Setup: According to the Ahrefs MCP documentation, the remote server is available on paid plans starting from Lite, and you authorize it through your AI client. Ahrefs also publishes a getting started guide.

Limitations: Calls made through MCP use your monthly API units in the same way as direct API usage, and rows per request depend on your plan. Ahrefs documents a default cap of 200,000 units per month for keys created through OAuth, which workspace admins can change. The data is also SEO-focused rather than first-party conversion analytics.

Best for: SEO specialists, content marketers and link builders who already use Ahrefs.

Yes, but start with the connections your team actually needs. Each additional server adds tools the assistant has to choose between, which increases complexity. Combining Ahrefs and HubSpot, for example, supports separate SEO and CRM investigations, but connecting several servers does not automatically create a reliable cross-platform reporting system.

For recurring reports, prepare consistent datasets and validate the first results against your existing dashboards. That catches errors caused by mismatched metrics or attribution rules.

Are MCP servers safe for marketing data? Security depends on the provider, the authentication method and the permissions you grant. Use trusted servers, review access scopes, and enable write operations only when necessary.

Do I need coding skills to use MCP servers? Not necessarily. Hosted services usually need less configuration, while local servers such as Google's need extra setup.

Which AI clients support MCP? Claude, ChatGPT, Cursor and other tools support MCP connections, though functionality and setup differ.

Do MCP servers cost extra? It depends on the provider. Some require paid plans, and others consume API credits or have usage limits.

Pick the server that matches the question you ask most often: Google Analytics MCP for GA4 behavior, Coupler for cross-channel reporting, HubSpot for CRM and lifecycle analysis, and Ahrefs for SEO research. Start with read-only access, check usage limits and permissions, and validate early answers against the reports you already trust.

#10453. Razer is getting back into wired earbuds with USB-C; yes, they have RGB

Razer just announced a pair of ANC earbuds with a cool take on USB-C, aimed directly at mobile gamers.

Wired earbuds are coming back. Not in the sense that they ever died for the hard-core audiophiles or those who want a simple audio solution, but from an investment perspective. Apparently, companies like Bose and now Razer think it’s a good time to start offering wired USB-C earbuds again, and I’m not complaining.

The Razer Hammerhead V3 Chroma are powered via USB-C. The gaming company decided to go with a right-angle USB-C connector, which positions them properly for landscape phone use rather than passive music listening.

The buds have ANC and come with an in-line mic with volume and playback controls, connected by a very green and very “Razer” round cable. They also wouldn’t be a Razer product without RGB, or “Chroma.” It’s not clear from the product images, but it looks like the Razer logo cycles colors on the back of each bud.

The new earbuds are a direct successor to the Hammerhead V2 earbuds, released just over 10 years ago. They’ve released several wired options since, but these are directly aimed at mobile gamers with support for PC gaming.

"We agree users should be explicitly informed"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Interaxon, the Canadian firm behind the Muse sleep and meditation headband, is one of them. Its devices monitor electrical activity in the brain, alongside other physiological measurements, while users sleep. The recordings are processed into reports that help customers track their rest and identify changes in their sleeping patterns. But there is another use for that information. Interaxon has already trained an AI model using about 50,000 hours of EEG recording and says it removes identifying information, including names, email, and addresses, before using the data. “We aim to use as much of that eligible corpus as possible,” Nadia Kumentas, its Chief Marketing Officer, said.

Notably, the idea behind these systems is particularly different from training a large language model. Instead of feeding AI an enormous amount of text, developers supply it with recordings of electrical activity in the brain. The model then learns recurring patterns that can be adapted to recognize different brain states or assist with specific research tasks. And there are already some interesting results. AI startup Piramidal has trained its model on more than 4 million hours of EEG data in partnership with Cleveland Clinic. The company developed a seizure-detection system that correctly identified nine out of 10 seizures in a pilot study after being fine-tuned on the clinic's recordings. That, however, doesn’t mean consumer headbands can suddenly read people's thoughts. Most wearable EEG devices use just one to six electrodes, considerably fewer than 19 to 25 typically found in clinical EEG systems. Also, their recordings are generally less comprehensive.

The industry has also been gathering neural recordings on a considerable scale. Last December, we covered Coundit’s effort to collect 10,000 hours of neural data from thousands of patients. The San Francisco startup built its own headset system to capture brain activity alongside speech and text, with the aim of developing AI capable of translating signals into language. Meta has also been working on BrainQwerty, a non-invasive brain-computer interface that translates brain activity into keyboard inputs. Its second-generation system improved average word accuracy to 61% from 40% in the earlier version, although the technology remains a far cry from practical everyday use.

#10450. Third Serengeti Sweep Tops 1,400 Arrests as Investigations Mature

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

Interpol's annual Operation Serengeti, now in its third iteration, led to 1,462 arrests across 20 African countries and the US, recovering $105 million in stolen funds.

A two-month operation in Africa led to the arrests of nearly 1,500 cybercrime suspects across 20 African countries and the US, the recovery of $105 million in stolen funds, and the shutdown of nearly 11,000 malicious networks.

The third annual roundup of cybercriminals and financial scammers, dubbed Operation Serengeti III, aimed to take down groups responsible for ransomware, phishing, and cyber-enabled fraud, Interpol stated. The operation expanded the impact of cyber-focused investigations from previous law enforcement efforts in 2024 and 2025, which netted 1,006 suspects in the original Operation Serengeti and 1,209 suspects in last year's effort, dubbed Operation Serengeti 2.0.

The increasing arrests show that the national law enforcement partners are becoming more mature in their investigative practices and collaboration across jurisdictions, says Neal Jetton, director for cybercrime at Interpol.

Related:UK Wades Into Tech Sovereignty Amid AI Policy Changes

"Rising arrest figures reflect stronger enforcement activity, not a change in the scale of offending," he says. "The success of law enforcement efforts should be judged by whether fewer people are victimized over time and not by the number of suspects arrested or victims identified during a specific operation."

The crackdown came as cybercriminals in the region continue to develop more sophistication in their operations, with a survey of African members finding that 72% of the 36 African countries have discovered a criminally operated scam center and more than half of cybercrime cases involved the use of AI — 8% frequently and 47% occasionally, according to Interpol's "African Cyberthreat Assessment Report 2026."

The data highlights the rapid improvements in cybercriminal infrastructure, says Jacomo Piccolini, vice president of global data partnerships at Team Cymru.

"Scams that once looked like individual schemes now run like businesses, with recruitment pipelines, international staff, and, increasingly, trafficked workers," he says.

In Uganda, for example, two transnational criminal organizations (TCOs) used a rotating call-center model with scripted communications, fraudulent websites, and fake trading platforms to convince elderly victims in Asia to invest in their financial scams, Interpol said in a statement on Operation Serengeti III's impact. Ugandan law enforcement authorities successfully dismantled the fraud rings, arresting 365 foreign nationals, consisting almost entirely of citizens from various Asian nations, Interpol's Jetton says.

Related:UK Warns Its Critical Infrastructure Faces Grave Risk

In another arm of the operation, national police arrested nine suspects in Angola, dismantling two illegal cryptocurrency mining sites and seizing more than 2,000 devices used to mine crypto along with six illegal power transformers valued at more than US $1 million. Cybercriminal mining operations are a common way to generate cryptocurrency, the favored method of cashing out on illegal schemes, Jetton says.

"Cryptocurrency played a significant role in moving the proceeds," Jetton says. "It is now the principal rail for this type of fraud."

In Nigeria, police shuttered two fraud operations: One using 2.1 million email addresses to create a large-scale phishing and business email compromise (BEC) operation, while another scheme focused on international investment fraud and garnered at least US $2.4 million in profits, Interpol stated.

The schemes showed off the role that AI is taking in enabling cybercriminal campaigns. The phishing and BEC campaigns used email addresses and AI agents to scrape information from LinkedIn and other social media to identify high-value targets, Jetton says.

"AI enabled the threat actor to create highly personalized phishing emails by mimicking the writing style of executives, colleagues, and suppliers," he says, adding that "AI helped criminals manage and prioritize millions of email addresses for large-scale campaigns and, in some cases, combined with voice cloning, deepfake videos, and AI chatbots to make social engineering attacks even more convincing."

Related:UAE Transitions 50% of Government Services to Agentic AI

Law enforcement collaboration is making the individual nations more effective in tackling transnational cybercrime. With international partners, the individual countries are able to gain intelligence on signs of cybercriminal activities in their country, says Team Cymru's Piccolini.

"The biggest change is timing. Since Serengeti 2.0, investigators have gone in with intelligence on suspicious IP addresses, domains, and command-and-control servers before the operation begins, along with training in open source, crypto, and ransomware investigation."

Without such collaboration, TCOs could evade repercussions, especially if they limited their activities to impacting only citizens of other nations, as in the case of the Asian investment fraud ring.

"Actors targeting victims in Europe and North America use infrastructure spread across multiple jurisdictions, which makes it harder to pursue through any single country," Piccolini says. "That’s a big part of why international operations like Serengeti, with partners who can see across borders, matter so much."

Both Interpol and Team Cyrmu stressed that several operations are ongoing, with investigators continuing to use seized data and other information to pursue suspected cybercriminals.

Rob is an award-winning, veteran technology journalist of more than 30 years, reporting on global cybersecurity issues, the latest offensive and defensive technologies, malware incidents, cyber conflict, and AI's impact on software and cybersecurity.

A former research engineer, Rob has written for more than two dozen publications, including CNET News.com, Dark Reading, MIT's Technology Review, Popular Science, and Wired News. He has received five awards for journalism, including Best Deadline Journalism (Online) in 2003 for his coverage of the Blaster worm. Rob also analyzes data on various trends using Python and R for both his reporting and his clients. Recent reports include analyses of the shortage in cybersecurity workers, annual vulnerability trends, and annual threat reports.

Unstoppable AMD X3D chip just hit a new low price

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Put simply, AMD dominates the gaming CPU world right now. While the AMD Ryzen 7 9800X3D isn't the most powerful, it's still the best option for gamers, offering a good performance-to-power ratio. It's simply a powerhouse, and right now, it's down to a new record low price of $399.99 at multiple retailers. Considering the performance you can get with a 9800X3D, you'll want to grab it if you've got an AM5 build.

Both Newegg and Amazon have it at this record price, but you'll need to apply some coupons to get the price. At Newegg, that means using the coupon code BFF36 at checkout to knock $70 off its current list price, while at Amazon, you'll need to click or tap to apply the $49.01 coupon, shown below the price.

There aren't many options for gaming that beat the AMD Ryzen 7 9800X3D. It's a powerhouse that was, until recently, the best CPU for gaming in the world, beating every other chip due to its boosted L3 cache capacity. You're getting an eight-core, 16-thread processor, made using AMD's current-gen Zen 5 architecture, with a boost clock speed of 5.2 GHz, and a 120W TDP.

The 9800X3D is fully unlocked, so it's possible to overclock it, which was a limitation in older 3D V-cache chips from AMD. It's that extra L3 cache that makes this such a special chip, too, stacked beneath the CPU cores closer to the heat spreader on a reconfigured die.

This 8-core X3D beast of a processor is on sale at its lowest ever price. While the Ryzen 7 9850X3D is faster by around 3%, the benchmarks prove how close of a run it is, with this CPU requiring 30% more power for almost the same performance.

Grab it from Newegg instead for $399.99 using the coupon code BFF36.

Faster chips do technically exist, like the AMD Ryzen 7 9850X3D, which our CPU benchmark data shows is 3% faster at 1080p than the 9800X3D, although with much higher power demands. The 9800X3D offers the best performance per watt of any other gaming CPU, so you can really maximise performance without a huge increase in electrical power. You can see, too, from the Bench charts below that the 9800X3D sits very close to the top as far as CPU performance goes.

Put simply, if you pair this processor with a recent GPU, you won't see many bottlenecks. Pair it with a top-class option like the RTX 5080 or RTX 5090, and you'll be hitting high frame rates in any game you can throw at it. CPU-intensive games like Cyberpunk 2077 won't be let down by that 96MB of L3 cache included in this X3D chip.

Given how good a processor this is, and how little Intel has been able to rival it, we can't see it falling out of date for a long while yet. It's sitting at the top of the food chain and will continue to dominate games for years into the future. AMD's AM5 platform, meanwhile, remains in use, and AMD has promised to support it for at least one more generation.

If you want even better performance, though, then the 9850X3D (which, as we mentioned earlier, does manage to beat the 9800X3D by 3%) is also on sale. You can pick up the 9850X3D for $419 at Newegg right now, thanks to a $65 promo code, a significant discount on its launch price. You'll need to apply the BFF39 code at checkout at Newegg to get the discount, or apply the on-screen code if you buy it at Amazon.

Built on AMD’s Zen 5 architecture, the AMD Ryzen 7 9850X3D combines 8 cores and 16 threads with a 5.6GHz boost clock and 96MB of 3D V-Cache.

#10445. How Developers Can Improve Their Learning and Productivity With AI

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Artificial intelligence (AI) is changing how developers learn, write code, and manage their daily work. Tasks that once required hours of searching, testing, and manual effort can now be completed with help from AI. Developers can use it to understand complex programming concepts, find coding errors, learn new technologies, and complete repetitive tasks.

However, AI is not only useful for writing code faster. It can also help developers improve their technical knowledge, solve problems, and build better learning habits. When used correctly, it can become a useful assistant throughout the software development process.

The key is to understand where AI can help and where human skills are still needed. Here are several practical ways developers can use AI to improve their learning and productivity.

Learning a new programming language or understanding a difficult concept can take time. Developers often need to read long explanations, watch tutorials, and test different examples before they understand how something works.

AI can make this process easier by explaining technical concepts in simple language. Instead of searching through several websites, developers can ask direct questions and receive explanations based on their current knowledge.

For example, a developer learning JavaScript can ask AI to explain promises, callbacks, or asynchronous functions. It can break these topics into smaller parts and provide simple code examples to make them easier to understand.

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain the same concept in different ways. If one explanation is difficult to understand, a different example or comparison may make the idea clearer.

AI explanations are not always accurate, especially when dealing with complex programming concepts or unfamiliar technologies. Developers should verify important information before using it in their projects. Hallucination detection can help identify fabricated facts, incorrect technical explanations, and misleading information in AI-generated responses, making it easier to check the accuracy of what they learn.

This helps developers spend less time struggling with unfamiliar topics and more time building their technical knowledge.

Software development changes regularly. New frameworks, libraries, programming languages, and development methods continue to appear. Developers need to keep learning if they want to stay up to date.

AI can support this process by helping developers create learning plans based on their goals and current skills.

For example, a developer who wants to learn Python can ask AI to create a beginner-friendly learning plan. The plan can include basic syntax, variables, loops, functions, object-oriented programming, and small projects.

AI can also recommend practical exercises for each topic. Instead of only reading about a programming concept, developers can write code and test their understanding.

Here are some ways to use AI when learning new technologies:

However, developers should not depend entirely on AI-generated explanations. They should also read official documentation and test examples themselves. This helps them understand how technologies work in real development environments.

AI can help developers improve their coding skills by providing examples, explaining different solutions, and identifying areas that need improvement.

For example, a developer may write a function that works but contains unnecessary steps. They can ask AI to review the code and suggest ways to make it easier to read, maintain, or understand.

AI can also explain why one solution may be better than another. This allows developers to learn different approaches instead of always following the first solution they find.

Some practical ways to use AI for coding improvement include:

Developers can also ask AI to create small coding challenges based on their skill level. For example, someone learning Python can request exercises about lists, dictionaries, loops, and functions.

The important thing is to understand the code instead of copying it without review. Developers who examine the logic behind each solution can improve their programming knowledge over time.

Developers learn new information from many sources, including online courses, technical blogs, documentation, tutorials, and coding projects. Without a proper system, it can become difficult to find useful information when they need it again.

AI can help developers organize this knowledge and make learning resources easier to review.

For example, developers can use AI to summarize long technical explanations, create revision questions, and organize important programming concepts into smaller topics. They can also turn their learning notes into structured study guides that make it easier to revisit important information.

A well-organized knowledge system can be useful when learning a new framework or preparing for technical interviews. Instead of searching through old browser tabs or reading the same documentation repeatedly, developers can return to their saved explanations and notes.

Here are some useful ways to organize technical knowledge:

Technical documentation can contain detailed explanations that are difficult to remember. Developers can use AI to summarize important sections and turn them into short notes.

For example, when learning a new API, they can save information about its main functions, parameters, common errors, and practical examples.

Reading information does not always mean remembering it. Developers can ask AI to create questions from their notes so they can test their understanding.

For example, after learning about database indexing, they can create questions about how indexes work, when to use them, and how they affect query performance.

Developers should also save important information about their projects. This may include coding decisions, API details, database structures, and solutions to common problems.

Keeping these details organized makes it easier to return to a project after a break or help another developer understand the work.

Developers spend a large part of their working time on tasks that do not always require complex problem-solving. These may include writing basic code structures, creating test cases, preparing documentation, and handling repeated coding patterns.

AI can help reduce this manual effort by generating suggestions and automating parts of these tasks.

For example, when starting a new project, developers often need to create similar code structures, such as basic functions, configuration files, and reusable components. An AI coding assistant can generate an initial version that developers can review and adjust.

Automating these activities gives developers more time to focus on important parts of their projects, such as system design, complex features, and problem-solving.

However, generated code should always be checked before it is added to a project. Developers need to make sure it follows project requirements, coding standards, and security rules.

Finding and fixing bugs is an important part of software development. Even experienced developers can spend hours trying to understand why a program is not working as expected.

AI can help make debugging easier by explaining error messages, identifying possible causes, and suggesting solutions.

For example, when a developer encounters a Python error, they can share the relevant code and error message with an AI assistant. The assistant can explain what the error means and suggest possible ways to fix it.

AI can also help developers review code before submitting it. It may identify common issues such as:

Another useful approach is to ask AI to explain why a particular bug occurs instead of only requesting a corrected version of the code.

This helps developers understand the problem and avoid making the same mistake in future projects.

Still, AI suggestions are not always correct. Developers should test proposed fixes, review the surrounding code, and confirm that the solution does not introduce new problems.

Managing several development tasks at once can be difficult. Developers may need to work on new features, fix bugs, review code, attend meetings, and meet project deadlines.

AI can help organize these responsibilities by breaking large tasks into smaller and more manageable steps.

For example, if a developer needs to build a user authentication system, they can ask AI to divide the work into separate tasks. These may include creating registration forms, setting up database tables, implementing login logic, adding password protection, and testing the system.

This makes it easier to understand the work involved and decide what needs attention first.

AI can also help explain technical requirements before development begins. This may reduce confusion and help developers identify missing details early.

However, AI-generated plans should be treated as starting points. Developers still need to consider their actual deadlines, technical limitations, and project requirements before following them.

Although AI offers many benefits, depending on it for every programming task can affect a developer's ability to solve problems independently.

If developers always ask AI to write code, fix errors, and explain every concept, they may miss opportunities to develop their own technical skills.

A 2026 Anthropic study found that participants using AI scored 17 percentage points lower on a programming knowledge quiz than participants who worked without AI in the experiment. This highlights why understanding the work still matters when using coding assistants.

Developers can avoid excessive dependence on AI by following a few simple practices:

When facing a coding problem, spend some time understanding it and trying possible solutions. If you get stuck, use AI to explore the issue rather than asking it to complete everything.

Never add AI-generated code to a project without understanding what it does. Review the logic, check the functions, and test the output.

AI can sometimes provide outdated information, incorrect code, or explanations that do not fit a particular project. Check important details against official documentation and reliable technical resources.

Regular coding practice without assistance helps developers maintain their problem-solving skills. It also shows whether they truly understand a programming concept.

The best approach is to use AI as a helpful assistant while keeping independent thinking and technical judgment at the center of development work.

Using AI occasionally can be helpful, but developers can get more value from it by making it part of a regular learning and working routine.

A simple daily routine can help developers balance their project responsibilities with continuous learning.

For example, developers can follow this approach:

Start with a learning goal: Spend some time understanding a new programming concept, framework, or technology. Use AI to explain difficult topics and provide practical examples.

Plan the day's development tasks: Ask AI to help organize important tasks and break complex work into smaller steps.

Use AI during development: Get help with unfamiliar code, debugging, test cases, and repetitive tasks. Review all suggestions before using them.

Save useful information: Keep important explanations, coding examples, and solutions in an organized knowledge base for future reference.

Review what you learned: At the end of the day, revisit important concepts and test your understanding through small questions or coding exercises.

Developers do not need to spend hours following this routine. Even a short period of focused learning each day can help them build knowledge while managing their regular development work.

AI is becoming a useful part of modern software development. It can help developers understand difficult concepts, learn new technologies, improve code quality, debug problems, and complete repetitive tasks more efficiently.

However, better productivity does not simply mean writing more code in less time. Developers also need to understand what they are building, improve their problem-solving skills, and keep learning as technology changes.

The most effective approach is to use AI for support rather than letting it make every decision. By combining AI assistance with regular practice, independent thinking, and organized learning, developers can improve both their technical skills and everyday productivity.

#10441. Huawei develops 'entirely Chinese' homegrown desktop PC system

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Goodbye, Wintel? Software for Windows too?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Huawei has introduced its first desktop PCs powered by its own 10-core processor as well as proprietary HarmonyOS operating system, reports the Southern China Morning Post. The company claims that the CPU and the OS are built entirely of using 'components manufactured' in China, which matters when it comes national security and domestic supply chains inside the sanctioned country. But whether these machines are truly 'entirely domestic' is an open question.

The Huawei Qinyun HM650 and HM650g desktop PCs come with 16 GB of LPDDR5 memory (probably made by China's CXMT) and either a 256GB or 512GB SSD (probably made by Chinese flash maker YMTC) are powered by the company's Kirin X90 system-on-chip, which originally debuted in Huawei's consumer systems in 2025. The key ingedient of the system it its processor, which features 10 Huawei-designed Armv8-based Taishan CPU cores in a 4+4+2 configuration, with all cores supporting simultaneous multithreading (SMT) to execute up to 20 threads simultameusly. The chip also integrates Huawei's Maleoon graphics processing unit (Arm Mali-based) and a dedicated neural processing unit for AI workloads.

Although Huawei doesn't officially disclose the Kirin X90's manufacturing process, a teardown analysis by TechInsights confirmed that the CPU is manufactured by SMIC using its 7nm-class N+2 process technology. This makes the X90 an example of Huawei's ability to develop PC-class processors using domestically available semiconductor manufacturing capabilities, albeit on a process technology that trails leading-edge nodes from Intel, TSMC, and Samsung.

The most interesting aspect of the Qinyun HM650 series is certainly Huawei's claim that all of its new system's components, including the processor, memory, storage, power supply, and wireless connectivity are manufactured domestically. Such certification itself does not necessarily mean that the entire semiconductor supply chain behind the machine is Chinese. While the CPU, memory, and storage chips are fabricated domestically, they are still made using foreign manufacturing equipment (from the Netherlands, U.S., and Japan), materials (from Japan), intellectual property (from the UK), and electronic design automation (EDA software from the U.S. and partly from China) tools.

Meanwhile, the claim about the operating system developed in China sounds ambitious. The machines run Huawei's HarmonyOS Enterprise Edition, which certainly reduces their dependence on foreign technology suppliers. Unlike conventional PCs that rely on Microsoft's Windows operating system, Huawei's desktops use a software platform developed and maintained by a Chinese company. Furthermore, unlike earlier HarmonyOS versions that incorporated Linux components, the HarmonyOS Enterprise Edition uses Huawei's own kernel and software architecture. This indeed gives Huawei greater control over operating system development, security updates, and integration with its hardware, although compatibility with existing Windows applications could remain a limitation for enterprise customers that rely on software by multinational corporations for Windows.

Keeping all these things in mind, can we say that Huawei's Qinyun HM650 series relies entirely on components developed and made in China? Probably yes. Yet, with major caveats, including a semiconductor supply chain that relies on tools and software designed and produced elsewhere. Furthermore, incompatibility of the OS with software designed for Windows could be a limitation for some customers.

#10439. Payday Aces High review: This year’s must-play multiplayer VR game

Join up with three other heisters and get revenge on the billionaire who sent you to jail in the first place in Payday Aces High, now available on Meta Quest, SteamVR, and Steam Frame.

Payday Aces High is the return to the series’ roots I had been hoping for. Payday 3 wasn’t received particularly well, and while the team behind it has turned things around in the past few years, it’s pretty clear that Payday 2 is still everyone’s favorite entry. But that stops for me now as we enter a new chapter in the series, with four new characters, a new “villain,” and made-for-VR mechanics that shine.

I’ve been playing Payday Aces High throughout the alpha and beta stages and got access to the final review build a week ago. Since then, my son and I have robbed banks, stolen from museums, hoisted a fragile quantum computer out of an icy compound, and generally made life a living hell for Warren Jupiter, the man who made billions framing the characters you play as in Payday Aces High.

If you’re familiar with Payday games, this one will mostly feel like a return to the original game with new mechanics in tow. Some of the fan-favorite mechanics from Payday 2 (and even 3) aren’t here — you can’t take hostages, for example — but other mechanics, like gun upgrades and perks, will keep you coming back over and over again.

While you can choose from the four main Aces characters — the Ace of Hearts, Clubs, Diamonds, and Spades — the character itself is just a visual change rather than a stats-based one. You customize your own stats throughout the adventure as you unlock points and earn money. You’ll also collect the infamous masks that each character dons, including DLC sets (and missions) scheduled for the next few months.

Payday Aces High begins with six unlockable missions, while a seventh free one (named Coup de Grace) is landing on December 14. Developer Fast Travel Games is a VR veteran and is hosting community challenges throughout the next few months, unlocking special loot when completed. The game has full crossplay support, so it should be very easy to find and invite friends, no matter which platform they prefer.

I played the standalone Quest 3 version, which looks quite nice visually and sports great performance. VR-specific mechanics are excellent, including on-body inventory, fun skill check-style minigames to keep equipment working, physical interaction with locks and other environmental details, and lots of shooting with physics-based gun mechanics. Hacking a computer while shooting in a completely different direction is something that only makes sense in VR, and I love leaning into those types of mechanics.

The biggest miss of Payday Aces High is not providing enough bespoke VR mechanics. You can’t grab characters, can’t break glass with a melee attack, and the stealth leaves a bit to be desired. I would love to be able to toss an object across the room to distract guards for a moment, for instance, as it would fit the medium very well.

But outside of a few immersion-breaking decisions like this, the action is incredibly fun, challenging, and, maybe most important of all, rewarding. My son and I have completed several missions many times over in hopes of perfecting the formula, and the increasing complexity and difficulty of each has proven to be an enjoyable challenge. Payday Aces High is my multiplayer VR game of the year, hands down, not just because I already loved the series, but because I find co-op multiplayer to be the most fun thing to do in a VR headset.

#10435. How Motion Design Improves SaaS Onboarding and Feature Adoption

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most SaaS products don't lose users because the software is bad. They lose users in the first few minutes, before anyone has seen what the product can actually do. Someone signs up, lands on an empty dashboard, feels a flicker of confusion, and quietly leaves. The industry has a name for the gap between signing up and getting value: activation. Closing it is one of the hardest problems in product design.

Motion design is one of the most underused tools for closing that gap. Used well, animation isn't decoration sprinkled on at the end. It's a layer of communication that tells users where to look, confirms that their actions worked, and makes a complex interface feel learnable instead of overwhelming. This article looks at why motion influences behaviour during onboarding, the specific patterns that move the needle on activation and feature adoption, and how to build them responsibly as a developer — with an eye on performance and accessibility throughout.

Motion works in onboarding because it speaks to how human perception actually operates, not because it looks modern. A few principles do most of the heavy lifting.

It directs attention. The eye is drawn to movement before it reads a single word. A gently pulsing button or an element that slides into view tells a new user where to go next without a paragraph of instructions. On a crowded first-run screen, that guidance is the difference between a confident first action and a confused exit.

It preserves context. When a panel expands, a card flips, or a new view slides in from the right, a short transition shows users where things came from and where they went. This spatial continuity keeps people oriented. Interfaces that snap instantly between states force the user to re-scan the screen each time and silently rebuild their mental model.

It confirms actions. Every click, toggle, and form submission is a small question: did that work? A quick visual response — a checkmark drawing itself, a row settling into place — answers it immediately. Without that feedback, users repeat actions, doubt the system, or assume something broke.

It shapes perceived performance. A thoughtful loading animation or skeleton screen makes a wait feel shorter and the product feel responsive, even when the underlying request takes exactly as long. Perception of speed is part of the experience, and motion is how you manage it.

These are the patterns that reliably help new users reach their first win.

Onboarding doesn't end after the first session. Most SaaS products keep shipping features, and each one faces its own adoption problem: users settle into a few familiar workflows and never discover the rest. Motion is how you reintroduce the product to people who already think they know it.

Contextual nudges that appear in motion — a tooltip that eases in near a newly relevant button, a gentle highlight on a feature the moment a user hits the limit it solves — are far more effective than a generic email. They arrive at the point of need and use movement to pull attention without hijacking the screen.

"What's new" reveals can announce an update with a small, well-timed animation rather than a modal that gets dismissed on reflex. A badge that animates in, or a panel that slides open on the first visit to a page, respects the user's flow while still being noticed.

Animated demonstrations carry the most weight for genuinely complex features. Showing a feature in motion — rather than describing it in text — lets users grasp a multi-step workflow in seconds. For intricate capabilities, this is where a short, purpose-made explainer earns its place, a point we return to in the next section.

The right tool depends on how complex the animation is and where it lives. Roughly in order of weight:

CSS transitions and keyframe animations should be your default. They cover the large majority of onboarding motion — hovers, state changes, highlights, progress bars — and the browser can run them efficiently. Reach for JavaScript only when CSS genuinely can't express what you need.

The Web Animations API gives you CSS-level performance with programmatic control, which matters when animations depend on data or user input, or need to be sequenced and interrupted. SitePoint's guide to the Web Animations API is a solid introduction, and the broader web animation archive covers libraries like GSAP for orchestrating complex timelines.

Lottie is the bridge between designer-made animation and the web. Designers export vector animations as JSON, and a lightweight player renders them natively at small file sizes — ideal for polished illustrative moments like success states and animated empty states, without hand-coding every frame.

Pre-rendered video is the right call when a feature is genuinely involved and a looping CSS animation or a tooltip simply can't carry the explanation. A short, well-produced walkthrough can compress a multi-step workflow into something a user absorbs in under a minute, which is why many teams lean on a dedicated SaaS explainer video for their most complex features and onboarding moments. The trade-off is weight and production effort, so reserve video for the handful of places where it genuinely outperforms in-interface motion, lazy-load it, and never autoplay it with sound.

Motion that stutters does more damage than no motion at all, and motion that ignores accessibility can actively harm users. Two disciplines keep animation on the right side of that line.

For performance, animate the properties the browser can handle cheaply: transform and opacity. These can run on the compositor thread without triggering layout or paint, which is what lets animation hold a smooth 60fps. Animating properties like width, height, top, or left forces the browser to recalculate layout on every frame and is the usual cause of janky motion. Keep animations short — most interface transitions should land between 150 and 300 milliseconds — because motion that overstays its welcome makes a product feel slow.

For accessibility, honour the prefers-reduced-motion media query. Some users experience dizziness, nausea, or disorientation from movement, and the operating system lets them ask for less of it. Respecting that setting is straightforward:

Beyond that, never let an animation block interaction. If a user has to wait for a flourish to finish before they can click, the motion is working against them. Animation should support the task and then get out of the way.

Motion design is a product decision, so treat it like one and measure it. The metrics that matter map directly to the problems motion is meant to solve.

Treat animations as hypotheses and A/B test them. Ship the animated onboarding step to half of your new users and measure activation against the control. Motion should earn its place with data, not assumptions — and occasionally the data will tell you an animation is slowing people down, which is just as useful to know.

The best onboarding motion is the kind users never consciously notice. It simply makes the product feel clear, responsive, and easy to learn — guiding attention, confirming actions, and turning a complex interface into something approachable. That's the real payoff: not animation for its own sake, but fewer confused users, faster time to value, and features that actually get discovered.

Start small. Pick the single point in your onboarding where users most often stall, add one purposeful animation that removes the confusion, and measure what happens. Motion, used deliberately, is one of the most direct levers you have on activation and adoption — and like any good tool, it works best when it draws no attention to itself.

Free Bash script files your details, rotating through eight subject lines, 10 humorous message boilerplates, and six signoffs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A new GitHub repository dubbed ram-altman features a script to monitor your PC’s RAM usage and report back to the eponymous OpenAI boss. Sam Altman infamously described people having spare, underutilized DRAM in computers at home as “quite terrible” in a recent podcast. Thus, developer Iain Kay was inspired to write a bash script that emails the OpenAI boss once a day to report on any RAM that is underutilized. Clearly this is not a serious tool, or rather “The memory accounting is. The correspondence isn't,” says Kay.

ram-altman leans on the MemAvailable query, which returns the kernel's own estimate of how much memory programs can claim without swapping, explains the developer of this script. When installed, it checks every five minutes and emails Altman once a day if you are found to be wasting this precious resource.

The emails take the .CSV file logs of your RAM use throughout the day as the data source to compose a summary email. These script-generated messages rotate through eight subject lines, 10 humorous message boilerplates, and six signoffs. Sample emails can be seen on the ram-altman.lol website. Altman’s email address isn’t supplied as part of the script.

If your machine is using 90% or more of its RAM, then you won’t be reported to the OpenAI boss for wasting memory. Also, if you don’t want to disturb Altman, you can run the tests without emailing anyone with the terminal command: bash ram-altman.sh --dry-run

The developer isn’t too scathing of the OpenAI boss. Kay quotes Altman’s explanations that his remarks were taken out of context. Moreover, the dev doffs his cap to the artificial intelligence leader’s humorous display name change to ‘Ram Altman’ on X last week. That’s where the name for this script came from.

ram-altman is a free bash script available on GitHub. It runs natively on Linux. On Windows, you can use WSL to run it, but someone has ported it for PowerShell too. It won’t run on Mac, but Altman and his minions would find it inconvenient, at best, to remove your soldered-down Apple RAM.

China's 800MW AI campus takes on Stargate with wind power, batteries and 800V DC

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

China is building an 800MW AI data center campus in Inner Mongolia that could challenge the economics of America's Stargate project, according to a new report from SemiAnalysis. Envision has built the first 120 MW of Galaxy AI data center campus in less than a year, a cost the researchers put at just one-fifth of comparable US projects, after visiting the site in September.

But getting the buildings up is only half of the battle. AI data centers need enormous amounts of electricity, and Envision is taking an unusual route to secure it— building its own wind farms and battery storage alongside the computing facility.

While US developers are turning to on-site gas turbines to work around grid constraints, Envision is building its data center alongside wind farms, battery storage, and dedicated power infrastructure. Galaxy is located in Ulanqab, Inner Mongolia, and is being developed across four phases for two unnamed internet companies. The completed campus is planned to provide up to 800MW of IT capacity by the end of 2027, putting it closer to the approximately 820MW of critical IT capacity at the Stargate campus in Abilene, Texas.

Envision is building its own power supply for Galaxy rather than leaving everything to the local grid. So far, the company has installed more than 100GW of wind turbines and wants its wind farms to provide over 60% of the electricity needed by the campus’s first two phases. Those farms, located about 80 kilometers apart, have a combined capacity of 420MW. That's well above the 240MW load expected from the first two phases, although wind turbines rarely get to run flat out. Ulanqab’s wind capacity factor is between 355 and 40%, so Envision is adding four-hour battery storage to keep the electricity coming when the wind dies down.

Power equipment is proving rather hard to come by for some US data center builders. We have previously reported on turbine shortages that have pushed delivery estimates to 2030, while SpaceX is looking to manufacture its own turbine blades for its gas-powered AI facilities. Envision, meanwhile, is putting wind generation and battery storage right alongside its servers.

The power hardware inside the Galaxy is worth a look, too. Semianalyss visited a 5MW hall where solid-state transformers take 10kV AC electricity and convert it directly into 800V DC for the server racks. That's a fairly substantial departure from conventional data-center power distribution, which typically involves several intermediate conversion stages. “In one 5MW hall, we saw the 800V system at work,” the researchers wrote. They explained that “Solid-state transformers (SSTs) take 10kV AC from the grid and convert it straight to 800V DC for the racks.”

The design removes several intermediate components found in conventional data center power systems, including low voltage transformers, uninterruptible power supplies and rectifiers. Fewer conversion stages can reduce equipment requirements and energy losses, although SemiAnalysis did not provide a measured efficiency improvement for Galaxy’s pilot. The 5MW installation is only a small part of the campus, but it puts the technology into operation at a live facility. Envision, however, plans an even larger deployment in the next construction phases, a move that aligns with Nvidia's push for 800V DC distribution in future high-density AI systems.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Galaxy's campus uses a cooling arrangement in which liquid-cooled and air-cooled servers share a single water loop, eliminating the need for conventional central chiller plants. That matters as AI servers generate more heat and require increasingly sophisticated cooling systems. Removing central chillers can simplify infrastructure, although the design depends on local climate conditions and the facility's ability to reject heat through its water loop. China has previously experimented with other unconventional cooling methods, including a wind-powered underwater datacenter off Shanghai that uses surrounding seawater to help cool its servers.

That said, Galaxy is the first major project in Invision's wider Inner Mongolia datacentre plans, which Semianalysis says add up to 3GW, with the rest of the campus still a busy construction site. The surrounding Chahar High-tech Zone is also expanding rapidly, with nearly 30,000 construction workers spread across 22 data center projects, according to a local report cited by the researchers.

The wider Chinese expansion is also attracting some of the world's largest technology companies. Alibaba, Huawei and ByteDance are among the firms expanding infrastructure in the region, which benefits from relatively cheap electricity, available land and abundant wind resources. However access to electricity and datacenter buildings does not automatically translate to available AI computing power. China's access to advanced accelerators remains a separate constraint, particularly as US export restrictions limit its ability to acquire Nvidia’s most powerful chips.
