#10426. Why Your AI Agent Can't Read the Web (and How to Build a Fetch Layer That Can)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

This is the least glamorous part of building with LLMs and it's where a lot of agent projects quietly fall over. The model is rarely the problem. The fetch is. In this article we'll look at why live web retrieval has become so unreliable for AI tools, what the numbers say about the shift, and how to build a small, well-behaved fetch layer in Python that fails loudly instead of feeding your model garbage.

A few years ago, a plain requests.get() worked on most of the public web. That's no longer true, and the reason is simple: there are now more bots than people.

According to the 2025 Imperva Bad Bot Report, automated traffic made up 51% of all web traffic in 2024. That's the first time in a decade it has passed human activity. Bad bots alone (scrapers, credential stuffers, scalpers) went from 32% to 37% of traffic in a single year.

Source: Imperva Bad Bot Report 2024 and 2025

From a site owner's point of view, your polite research agent and a credential-stuffing botnet look much the same at the network edge. Both are non-browser clients making fast requests from data-center IP ranges. So the defaults have tightened. In July 2025 Cloudflare began blocking known AI crawlers by default for new customers, and bot management products now look at TLS fingerprints, header order, IP reputation and JavaScript execution, not just the User-Agent string.

It helps to understand the publisher's side of the deal, because it explains why your agent is being treated with suspicion.

Cloudflare splits AI crawling into three purposes: training (collecting data to train models), search (building an index for AI search answers) and user action (fetching a page because a person just asked a question). Training dominates.

Training crawls take content and send nothing back. Cloudflare measures this with a crawl-to-referral ratio: how many pages a platform crawls for every visitor it sends to the site. In its crawl-to-click gap analysis, the ratios for some AI platforms ran into the tens of thousands, compared with single digits for traditional search.

If you ran a publication and saw those numbers, you'd block too. The catch is that blocking is usually done by user agent or IP range, so it hits user-action fetches as well. Those are the ones that happen when a person asks your agent to read a specific article, and Cloudflare's 2025 Year in Review found they grew more than 15x during the year. Your agent is caught in a fight it didn't start.

The mix of crawlers also shifts quickly, which makes static allow and block lists fragile on both sides:

And restrictions keep piling up. The Consent in Crisis study audited 14,000 web domains and found that a large and fast-growing share of the sources behind the C4 training corpus now restrict AI crawlers, either through robots.txt or their terms of service.

Source: Longpre et al., Consent in Crisis (NeurIPS 2024)

So that's the environment your agent fetches in. Here's how to build for it.

Before writing any retry logic, put names on your failures. "The request failed" is useless to both you and the model. In practice almost every failed fetch falls into one of four buckets:

Bucket 2 is the dangerous one, because a block page often comes back with a 200 OK. If you don't check, the model will happily summarise "Checking your browser before accessing..." as if it were the article.

Here's a compact fetch layer in Python using httpx and the standard library's robots.txt parser. It's around 100 lines and covers the four buckets above.

Use a descriptive user agent with a contact URL. It costs you nothing and site operators are far more likely to allow a client they can identify and reach. Then check robots.txt (standardised as RFC 9309) before every new host.

The lru_cache means you fetch robots.txt once per origin rather than once per page. In a long-running service you'll want a cache with a TTL instead, since robots.txt files do change.

This is the core of the layer. Instead of returning raw HTML, return a result that says which bucket you landed in.

The marker list is deliberately simple. It won't catch every interstitial, but it catches the common ones, and a false "blocked" is much cheaper than a confident summary of a CAPTCHA page. Log every blocked result with its first few hundred characters so you can grow the list from real data.

Retries should depend on the bucket. Throttled and broken responses get exponential backoff with jitter. Blocked and not-allowed responses get no retries at all.

Note the cap on wait. A site sending Retry-After: 3600 is telling you to go away for an hour. An interactive agent should report that to the user rather than block a request thread for that long.

The cheapest fetch is the one you don't make. Agents often re-read the same documentation page several times in one session. Store the ETag and Last-Modified headers and send them back as If-None-Match and If-Modified-Since. A 304 Not Modified response costs the server almost nothing and lowers the odds of being rate limited.

Wire these into fetch() by passing headers=conditional_headers(url) to client.get(). On a 304, return the cached text. On a 200, call remember().

Finally, don't hide failures from the LLM. Give the tool output a structured shape so the model can reason about it:

(extract_main_text is whatever readability or boilerplate-removal step you already use. Libraries like trafilatura work well here.)

With this in place, the model's answer changes from a made-up summary to "I couldn't read that page because the site blocks automated access. Here's what I found from two other sources." That's a better product, and it builds trust with users.

Once you've done everything above, some legitimate user-action fetches will still fail. That's usually because the site blocks data-center IP ranges outright, not because of anything your agent did. Teams handle this in a few ways, roughly in order of preference:

The principle is the same in every case: the network path is a reliability decision, not a way around a site's wishes. Your robots.txt check and your honest user agent should stay in place whichever route the request takes.

Once your fetch layer returns structured results, observability is easy. Emit one event per fetch with the host, status bucket and latency, and watch these:

If you're prototyping agents with AI coding assistants, ask them to generate these metrics hooks from the start. They're easy to bolt on now and painful to add later. For a sense of how well current tools handle this kind of infrastructure code, Vibe Coder Journal publishes hands-on reviews and benchmarks of AI coding tools.

The web your agent reads in 2026 isn't the open, fetch-anything web many of us learned on. More than half the traffic is automated, publishers have good reasons to be wary of AI crawlers, and the defaults now lean toward blocking. You can't change that. You can make your agent a good citizen, though: one that identifies itself, respects the rules, backs off when asked, and tells the truth when it can't read something.

Most of the reliability gains come from the unglamorous parts: classifying failures, caching and passing honest errors to the model. Get those right and your agent will feel much more dependable, even on the days when half the web won't let it in.

It depends on the jurisdiction, the site's terms of service and what you do with the content. Respecting robots.txt and site terms is the baseline. For commercial products, get proper legal advice.

Many site owners expect it to, and RFC 9309 makes no exception for user-triggered fetches. The safest default is to obey it and tell the user when a page is disallowed.

Many bot-protection systems serve a challenge page with a 200 status. Check the body for challenge markers before passing it to the model, as in the looks_blocked() function above.

Only for pages that render their main content with JavaScript. Try a plain HTTP fetch first. Headless browsers are slower, cost more to run and are easier to fingerprint.

#10424. Lawyer builds private AI server with eight used Nvidia V100 GPUs

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The builder estimates 50% to 60% of their AI work can now skip frontier models, running on two office circuits.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A lawyer posted on Reddit about their private AI server built from eight used 2018-era Nvidia Tesla V100 data center GPUs so case files never have to leave the office. With two 4-card SXM2 NVLink boards, four PEX switches, and SlimSAS cables everywhere — plus one burnt power connector — the builder now runs the open DeepSeek V4.1 Flash model with every expert in GPU memory, at up to 215 tokens per second. The four-GPU boards cost about $2,400 apiece when the builder bought them.

The eight Tesla V100-SXM2-32GB cards, each with 32GB of HBM2 at 900 GB/s, plug into SXM2, Nvidia’s mezzanine socket for data center GPUs, rather than PCIe slots, and are split across two baseboards. Inside each board, all four cards are NVLinked — NVLink is Nvidia’s direct GPU-to-GPU link — for 128GB per board, but the two boards only communicate over PCIe. Each board connects to the host through two PEX (PLX) switch cards over SlimSAS cables, four switches in all, with each switch handling two GPUs at PCIe 3.0 x8 speeds over its own x16 uplink.

The given prices are already in the past, though: used V100 SXM2 32GB cards are now listed on eBay for about $900, more than double the roughly $400 apiece from the post, and listings suggest prices are still climbing. The system also already has some other necessary components: a Gigabyte Threadripper Pro motherboard with a 12-core 3945WX CPU to match, 8x 32GB DDR4 RDIMM, one NUMA node, and two PSUs, 2,800 W in total, are on separate regular office circuits. This is a real-world case for used data center hardware as a cheap route to getting lots of fast VRAM together in one place.

DeepSeek V4.1 Flash is compressed to mostly 2 bits here, and its weights still total 248.8GB. The model’s “experts,” the parts that do most of the work for each word, live entirely in GPU memory, while about 110GB of lookup tables, called Engram tables, stay in the system’s regular RAM. Fetching from RAM costs about 0.12 milliseconds per step, which the builder says barely affects the speed numbers.

The first half of the model’s layers (0–19) runs on the first SXM2 board and the second half (20–39) on the second. Each board handles its heavy traffic over its own NVLink, so only one hand-off per step has to cross the slower PCIe link between the boards. In an earlier post, the builder found that splitting every layer across all eight cards made speeds “fall off a cliff” because of that PCIe hop.

Performance is surprisingly good for a model this size. Code comes out fastest, at up to 215 tokens per second, because a small draft model built into DeepSeek guesses five tokens ahead, and code is easy to guess. Legal writing, which is what the server is for, runs at up to 118 tokens per second.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Long documents take longer to get going: the model reads prompts at about 2,200 tokens per second, so at 128K tokens, the first token arrives after about a minute. It can accept up to 524K tokens of context. Three days before the post, the same model on four of these cards managed just 36–38 tokens per second. The jump came with the second board, which could presumably double performance under ideal circumstances, and the builder credits custom kernels and prefill work for the rest. See the table for the full breakdown.

Builder’s measurements, Nov. 5; greedy decoding, 1,024 tokens out, mean of two runs.

It wasn’t as simple as hooking everything up and turning on the power. The host reset itself twice with a fatal PCIe error on the link to one GPU board, both times with a higher power limit and uncapped GPU clocks. In this state, the cards drew 192–232 W, past the 180 W limit. The best guess, although “not proven,” is that a board stops responding during load spikes.

The resets stopped after the builder capped the clocks and removed one board whose power connector had a burn mark; “Check your connectors, people.” Each card is now capped at 150 W, half of its 300 W rating, with the graphics clock capped at 1522 MHz. A 200 W cap brought no appreciable performance gains, according to tests.

The system memory hit uncorrected ECC errors, too, which went away after the builder dropped the RAM from DDR4-3200 to DDR4-2666. One GPU was connected at x4 instead of x8 at last check, and the builder plans to reseat it; one commenter traced the same kind of problem on their own V100 to a bad cable.

The pain was worth the payoff as an estimated 50–60% of the lawyer’s existing LLM workflows can now run on the box instead of frontier models. Helping bridge the gap is a second DeepSeek server on two DGX Sparks, where the two systems hand work off to each other. The setup does appear to save money, at least when compared to API pricing, although the builder says it’s not really faster. However, it will protect client data “once it is self contained, which is the goal.”

The builder added that the V100 box might also get a 240 V circuit. Beyond power, the system is also on borrowed time with the Nvidia R580 driver branch, which is the last to support Volta. For now, the lawyer admits that it’s mostly a hobby, but in the future it may be a business idea. The next step is to handle two or three requests at once, something built and checked on a test system but not yet on the server.

#10422. I bought the knock-off Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air bands, and it’s crazy how good they are [Gallery]

I love swapping out the bands on my Pixel Watch 5 and Fitbit Air, but Google’s official options are not cheap. It doesn’t take long to find a plethora of knock-off Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air bands around the web and, as it turns out, they’re actually really good.

Over the past few generations, I’ve collected a ton of Pixel Watch bands and, at first, that was almost entirely first-party bands. For the first couple of years of Pixel Watch, third-party bands were either non-existent or of terrible quality, but they’ve gotten a lot better.

Last year, I showcased some knock-off Active Sport bands that, for $8, very effectively filled the gap for me while Google’s lineup was lacking some colors I wanted.

Since then, I’ve tried others. The most recent batch is a 6-pack of Pixel Watch bands that are copies of Google’s standard “Active” band. They’re made from silicone, not the fluorelastomer from past generations, nor the “Hydrogenated Nitrile Butadiene Rubber (HNBR)” used on Pixel Watch 5. But, in action, it gets the job done.

The bands sit somewhere between Google’s small and large sizes and slot into the Pixel Watch 5’s mechanism perfectly. If you handed this to me and said it was an official band from Google, I’d probably believe you.

To get this level of quality at such a low price is wild, all while getting colors that, again, Google doesn’t make. I found this while just wanting a purple band, but the price tag pushed me to just buy a whole pack, and the striking deep red included sneakily became one of my favorites.

Pixel Watch isn’t alone here – third-party knock-off bands for Fitbit Air are very good, as my colleage Damien Wilde has pointed out repeatedly.

I originally bought the Stephen Curry edition Fitbit Air and, long story short, I don’t have that official band anymore. But the $9 (currently even cheaper!) knock-off I bought on Amazon is quite literally identical in quality, feel, and even the details. The only thing it’s missing is the Stephen Curry badge (which, frankly, I don’t miss anyway), but it still has that little piece of added material where the wordmark would have been.

The same goes for the matching band I picked up for my Pixel Watch 5.

Google doesn’t sell this band on its own, which is annoying, but the knock-off I bought is basically identical to what I felt during our hands-on with the official one back in August, though this one doesn’t have the space for the Stephen Curry badge the real one does. The lugs connecting to the watch are also different and not nearly as stylish, but I’m absolutely not complaining here.

Is it possible that Google’s supply chain is producing these near-identical versions? Given the quality at play, I really wouldn’t be surprised! That said, a lot of these are simple copies. It’s not all that hard to spin up generic versions of products like these.

Most third party cheap Fitbit Air bands I've tried range from "decent" to "very good".

Most third party cheap and expensive Pixel Watch bands I've tried range from "very, very bad" to "almost decent".

The major issue with PW 3rd party bands isn't the band itself. It's the connecting lugs.

Not how they look, but how they're manufactured. High tolerances, rattling, too lose or too tight connections.

And that applies also to certified "made for Google" bands (i.e. the Spigen ones).

I've given up. I'm going to buy the official Pixel Watch leather band and take the official lugs out of it, so I can use with any 22mm strap.

I’ll still happily pick up some of Google’s official bands because it’s hard to beat some of those color options but, for the most part, it’s getting hard to go wrong with the knock-off Pixel Watch bands, and even more so with the Fitbit Air. The simpler tracker gets even better third-party options seeing as Google made the mounting mechanism free for everyone.

Whatever the case, third-party / knock-off Pixel Watch and Fitbit Air bands are in a really good place right now, and that’s refreshing after a few years of using this lineup every day.

#10419. Memory shortage spurs smartphone makers to melt used memory off old devices, then re-solder it onto new products

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The smartphone industry is expected to see a spike in reballed memory adoption. According to a report by global market intelligence and research firm TrendForce, the current penetration rate is estimated at around 3–4%, with reballed memory primarily used in entry-level and budget smartphone models. However, this figure is expected to rise to 7–9% in 2027.

Reballed memory is recycled memory salvaged from older or discarded devices. The term reball comes from the process itself, in which the solder balls on a used memory chip are first melted so the chip can be removed from the old device, then re-soldered to a new device, thus allowing the component to be reused rather than discarded. You can see the process in action in the video below.

This offers manufacturers a cheaper alternative to sourcing new memory chips, potentially helping them keep production costs under control. Currently, reballed 4GB LPDDR4X memory modules make up the majority of such components on the market, and they are used in 4G smartphones priced below CNY 1,299 (approximately $200). This tactic is becoming increasingly important for smartphone makers, as customers are particularly sensitive to price increases in the budget segment. This leaves limited room to raise retail prices without affecting demand. Using reballed memory can help offset rising bill of materials (BOM) costs while allowing brands to keep entry-level devices affordable.

TrendForce expects DRAM prices to rise even further in the first quarter of 2027, which may put additional pressure on smartphone manufacturers. As a result, the use of recycled memory and other lower-specification components could gradually expand beyond the entry-level segment and into more premium smartphones. What is currently a cost-saving measure for budget devices could eventually become a broader strategy for manufacturers looking to manage expenses amid a prolonged period of high memory prices.The rising cost of memory is also pushing PC enthusiasts and hardware hobbyists to explore unconventional ways to reuse existing DRAM chips. Recently, an electronics hobbyist converted a relatively inexpensive $120 DDR5 SO-DIMM kit into a desktop memory kit valued at around $1,000. The project involved salvaging an SK hynix 2x 16GB DDR5-5600 SODIMM kit purchased from Facebook Marketplace and reballing the chips onto custom desktop DIMM PCBs, with the resulting modules reportedly capable of reaching DDR5-7800 speeds.

Converting laptop memory into desktop DIMMs is technically demanding and requires considerable expertise and specialized equipment. Similarly, reballing memory for use in smartphones is far from straightforward, particularly given the tiny solder connections in modern BGA (Ball Grid Array) packages. Nevertheless, both developments highlight how manufacturers, repair specialists, and hardware enthusiasts are exploring alternatives to reuse existing memory to offset rising costs and limited availability.

#10416. Attacker Relentlessly Scans for Credentials by Impersonating AI Bots

The threat actor lurked behind bots posing as agents from OpenAI, Anthropic, Meta, and others to find exposed data.

A threat actor lurked behind more than 30 different AI bots to find and steal exposed credentials, continuously scanning for sensitive data without success. The findings highlight a growing problem for organizations: distinguishing between which AI agents belong in their networks and which may be malicious.

A scanner impersonating 32 bots — including crawlers and assistants from OpenAI, Anthropic, Perplexity, Meta, and others — hunted for exposed credentials to various organizations, according to a report published today by CQ Prime Research, part of Cequence Security. The scanner requested files from affected organizations — which spanned retail, financial services, telecommunications, energy, and software — that hold passwords, API keys, and cloud credentials.

The scanner has been running since late September and was still active as of late October, according to the researchers. They identified the activity on Oct. 21, discovering that on a single day this month, 48% of Cequence-protected organizations received the malicious AI bot traffic.

Related:AI Gave Your Incident Archive a Second Job

The findings highlight a growing challenge for security teams at a pivotal time, as businesses must figure out which AI agents to trust and permit access to their services.

"Attackers can test every one of those allow and deny decisions, and Cequence is seeing them probe which agents a business trusts, then impersonate the agents they expect to get through," Will Glazier, director of CQ Prime threat research and data science, wrote in the report.

The scanner operates by firing off a burst of 50 to 190 requests in about 8 seconds and then moving on, working through targets one at a time, around the clock. Fortunately for the organizations affected, nearly all of the requests were blocked or failed, according to the report.

"It sends random nonsense paths first to find websites that answer every request, a common scanning technique," Glazier wrote.

CQ Prime Research identified the traffic as malicious through some telling behaviors that undermined the scanner's attempt to blend in with other traffic. The scanner rotates through about 32 bot identities evenly, which real bots do not do, according to the report; one machine claimed to be an agent from OpenAI, Anthropic, Meta, and Perplexity, all within seconds.

The attacker also used the contact address of Anthropic's crawler for all of its fake Claude identities, even when posing as different Claude agents, which shows the text was copied and not unique to each agent, according to CQ Prime Research.

Related:What We Missed: Knicks Phishing Test Goes Out of Bounds

The scanner also makes requests for configuration files, cloud credential files, and private SSH keys, from a list of about 90 paths, while never identifying itself or providing a contact address. Legitimate Internet-scanning services would never need these files and always identify themselves.

"The requests gave it away," observes Collin Hogue-Spears, senior director of solutions management at security firm Black Duck, which means "what Cequence documented is the test, not a breach."

However, what happens when AI agents scanning a network get through? This is what organizations are facing, particularly banks and financial institutions, with growing concern among experts that rogue agents are already logging into customer accounts at these organizations without them knowing. The result of such activity could cause global economic fallout.

For now, CQ Prime Research found that at financial services firms, AI agents acting for customers represented fewer than 1 in 4,000 requests. "The agents banks worry about most log in with a customer's own credentials through an ordinary browser and don't announce themselves," Glazier wrote in the report.

At this point, financial institutions should be starting to take action to identify which AI agents are legit and which aren't. But acting now already could mean they're playing catch-up to the activity of threat actors, observes Shane Barney, chief information security officer at Keeper Security.

Related:9 Cyber Awareness Tips That Actually Match Today's Maze of Threats

"Banks are currently scrambling to figure out which AI bots belong on their networks, but cybercriminals have already moved on to the next phase," he says. "They are no longer trying to break into these systems. Instead, they are simply logging in disguised as trusted AI agents."

To mitigate the growing threat from AI bots and agents, security leaders must treat AI agents, API keys, and service accounts with the same operational rigor as human administrators, Barney suggests. "That requires eliminating implicit trust across every access path," he says.

Indeed, organizations should not trust a bot's claimed identity on its own, according to CQ Prime Research, and they should verify agents through signed requests, published address ranges, or direct integrations.

They also should monitor for behavior that does not match the claimed identity, "such as an AI assistant requesting configuration or credential files," Glazier wrote.

Organizations also should stop leaving static credentials in vulnerable files where scraping bots can easily find them to eliminate obvious security gaps. Instead, Barney says, "Security teams must move away from long-lived local credentials in favor of centralized, zero-knowledge secrets vaults."

#10414. How to Use CSS Attribute Selectors to Build More Flexible User Interfaces

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When developing a website, developers often rely on CSS classes to style buttons, forms, navigation elements, and other interface components. This approach works well, but some situations require styles to depend on an element's HTML attributes rather than additional class names.

CSS attribute selectors provide a practical solution. They allow developers to target elements based on whether an attribute exists, what value it contains, or whether its value matches a particular pattern. Used carefully, they can simplify styling and make reusable components easier to maintain.

An attribute selector targets an HTML element using square brackets. For example, a developer can select every input field that has a required attribute:

This rule applies to inputs with the required attribute, regardless of their input type.

Developers can also target a specific attribute value:

This is useful when different input types need different visual treatments without introducing additional classes into the HTML.

Forms are a practical place to apply attribute selectors because HTML already provides attributes that describe how individual fields behave.

Consider a registration form containing email, password, and telephone fields. Each input can be styled according to its purpose:

The CSS can target these fields independently:

This approach keeps the markup descriptive while allowing the stylesheet to respond to the attributes already present in the document.

One important distinction is that required indicates a validation requirement, while :invalid represents an input's current validity state. These should not be treated as interchangeable.

CSS provides several matching operators that help developers target more specific attribute values.

These operators can be useful when working with links, downloadable files, language attributes, and reusable components.

For example, a website might want to distinguish PDF downloads from ordinary navigation links:

This adds a small visual indicator to links whose URLs end in .pdf.

Attribute selectors can also help identify links with different destinations or behaviors.

For example, external links that use HTTPS can receive a subtle visual indicator:

However, matching an HTTPS URL does not necessarily prove that a link points to an external website. Internal links can also use HTTPS, so developers should consider the actual navigation requirements before applying special styling.

For more examples of selector combinations and matching rules, SitePoint's guide to CSS Attribute Selectors provides a useful reference.

Although attribute selectors are flexible, they should not replace every class-based styling decision.

A selector such as [class*="button"] can match unintended elements if their class names happen to contain the same text. Similarly, broad attribute rules may affect components that were not originally intended to receive those styles.

For predictable results, use exact attribute matching whenever possible and prefer descriptive classes for major component styling.

Developers should also remember that CSS selectors only control presentation. They do not replace JavaScript validation, server-side security checks, or proper HTML semantics.

The MDN documentation on attribute selectors provides further details about selector syntax, matching behavior, and supported operators.

Before adding attribute selectors to a large stylesheet, inspect the affected elements using browser developer tools.

Check whether the selector matches the intended elements.

Test fields with different attribute values.

Verify disabled, required, and invalid form states.

Check navigation links and downloadable files.

Test the layout on mobile and desktop screens.

Review whether existing component styles are being unintentionally overridden.

This process helps identify unexpected matches before they affect the production interface.

CSS attribute selectors give developers another way to create flexible and maintainable interfaces without adding unnecessary classes to every HTML element.

They are particularly useful for form styling, link identification, and components that already contain meaningful HTML attributes. By choosing precise selectors, understanding matching operators, and testing changes before deployment, developers can use this feature to write cleaner CSS while keeping their markup descriptive and organized.

Anthropic takes over Caterpillar generator orders as AI companies increasingly secure their own electricity supplies

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Anthropic, the AI company behind Claude, has agreed to spend $3.4 billion securing 1.7 gigawatts (GW) of gas-fired generators for its AI data centres, taking over equipment orders originally placed with Caterpillar by Texas-based supplier OnePWR Solutions. According to Reuters, the agreement was reached in late September, and also covers the engineering and construction work needed to install the equipment. Anthropic is not buying OnePWR itself. Instead, it is taking over the supplier's existing generator commitments, which were placed roughly two years ago but required additional financing. By taking over the existing orders, Anthropic avoids having to start from scratch and wait for Caterpillar to find another larger order into its production schedule. That timing matters too, as larger orders for large gas turbines and generators are piling up, and manufacturers are struggling to keep up with demand, especially from AI companies.

Notably, global turbine orders have reportedly exceeded 100GW, while manufacturing capacity stands at an estimated 60GW to 70GW. Average delivery times in the U.S. have reached six years, with some prospective orders extending into 2030 or 2031.

The latest purchase puts Anthropic increasingly in familiar territory. Elon Musk’s AI has already turned to gas-powered generation to bring its large computing facilities online without waiting for conventional grid connections. Recently, xAI installed eight converted General Electric jet turbines at a data center in Mississippi. Each unit can generate up to 50MW, giving the installation a potential combined output of 400MW. The deployment has also attracted environmental scrutiny, with the Southern Environmental Law Center challenging the site's emissions permits.

The environmental implications of relying on gas-fired generators have also attracted criticism. “This is pretty grim—these kinds of generators are terrible for the environment.” Shakeel Hashim, founder of ReadTransformer, tweeted, commenting on Anthropic's purchase. His reaction points to an issue Anthropic will have to contend with as it expands its power infrastructure: securing electricity quickly does not remove the environmental costs of generating it.

Musk's companies have been moving further into the equipment business, too. In August, it emerged that the company was bringing turbine-blade and vine manufacturing in-house, with Musk identifying components production as a major constraint on generator deliveries, with some turbine blades reportedly taking between 60 and 90 weeks to be manufactured. Earlier, in July, SpaceX also acquired APR Energy, a company with a fleet of trailer -mounted gas and diesel generators capable of producing more than 1GW.

The approach is not exclusive to Musk's businesses. OpenAI has followed a similar route for its first Stargate data center in Abilene, Texas. In July, we reported that Crusoe, the company developing the facility, had acquired 29GE Vernova LM2500XPRESS gas turbines, each rated at 34MW. Together, the 29 turbines could generate up to 986 MW. Anthropic is taking a different path, though, by stepping into OnePWR’s existing Caterpillar orders, securing generators that were already in use, rather than buying new equipment.

That said, the power shortage is forcing AI companies to look beyond new generator orders. There has been an upsurge in demand for used and refurbished turbines, including retired jet engines converted to generate electricity. But even those alternatives have limitations, including maintenance requirements, remaining service life and suitability for continuous operations. For Anthropic, however, taking over OnePWR’s Caterpillar orders could provide a way around some of those hurdles. The agreement also provides installation capabilities, meaning the company is securing more than the generators themselves.

#10409. Two more Garmin smartwatches are down to record-low prices

The Garmin Fenix E and Garmin Venu 3S are for two different types of people, but both are available at a surprisingly low price thanks to these limited-time Garmin deals.

Garmin watches are often expensive, which is why I always recommend them on sale. For example, the Garmin Fenix E, usually $799, is now down to $399. That’s the all-time low price for this model and probably the best Garmin deal I’ve seen so far this fall.

The Garmin Fenix E is essentially a Garmin Epix Gen 2 with a newer OS. It sports multi-GNSS navigation plus a 3-axis compass, gyroscope, and barometric altimeter that should cover basically all your tracking needs. It’s got pulse ox monitoring, respiration tracking, advanced sleep monitoring, real-time stamina tracking, and a training readiness score (so you know what days you should be taking it easy).

Despite all the features, this one will last you two weeks per charge and can utilize GPS for up to 42 hours on each charge. The stainless steel bezel looks great, and the 47mm fiber-reinforced polymer case is built to last. This is one of those watches that, quite frankly, makes no sense at full cost. At half the price, though? Yeah, that looks much better.

If you’re looking for something a little more elegant-looking, the Garmin Venu 3S is the right choice. At $299, this Garmin deal makes it $250 less than the Garmin Venu 4. You might think buying the newest one is automatically better, but that’s not necessarily the case between these two models.

For example, the Venu 4 is thicker and heavier because it uses an all-metal casing, has one less button on the side, lacks the permanent hour markers that are on the Venu 3S’s bezel, and has worse battery life by 1-2 days, depending on your usage. The Venu 4 has more features and a newer OS, but I personally like the OS design on the 3S better (and I’m apparently not alone on that front).

The Venu 4 also has new sleep guidance and coaching features, training load and several tools that used to be for Garmin Forerunner watches, plus lifestyle logging, but I’m not sure I’d want to pay $250 to get those. Either way, you’ve got a great option now that the Venu 3S is at such a fantastic price.

#10408. Nvidia RTX PRO 6000 user’s cables almost melted, even when limited to 500W

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

12V-2x6 strikes again, but thankfully it didn't take out a GPU this time.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Reddit user u/Doomslayer606 posted about frayed braided sleeving on both 12V-2x6 cables for their two RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Workstation GPUs the other day, asking whether it’s normal cable degradation or a sign of overheating. The GPUs were running at 500W, below the maximum 600W power draw, but commenters are confident it’s overheated cables, as the poster reports stiff, cracked, and easily peeling sleeves on only some of the wires.

u/Doomslayer606 posted on r/PcBuild with the title “Did I just save my RTX PRO 6000 GPUs from melting?” with photos of the 12V-2x6 cable’s braided sleeving frayed. The poster asked in the description whether this was normal degradation, a manufacturing issue, or overheating. The poster reported that the damaged braided sleeving felt stiff and peeled off easily, which is a strong indication of heat damage, and the comments were sure that it was.

Luckily, the poster’s GPUs were safe, but the 12-2x6 connectors are notorious for melting due to high power draw. One comment asked if they kept the stock power limit, to which the poster responded that they lowered both GPUs to 500W — another user responded asking why the poster wouldn’t use 300W, but the poster said it would reduce performance by up to 50% and they need to keep up with their deadlines.

Theoretically, the cable should be able to handle 500W, as each positive pin would only handle 6.94 A, which isn’t enough current to significantly heat up 16 AWG wire, but one of the main causes of cable overheating is uneven current distribution across the pins. Since only two of the pins melted on both cables, it’s likely those received high-current spikes often, leading to heat buildup.

Users have suggested aftermarket cables like the Corsair ThermalProtect 12V-2x6 cable that detects overheating cables, and the Thermal Grizzly WireView Pro II or Aqua Computing AMPINEL, which have active current balancing to prevent any wire from taking too much current alone. In a recent story, someone even created a power distributor, distributing the 12 pins into two 8-pin cables with a custom-designed PCB, which lowered the cable temperatures significantly — while another modder had a cursed solution, soldering thick cables directly to the RTX 5090’s power pins.

In an ideal world, consumers shouldn’t need another product or mod their GPUs to stop them destroying themselves. GPUs have only gotten more powerful, but instead of using more or thicker wires, we’re getting fewer wires of the same thickness. Understandably, having multiple 8-pin connectors gets messy, but surely there has to be a better solution. Maybe GPUs have outgrown the Mini-Fit Jr. connectors and need to have their own special connector with thicker pins and thicker wires.

#10406. Discord is about to get way, way faster on your Android phone

A new Discord client built in Rust is looking pretty shiny and clean, even on low-end Android phones.

I love Discord, but if there’s one thing the app isn’t known for is it’s speed. That’s particularly true on phones that don’t have much RAM, but an upcoming new Discord client is changing the game for all users, regardless of the platform.

Discord CTO and co-founder Stanislav Vishnevskiy posted on X about several new Discord updates that are getting users excited. In the video, embedded below, you can see Discord running on two very old, lower-end phones (looks like a Samsung A-series to me). The current client, on the left, takes a full 3 seconds to launch, and then another 2 seconds to fully load. The new client, on the right, launches in under 1 second and is finished loading everything before the 2-second mark.

A release date has not been announced for the new client, although it is presumably launching soon.

We didn't forget about mobile, especially Android. Inspired by React Native, it drives the OS-native view layer through the same Rust codebase as the new desktop app. I think it might be the most responsive app on my phone now? https://t.co/uf5EwUtQLh pic.twitter.com/F8QCedZcJj

#10404. How to Get a YouTube API Key and Use the Data API (2026)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You need a YouTube API key the moment your app has to read anything from YouTube: a video's length, its view count, a channel's latest uploads. Getting one takes about five minutes. Using it well takes a little longer, because YouTube changed how its quota works in June 2026, and plenty of guides still describe the old rules.

In this guide we'll get a key, lock it down and test it. Then we'll use it from Node.js for three real jobs: adding up the length of a playlist, listing a channel's videos without spending a search, and estimating what a video earned. Along the way we'll cover what the YouTube API costs (nothing), how the 2026 quota works, and the errors you're most likely to hit.

A YouTube API key identifies your project to the YouTube Data API v3. With just a key, you can read public data:

A key isn't enough for anything that touches someone's account. Uploading videos, editing playlists or reading private data needs OAuth 2.0, where the user signs in and grants your app access.

You'll need a Google account. The YouTube Data API is free, so there's nothing to pay for.

The restrictions matter more than they look. A key is just a string in a URL, and anyone who copies an unrestricted one can spend your daily quota for you. With an API restriction, the key is useless for other Google APIs. With an application restriction, it only works from your server or your site. Changes to restrictions can take up to five minutes to apply, so don't panic if a request fails straight after you save.

Before writing any code, check the key with a single request. This one asks for the first video ever uploaded to YouTube, and uses the fields parameter to trim the response down to four values:

That request cost 1 unit of your daily quota, which brings us to the question everyone asks.

Yes. There's no charge per request and no paid plan. Instead you get a daily quota, and since June 1, 2026 it's split into separate buckets:

Google's quota calculator lists the cost of every method. Quotas reset at midnight Pacific Time. Every request costs at least 1 unit, including requests that fail, so a retry loop around a broken call can use up a day's quota on its own.

If you've read older guides, search is the big change. A search used to cost 100 units out of the same 10,000, so 100 searches emptied your whole day. Uploads were worse still, at about 1,600 units each until December 2025. Now both have their own buckets, and the 10,000 units are left for everything else.

To give you a feel for what 10,000 units buys, here's what each example in this guide costs:

You can't pay for more. To go above the defaults, fill in the YouTube API Services Audit and Quota Extension Form, which is linked from Google's page on quota and compliance audits. YouTube first audits your project to check it follows the YouTube API Services Terms of Service, then someone from the API team gets back to you. Google doesn't give a timeline, so apply well before you need the extra quota.

Most projects never need to. The rest of this guide is about getting a lot done with very few units.

The examples need Node.js 20.6 or later, for the built-in fetch and the --env-file flag. There are no packages to install. Make a folder with a .env file in it:

Every example imports one small helper. It builds the URL, adds your key and turns API errors into readable messages:

Run any example with node --env-file=.env followed by the file name.

YouTube doesn't tell you how long a playlist is. To work it out, you need two methods:

Durations come back in ISO 8601 format. PT15M33S is 15 minutes 33 seconds, PT1H2M3S adds an hour, and a stream longer than a day looks like P1DT2H3M4S. The toSeconds() function below handles all three.

Here it is on freeCodeCamp's Full Courses in One Video playlist:

That's 184 hours of courses for 4 units of quota. Private and deleted videos still show up in a playlist's items, but videos.list doesn't return them, which is why the script prints "52 of 52 videos available". On a playlist with removed videos, the two numbers will differ.

This is the core of the free YouTube playlist length calculator I built on createthat.ai. The live version adds a target time box that works out the speed you need to finish by a deadline, and it accepts channel, video and Shorts links as well as playlists.

The obvious way to find a channel's videos is to search for them. Don't: search is now capped at 100 calls a day, and it isn't built to list everything a channel has posted. Every channel has an uploads playlist instead, and you can reach it in two cheap calls:

Two units, and none of your 100 daily searches. To get every upload rather than the newest five, page through the playlist with the same pageToken loop as Example 1.

videos.list with part=statistics returns a video's view, like and comment counts. Dislike counts have been private since December 2021 and favoriteCount is always 0, so don't build anything on those. The script also copes with a missing like or comment count, which you'll see on some videos.

This time the fields parameter trims the response to the values we use. That makes the response smaller, but it doesn't change the cost: the call is 1 unit either way.

Here it is on freeCodeCamp's Learn Python full course, one of the most-watched videos in that playlist:

Treat the last line as a rough range, not an answer. The Data API doesn't share anyone's revenue, so any estimate is views multiplied by an RPM: what a creator keeps per 1,000 views. RPM moves a lot with the topic and where the viewers live, and the estimate assumes the video shows ads, which not every channel does.

And sometimes you don't need an API at all. When I built a font tester, I needed a list of Google Fonts. The Google Fonts Developer API would have meant a key and a call on every visit, for a list that barely changes. Instead I generated a file of the 500 most popular fonts once, from Google's public font metadata, and the page loads it like any other file. No key, no quota and nothing to fail.

When a call fails, the response's error.message and error.details[0].reason tell you why. These are the ones you'll see most:

For the invalid key error, the full response looks like this:

Yes. Creating a key and calling the YouTube Data API costs nothing. You're limited by the daily quota instead, and there's no paid tier: more quota comes through an audit.

In the Google Cloud console, select your project and go to APIs & Services > Credentials. Your keys are listed under API keys, with a Show key option next to each one.

It works, but anyone can read the key from your page. If you do it, restrict the key to your website and to the YouTube Data API v3. A small server route that calls the API for you is safer, and it gives you somewhere to cache results.

By default, 100 searches, 100 uploads and 10,000 units for everything else. Most reads cost 1 unit and videos.list takes 50 IDs per call, so that's enough for the details of up to 500,000 videos a day.

Getting a YouTube API key is the easy part: one project, one API to enable, one key and two restrictions. The 2026 quota is generous if you work with it:

All three examples together cost 7 units, out of 10,000. From here, try commentThreads.list to pull a video's comments 100 at a time, or extend Example 2 to page through a channel's entire upload history and add it all up with the code from Example 1.

#10403. Acer Predator Helios Neo 16S AI Review: Slim OLED gaming that doesn't last long

The Acer Predator Helios Neo 16S AI mostly succeeds as a thin-and-light gaming laptop, offering solid RTX 5070 Ti performance, a beautiful 240 Hz OLED display, comfortable input devices, and plentiful ports. Its biggest weakness is ironically its battery life, which falls short of what you'd expect from a system focused on portability.

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

Not everyone wants a hulking gaming laptop. Some prefer something stealthier. Acer’s Predator Helios Neo 16S AI aims for portability but still packs a Core Ultra 9 290HX Plus processor and Nvidia RTX 5070 Ti graphics into a sub-five-pound package. It also includes plenty of ports and a beautiful 240 Hz OLED display. That hardware gives it plenty to work with, but the question remains whether the Helios Neo 16S AI can still deliver solid gaming performance while being portable.

Acer’s obsidian black chassis maintains the look of the Predator series – angular, dark, and visible in the dark with its illuminated lid logo. At 14.05 x 10.85 x 0.74 inches and 4.85 pounds, it’s respectably trim for a gaming laptop, though the Gigabyte Aero X16 (14 x 9.9 x 0.78 inches, 4.2 pounds) and the HyperX Omen 15 (13.5 x 9.96 x 0.93 inches, 4.89 pounds) both have about an inch less depth since neither use a rear thermal shelf like Acer.

The build quality is decent – both the chassis and lid show little flex. The stamped aluminum panels on the lid bottom and chassis lid aren’t ultra-premium but still beat plastic. One downside is that the metal shows fingerprints easily, which are difficult to clean off.

Acer provides an impressive port variety across three sides: Thunderbolt 4, three USB-A 3.2, USB-C 3.2, HDMI 2.1, 2.5 Gbps Ethernet, a MicroSD card reader, and an audio jack. The rear ports conveniently leave cables out of sight.

Inside, the laptop offers Wi-Fi 6E, which isn’t as modern as it could be, though the Killer AX1675i card and accompanying Killer Ethernet jack offer simultaneous network connections via Killer Doubleshot Pro for gamers that want to ensure network priority for the games over one connection and all other apps over the other. It’s also notable that Acer includes a zippered, padded travel sleeve.

Nvidia GeForce RTX 5070 Ti (12GB GDDR7, 1,612 MHz boost clock, 115 W maximum graphics power)

16-inch, OLED, 16:10, 2560 x 1600, 240 Hz, G-Sync

Thunderbolt 4, 3x USB-A 3.2, USB-C 3.2, HDMI 2.1, Ethernet, MicroSD card reader, audio

14.05 x 10.85 x 0.74 inches (357 x 276 x 18.8 mm)

We tested the Predator Helios Neo 16S AI with a Core Ultra 9 290HX Plus processor, Nvidia RTX 5070 Ti graphics card, and 16GB of RAM.

Playing 007 First Light, I saw about 65 to 75 frames per second (FPS) in most scenes, with spikes to over 100 FPS in wide open areas. The game remained all-around playable, though it did require using DLSS to achieve these framerates at native 2560 x 1600 with High settings.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Our comparison systems for this Acer are Gigabyte’s Aero X16 (Ryzen AI 7 350, RTX 5070 85W, $1,899.99 when tested, $1,499.99 as of this writing) and HyperX’s Omen 15 (Core Ultra 9 386H, RTX 5070 85W, $2,099.99 when tested, about $3,159.99 as of this writing).

Comparisons will focus on the 1920 x 1080 results, but we also included numbers at each laptop’s native resolution: 2560 x 1600 for the Acer and Gigabyte while the HP uses 2880 x 1800.

Overall, the Predator Helios Neo 16S AI performed significantly better than both the Gigabyte and HyperX laptops. Much of that has to do with its higher-wattage RTX 5070 Ti, though its Core Ultra 9 290HX Plus processor is also more modern.

In Shadow of the Tomb Raider (Highest detail preset), Acer’s 125 FPS at 1080p was on par with the HyperX (126 FPS), though it dominated at its 1600p native resolution, with 76 FPS against Gigabyte’s 60 FPS and HyperX’s 64 FPS.

Acer’s advantage continued in Far Cry 6 (Ultra), where it produced 122 FPS to Gigabyte’s 85 FPS and HyperX’s 107 FPS at native resolution. The lead narrowed in Red Dead Redemption 2 (Medium) where Acer and Gigabyte practically tied at 1080p and were within a few FPS at native (52 FPS for Acer and 49 FPS for Gigabyte). The HyperX remained behind.

The Acer also demonstrated markedly better performance in Cyberpunk 2077 (Ray Tracing Ultra) particularly at native resolution, where its 21 FPS outran Gigabyte (17 FPS) and HyperX (5 FPS).

In Forza Horizon 6 (Extreme), Acer’s 72 FPS at 1080p was strangely lower than HyperX’s 80 FPS, but the results flipped at native, where Acer’s 60 FPS was considerably ahead of HyperX’s 51 FPS.

While not a powerhouse, the Predator Helios Neo 16S AI demonstrates competent gaming performance at its 2560 x 1600 native resolution. Crucially, the extra depth from Acer’s thermal shelf design lets its GPU draw 115 W, considerably more than its Gigabyte and HyperX competitors

We test the sustained performance of gaming laptops running 15 loops of the Metro: Exodus stress test, simulating half an hour of gaming. During the test, the Predator Helios Neo 16S AI averaged 86.82 FPS across all runs. The variance was less than one FPS between runs, indicating stable thermal performance. The Core Ultra 9 290HX Plus ran at an average of 2.94 GHz on its P-cores, 2.16 GHz on its E-cores, and had an average temperature of 73 degrees Celsius. Meanwhile, the RTX 5070 Ti averaged 1.622 GHz and 74 C.

Our Predator Helios Neo 16S AI features a Core Ultra 9 290HX processor, 16GB of dual-channel DDR5-6400 memory, and a 1TB PCIe 4.0 SSD.

The Acer’s Core Ultra 9 290HX produced strong numbers in Geekbench. Its single-core score of 3,349 and multi-core score of 19,313 were well ahead of the Ryzen AI 7 350-powered Gigabyte (2,916 and 14,074) and Core Ultra 9 386H-powered HyperX (2,857 and 15,916). This continued into our 4K-to-1080p Handbrake transcoding testing, which Acer dominated with 2 minutes and 30 seconds. The Gigabyte (3:54) and HyperX (3:39) weren’t even close.

Acer also produced solid numbers in our 25GB file transfer test, averaging 2,034.84 MBps next to Gigabyte’s1,480.57 MBps and HyperX’s far slower 999.57 MBps.

Overall, this Acer is very capable for almost any kind of use. Multitasking will be limited by 16GB of RAM, but Acer sells versions of this laptop with 32GB and it can be upgraded after purchase.

Once glance at this Acer’s display leaves no doubt it’s a key selling point. Its OLED panel technology creates exceptionally vivid colors matched by excellent brightness, and its glossy surface gives it an almost liquid appearance. The 2560 x 1600 resolution is a good balance between detail and what the system’s RTX 5070 Ti graphics card can drive in today’s games. Gamers will also appreciate the smooth 240 Hz refresh rate and Nvidia G-Sync support. (As we’ll see in the battery life section, the screen only supports 240 Hz, resulting in higher-than-expected power consumption.)

Watching nature documentaries on National Geographic, I noted the vibrant colors of tropical scenes and the pitch blacks of the depths of the sea. Gaming was equally immersive – 007 First Light felt supremely fluid and looked sharp. I could even make out the writing on character’s uniform badges without zooming.

The Acer’s display covered a healthy 82.7% of the DCI-P3 color gamut, matching the Gigabyte, although it wasn’t quite in the same league as the HyperX, which also used an OLED panel. It also didn’t match the HyperX in brightness, but with a peak of 372.4 nits in SDR, the Acer boasts solid brightness for OLED. Our 40% HDR testing revealed up to an impressive 608 nits of brightness.

Acer’s keyboard offers a satisfying feel, with ample throw and cushioning without feeling rubbery. In MonkeyType, I reached 116 words per minute with 99% accuracy on my first try, a great result for me on any laptop. The Acer Key app lets you customize the user-customizable key above the plus key, which is handy for creating one-press access to a favorite app or site.

Though the keyboard doesn't feature per-key backlighting, its four RGB zones can be set to many patterns and colors in the PredatorSense app. When changing lighting settings, the app initially says they’re controlled by Windows Dynamic Lighting, but that isn’t the case since Dynamic Lighting says no compatible devices are detected. But once dismissing the warning in PredatorSense, the app provides intuitive if limited control over the zones and lid logo. Each zone can be assigned a static lighting effect or the entire keyboard set in a dynamic pattern: breathing, neon, wave, ripple, combo, snake, and disco, with customizable speed, direction, and brightness. Settings can be stored in profiles.

Acer also did well with the large touchpad, which offers predictable clicking and my fingers glided smoothly over the matte surface.

Audio performance isn’t a strong point for the Neo 16S AI. Twin speakers embedded in the palm rest barely have enough volume for casual listening, with average clarity and thin bass. In Phil Collins’ “Don’t Lose My Number”, drum hits sounded flat and vocals were somewhat muted.

The speakers are better-suited gaming, offering a decent sense of direction because of their placement on either side of the palm rest, but lack of bass and volume means you'll want a headset. The DTS:X app offers equalizers – without them, the sound is quite tinny. I had the best results leaving the equalizer on Auto.

With ten Torx T6 screws securing the bottom cover, Acer doesn’t make it easy to get inside the Predator Helios Neo 16S AI. Removing the screws is the easy part – finding an entry point for my plastic pry tool provided harder. I eventually found one at the front corners of the chassis by using mild force. The fact that the side vents are part of the bottom cover compounds the difficulty – I needed to find an opening for my pry tool above those as well. The clips made cringeworthy snapping sounds as they were released, though surprisingly, none cracked off.

The effort is not for naught – Acer offers solid upgradeability, with two SODIMM slots under the black plastic cover in the middle, plus an M.2 2280 SSD slot and M.2 2230 wireless card slot. The battery is also replaceable.

Our battery testing consists of web browsing, running OpenGL tests, and streaming videos with the screen at 150 nits while connected to Wi-Fi. The Predator underwhelmed here despite having a large 92 WHr battery – the HyperX laptop outlasted it by almost two hours and the Gigabyte nearly two and a half hours. Most of the blame goes to Acer’s decision to use a screen locked at 240 Hz; more advanced displays would support a variable refresh rate or at least allow it to be lowered to 60 Hz.

We measure laptop surface temperatures during 15 loops of the Metro: Exodus stress test. During the test, the Acer’s temperatures peaked at 103 degrees F between the keyboard G and H keys, 81 F on the touchpad, and a toasty 129 F on bottom-center. Fan noise is audible particularly while gaming; hearing softer sounds in games will require using a headset. For daily use, the laptop hardly makes any noise.

PredatorSense remains the prominent app on Acer’s Predator gaming laptops. On the Predator Helios Neo 16S AI, it offers power settings, system monitoring, updates, and system settings. Power profiles include quiet, balanced (the default), performance, and turbo. Performance mode indicates it overclocks the CPU and GPU, but doesn’t specify details, while turbo appears to be performance mode but with the fan speeds maxed.

The app’s settings let you disable Nvidia Optimus, set up optimized battery charging, a bluelight shield, and toggle a 30-second keyboard backlighting timeout. Fan control is possible, though the custom option only allows you to set the RPM, not a curve. Most of the settings above plus lighting effects and a sound equalizer can be saved in scenario profiles.

Acer loads many more apps than PredatorSense – too many: Acer Assist, Acer Chroma Tune, Acer Intelligence Space, Acer LiveArt, Acer documents, Acer Jumpstart, Acer Legal Information, Acer My Key, Acer Purified Voice Console, Acer PurifiedView, and Acer QuickPanel create a lengthy list under the Start Menu’s “A” section. Many of them are simply links to the Windows Store.

Some of the apps actually installed are useful, like Acer My Key mentioned earlier. Others could be: Acer Intelligence Space provides many AI-driven features like a meeting assistant, an image generator, a media search tool, and a game assistant that claims to automatically change the color of your crosshair for maximum visibility. I didn’t test all these out – they aren’t specific to the Predator Helios Neo 16S AI – but it seems that they could add value if you’re not already vested in other AI services.

There’s also a decent amount of bloatware, including a McAfee trial, a Dropbox promotion, and Forge of Empires. While easily uninstalled, they simply don’t belong on a laptop this expensive.

Our Predator Helios Neo 16S AI includes a Core Ultra 9 290HX Plus CPU, Nvidia RTX 5070 Ti graphics card, 16GB of dual-channel RAM, a 1TB PCIe 4.0 solid-state drive, and a 16-inch 2560 x 1600 OLED display with a 240 Hz refresh rate. It retails for $2,774.99 from Best Buy, though I did see it briefly drop to $1,999.99 during my review period.

Best Buy also offers a 32GB version for $3,199.99, but it was discounted to $2,459.99 during our review period, making it less expensive than our model. Pricing is always in flux with the ongoing memory shortages, but Acer’s list prices are in line with market expectations. At Best Buy, many RTX 5070 Ti models hover around the same price as our model and many are pricier.

Acer also lists a $2,099.99 model on its site with 16GB of RAM, a 1TB SSD, and an RTX 5060, though I didn’t find this model for sale.

The Acer Predator Helios Neo 16S AI delivers on most of its thin-and-light gaming promise. Its RTX 5070 Ti provides solid gaming performance on its beautiful 240 Hz OLED display, and it follows up with a comfortable keyboard and touchpad, useful port selection, and solid upgradeability.

The trouble is that its portability is undermined by its below average battery life, an ironic shortcoming for a thin-and-light laptop. Audible gaming fans and lackluster speakers are further compromises, but the limited unplugged runtime is the one that those seeking a more portable package ought to be most concerned about.

But if you’re after a relatively slim gaming laptop to take between places where you can plug in, the Helios Neo 16S AI is an otherwise appealing package.

Charles Jefferies is a freelance reviewer for Tom’s Hardware US. He covers laptop and desktop PCs, especially gaming models.

#10399. AI Takes Root in Hospital Corridors, Raises Security Stakes

Cybersecurity In-Depth: Feature articles on security strategy, latest trends, and people to know.

Artificial intelligence is everywhere in healthcare, from doctors' intake forms to cancer screenings. But in some cases, life-and-death uses are outpacing cybersecurity.

Patients walk into a doctor's office and are swiftly asked permission for artificial intelligence (AI) scribes to document notes full of sensitive health information. That intake conversation is just one entry point, now that AI is embedded throughout the patient life cycle and accessing more data than most organizations can secure.

This raises significant concerns for a sector that historically struggles to secure legacy equipment and highly sought-after data, even before AI was added to the mix. Advancements could save invaluable time during patient care, but security will present hurdles in the form of supply chain risks that stem from AI vendor compromise, data leakage through prompts, and AI-generated social engineering campaigns.

A recent Proofpoint report, "2026 Study on Cybersecurity in Healthcare," notes that "AI adoption is accelerating faster than governance." Forty-five percent of organizations confirmed they had embedded AI into their systems, but 60% lacked the necessary controls to govern that sensitive data.

Related:NIST: Solo Shouldn't Mean Less Secure

Healthcare organizations are rapidly deploying AI to record intake conversations, and customer conversations increasingly revolve around how physicians are using AI for clinical decisions and, more importantly, how they are protecting those tools, says Susan Biddle, director of healthcare solutions at Proofpoint. Some Proofpoint customers use the OpenEvidence open source AI tool for research and diagnosis because it can integrate with Epic Systems, a widely used platform for electronic health records (EHR).

"The challenge is making sure physicians aren't putting sensitive information into that tool, so it does get challenging," Biddle tells Dark Reading.

Even seemingly generic details, like a patient's eye color, combined with a specific injury, like a left tibia fracture, could qualify as protected health information (PHI). That makes data sensitivity a major concern when feeding information into AI tools. Therefore, organizations should focus on securing the underlying data rather than the tool itself, Biddle says.

AI's use is spreading fast, which complicates how organizations think about risk. Tony Sabaj, head of Americas channel SEs at Check Point, says AI's pattern-recognition capability is already improving clinical care, catching early signs in cancer screenings, triaging critical patients, and reviewing electrocardiograms to "generate reports faster and with more accuracy."

Beyond the clinic, health insurers are using AI for claims processing and fraud detection; like other industries, healthcare is also building digital twins to model operational scenarios at scale.

Related:Incidents Move Too Fast for Pen & Paper

Some of AI's most visible momentum is in reducing administrative burdens, including documentation that converts clinician-patient conversations into draft notes, scheduling, intake, coding, and patient communications, says Cindi Carter, global chief information security officer at Check Point. It's also being used behind the scenes to support operational planning, research, and cybersecurity, she adds.

Organizations often sort AI tools into "medical" or "administrative" buckets. The first consists of diagnostic AI or treatment recommendation systems, while the latter includes intake forms or scheduling. Carter is concerned that these labels may undermine the downstream risks that administrative tools can still pose because a "documentation tool can still influence what becomes part of the medical record" and lead to clinical consequences.

In pharmaceutical and precision medicine, AI investment is outpacing security investment, Sabaj says. And as AI reaches further into healthcare, organizations need to make sure that existing regulatory frameworks keep pace. Guardrails often center on protecting data leakage, but safeguards should also ensure that if AI is taking any kind of medical action, it's limited to known parameters, he says.

Related:Behind the Scenes of How Operation Blackout Went Down

"We continue to see breaches of medical information, and AI has the potential to exacerbate this issue," Sabaj tells Dark Reading.

To get ahead of the threat, some hospitals are building their own AI tools, using open weight models and training large language models on proprietary data, rather than relying on public platforms to handle sensitive PHI.

Carter's concerns extend beyond confidentiality. Despite progress, AI is known to hallucinate. What if AI produces an inaccurate summary, omits an important detail, or acts on manipulated information? The organization can lose confidence in the information supporting care without a record ever being stolen, she says.

The CISO question then becomes broader, she says, shifting from "What data left?" to "What can this system see, what can it change, and can we reconstruct what happened?"

"In healthcare, we are protecting sensitive information and the integrity of decisions that depend on it," Sabaj says.

In the relatively short time span since rapid AI adoption took off, Phil Englert, VP of medical device security at Health-ISAC, says he has already observed changes that will affect how much sensitive data floods systems. As AI becomes increasingly integrated, when a cyber incident occurs, organizations frequently lose patient identifiers, medical records, insurance details, and other clinical or financial data — all considered PHI.

Englert cites recent Health and Human Services data that shows hacking and other IT-related incidents continue to be the leading causes of major healthcare breaches. Denver Health Medical Plan reported a breach Oct. 1 that affected 238,083 individuals, triggered by an exposed vendor access key through AI platform SquareML.

This tracks with Health-ISAC's 2026 threat landscape findings, which found that many significant breaches have originated through third-party vendors, "reinforcing the idea that an organization's security posture is often only as strong as that of its partners."

While organizations need to secure AI within their own environments, they also need to protect themselves as threat actors use it to craft more convincing and effective social engineering attacks, Englert warns. Healthcare organizations are seeing increasingly sophisticated phishing campaigns, deepfakes, voice impersonation attacks, and QR-code phishing — any method to exploit human trust in a sector driven by speed and precision.

While healthcare organizations can't seem to shake the ransomware threat, which "remains one of the most damaging facing the sector," AI-enabled attacks are a top cybersecurity concern for 2026, he adds.

Implementing effective guardrails has proved significantly challenging. Carter offers five recommendations informed by the governance and life cycle risk themes in the Joint Commissions and the Coalition for AI Health guidance: visibility, boundaries around data and actions, evidence from vendors, validation and meaningful human oversight, and continuous monitoring.

Organizations should maintain AI inventory and give staff a straightforward way to disclose unsanctioned uses as shadow AI risks pummel the sector. Limiting what data AI can handle is also essential; Carter recommends that access to information should not automatically confer authority to update records, send communications, or initiate transactions.

"AI compresses the time between a mistake and material loss," Carter says, and that should be reflected in organizations' safeguards.

Implementation also depends on organizational maturity. While talking with security officers across the industry, Proofpoint's Biddle has observed varying levels of AI adoption. Some are quite far in the process, while others are still cautious and taking it slow, she says, noting that healthcare tends to lag behind other industries when it comes to technology.

Many organizations lack resources but continually face disruptive attacks that severely affect patient care.

"It doesn't seem to be getting any better, unfortunately," Biddle says.

Getting better requires money, but when CISOs stand in front of the board and request investments to shore up defenses, they are often hit with: "Why do you need to spend this amount?" says Biddle. It's difficult because they are competing with budget dollars for life-saving devices, she says.

"But I would say: Leverage the impact of cyber safety," Biddle adds. "Cyber safety is patient safety."

Arielle spent the last decade working as a reporter, transitioning from human interest stories to covering all things cybersecurity related in 2020. Now, as a features writer for Dark Reading, she delves into the security problems enterprises face daily, providing context and actionable steps. She looks for stories that go past the initial news to understand where the industry is going. Her coverage areas include identity and access management, cyber risk and operations, industrial control systems, operational technology, and ransomware trends.

She previously lived in Florida where she wrote for the Tampa Bay Times before returning to Boston where her cybersecurity career took off at TechTarget SearchSecurity. When she's not writing about cybersecurity, she pursues personal projects that include a mystery novel and poetry collection.

#10397. Bizarre AI chip demo uses butter to highlight how new chip runs 40 degrees cooler than the competition

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

South Korean chip startup DEEPX says its new AI chip will be at the heart of upcoming Hyundai robots.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Processors generating excess heat play a significant part in contemporary data center design, cost, and resource use. However, things could be vastly different if AI chips were more efficient and ran far cooler. South Korean chip startup DEEPX, recently featured by CNBC, thinks its AI chip designs are the answer. DEEPX demonstrated its DX-M1 processor’s cool-running capabilities in a bizarre new dairy-based thermal benchmark. While rival chips caused a knob of butter to sizzle in seconds, atop the DEEPX solution, the butter block remains solid.

Concerns over AI data center proliferation are growing worldwide. These developments are shaping business, the environment, and politics. Some people have very strong feelings about them, and hundreds of people have risked being thrown into jail protesting data centers. The CNBC report linked in the intro looks into why modern data centers are problematic, and indicates that the core issues stem from the use of hot-running processors and the extensive power and cooling apparatus they need.

DEEPX says its simple answer is to “make the AI chips themselves run cooler.” The CNBC video includes a demo of the ultra-low-power DX-M1 processor. In a side-by-side test against unnamed rival AI chips, you can see the DX-M1 NPU is clearly cooler. DEEPX CEO Kim Nok-won claims a temperature difference of 25 to more than 40 degrees Celsius compared with competitors. Kim said the side-by-side chips processed “the same AI workload… and every chip melted the butter except the last chip [DX-M1].”

The dairy-based thermal challenge is certainly eyebrow-raising, but we note some big gaps in the comparison information. Most notable was the question of how fast each processor tackles the test workload. Anyone can run a chip cool if the power and clocks are scaled back. PC enthusiasts undervolt and/or underclock their GPUs all the time for efficiency, better thermals, and reduced fan noise.

DEEPX’s DX-M1 NPU has been produced on Samsung 5nm since August 2025, according to The Elec reports. It has won approximately $13.3M worth of orders thanks to this sliver of silicon. The company is planning to move forward with the next-gen DX-M2 AI chip, which is based on Samsung 2nm, with its mass production aiming for H2 2027.

In conclusion, these NPUs look set for edge computing and robotics applications, so they aren’t really competing in the same space as the racks of power-gulping Nvidia Blackwell and Ruben GPUs featured in the CNBC news feature. Eventually, we might see the hundreds or thousands of hot-running Nvidia GPUs with beefy liquid cooling apparatus get replaced by cooler-running custom accelerators. Such an innovation could cool the backlash against AI data centers. However, DEEPX’s DX-M1 or even DX-M2 chips aren’t going to compete in that space, so this butter test is a bit of a red herring.

The CNBC report also highlights SK hynix’s work on iHBM, a next-gen low-power consumption, and cool-running. The ‘i’ stands for ‘Integrated Cooling Element,’ according to an exec talking to CNBC. CoolIT is also trying a similar approach by bringing water cooling even closer to the heat-generating silicon.
