#10394. Top 6 AI Image Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A designer who once spent an afternoon searching stock libraries can now describe the picture they want and have four versions in under a minute. AI image generators have moved from curiosity to everyday tool for blog headers, product mockups, ad concepts, game art and slide decks. The quality gap between tools has narrowed, but the differences that remain matter a lot: how well each follows a detailed prompt, whether it can render readable text, how it handles edits, and what you are allowed to do with the result.

This guide compares six of the best-known options, what each does well and where each falls short. Models, plans and limits change often, so check each vendor's own pages before you commit to a workflow.

OpenArt’s AI image generator puts several image models behind one interface. Its generator page lists models including GPT Image 2.5, Grok Imagine, Qwen Image 3, Nano Banana 2 and Seedream 5.0 Pro, and lets you create images from text prompts or reference photos. It also lists tools for camera angle control, upscaling, collages and a Character Builder for keeping characters consistent across scenes, with 4K export.

Best for: Creators who want to compare several models on the same prompt without juggling separate subscriptions and accounts.

Limitations: The page offers a free start but does not state credit amounts or plan details, and it does not address commercial use, so check the pricing and licensing pages before using results for client work. Output quality depends on which model you pick.

OpenAI's image generation in ChatGPT works inside the chat interface, which makes iteration natural: generate an image, then ask for changes in plain language. OpenAI highlights accurate text rendering, the ability to follow prompts with many distinct objects, and consistent characters across multi-turn edits. You can also upload reference images and have the model transform them. It handles both photorealistic and stylized looks.

Best for: Marketers and writers who want to describe, refine and re-describe an image without learning a new tool.

Limitations: OpenAI notes that tall images such as posters can be cropped too tightly, and that dense small text, multilingual text and precise graphs can still go wrong. Images can take up to a minute to render. All generated images carry C2PA metadata identifying their source.

Gemini's image generation runs on Google's Nano Banana models. Google highlights character consistency, text rendering in many languages, combining several photos, precise editing and 2K resolution. A free tier covers image generation and editing, while the most advanced model requires a Google AI plan. You start from the "Create images" option in the Gemini app, then prompt or upload a picture to edit.

Best for: People who need to keep the same subject across several images, or who want to edit existing photos quickly.

Limitations: Usage limits apply, and the strongest model is behind a paid plan. Images include an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible AI label, which is good for transparency but may not suit every use.

Midjourney is an independent research lab known for images with a strong, painterly look. Its official site points users to a sign-up flow and to a Discord community and help center for support. Many creators choose it for concept art, mood boards and illustration where style matters more than literal accuracy.

Best for: Artists, art directors and game designers who want rich, stylized results and enjoy exploring variations.

Limitations: I could not confirm current pricing, trial terms or feature details from Midjourney's public pages, so check them yourself before subscribing. The workflow can feel less like a normal app than the others on this list, and it is not the easiest route for precise, text-heavy graphics.

Adobe Firefly is built for professionals who already work in Creative Cloud. Adobe states that its Firefly models are commercially safe, trained on licensed Adobe Stock and public domain content, and outputs carry Content Credentials that show AI involvement. It offers text-to-image, image-to-image, sketch-to-image and style transfer, plus editing tools such as background removal, object removal, upscaling and generative expand. It connects with Photoshop, Illustrator, Lightroom and Adobe Express, and also lets you use partner models from other companies.

Best for: Designers and brand teams who need clear licensing and want AI inside the apps they already use.

Limitations: Plans work on a credit system, so heavy use adds up. Adobe lists a free tier with limited daily generations and paid plans starting at about $9.99 a month, but confirm current terms on its site.

Ideogram built its reputation on a problem that trips up many generators: readable text. Its current models are presented as producing clean typography in different layouts, with attention to prompt fidelity and reliable editing. It also offers an API with access to a catalog of models, and has released open weights for one earlier model for local use and customization under its license.

Best for: Posters, social graphics, logo concepts and anything where words are part of the image.

Limitations: The pages I checked did not describe a free plan, so look at current plans before relying on it. Like every generator, it still needs a human to proofread the text it produces.

Start with the job. If you want to compare several models side by side, try OpenArt. If you want to talk your way to a finished image, try ChatGPT or Gemini. If you need images that will sit inside a branded design workflow, Firefly is the safer fit. If you are chasing a distinctive look, Midjourney deserves a trial. If text must be perfect, test Ideogram.

Run the same prompt in two or three tools before paying. Differences in composition, hands, faces and text show up quickly, and they matter more than any feature list.

Reviews only go so far, because your own use case is what counts. Pick one realistic job, for example a 16:9 header image for a blog post about web performance, and run the same prompt through two or three tools. Write the prompt once and paste it unchanged, so the comparison is fair:

Then score each result on five questions: Did it follow every instruction? Are there odd details in hands, edges or objects? Is any text spelled correctly? Does it still look good when cropped to a square? Could you license it for your project? Ten minutes of this teaches more than any feature list, and it shows how much editing each tool needs before you can publish.

Most readers of this guide will put these images on web pages, where a few habits matter more than the generator you choose.

There is no single best AI image generator. The right pick depends on whether you value conversation, style, licensing, control or typography. Test two or three on a real project, compare the output, and keep a human in charge of the final choice. Whatever you generate, review it for errors and label AI-made images clearly where your audience would expect it.

Are AI-generated images free to use commercially? It depends on the provider and your plan. Read the licensing terms for the tool you use, and consider vendors that publish how their models were trained if you work for clients.

Which AI image generator is best for text in images? Ideogram and ChatGPT both highlight text rendering, and Gemini lists it as a feature. Test each with your own wording and proofread the result.

Do AI images have watermarks? Some do. OpenAI adds C2PA metadata, Google adds an invisible SynthID watermark and a visible label, and Adobe attaches Content Credentials.

Can I use more than one image model without separate accounts? Yes. Platforms such as OpenArt put several models in one interface, which makes side-by-side testing easier. Check the licensing terms for each model you use.

Should I add alt text to AI-generated images? Yes, when the image carries meaning. Describe what it shows or why it is there. Purely decorative images can use an empty alt attribute, following the W3C guidance.

#10393. Ray-Ban Meta Audio review: Great specs, shame about the price

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses attempt to curb the controversy by removing the cameras, and while they’re still an excellent product, the price makes them harder to recommend.

This has been a bit of a rollercoaster year for Meta wearables. Meta’s VR division shrank considerably earlier this year, then later announced a massively redesigned follow-up that’ll launch next year. And while Meta has debuted dozens of new smart glasses styles throughout the year that have sold quite well, the company can’t seem to get away from controversy. That’s where Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses come in.

Now, that doesn’t mean Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses aren’t worthwhile. They feature a much wider prescription range (-12 to +6) than the majority of Meta smart glasses models, which often cap out at -6 to +6. The battery life is also significantly better thanks to the lack of a camera and other improvements in the hardware. And, of course, if you want a pair of AI glasses but don’t want the camera, these are the only options from Meta.

If I were to choose between the two available models, I’d pick the Clubmaster style every time. Not only do they look better overall and feature the thinnest frames of any Meta smart glasses, but they also have substantially better nosepads that are adjustable and can be easily replaced.

The Burbank model looks just fine, but the nosepads aren’t the same, and the glasses feel more rigid overall (which I find less comfortable). Both models come in lots of colors with many different lens options, including clear, tinted, polarized, Transitions, and prescription.

Both models feature a touchpad on the outside of the right temple arm, as well as a small, circular button up top to activate Meta AI. You can alternatively use the “Hey, Meta” keywords to activate Meta AI. The button and touchpad can be disabled if you like. Only the touchpad has an alternative function, and that alternative function only controls music playback via Spotify or Amazon Music. Other Meta glasses offer far more options.

Both styles are the lightest Meta glasses available. Most Meta smart glasses with cameras weigh in around the 54g mark. Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses weigh just 44g. They’re not as light as Even Realities G2, RayNeo iO, or MemoMind One, but 44g is still considered pretty light for smart glasses.

Ray-Ban Meta Audio glasses reuse the case from Ray-Ban Meta Gen 2, which looks more like a traditional Ray-Ban case rather than the foldable one that ships with Meta Ray-Ban Display or the 2026 Meta Camera Glasses. I don’t have an issue with Ray-Ban Meta Audio’s case at all, but I really enjoy the foldable one and wish it were included.

Audio playback on these is great, and while the speakers are identical to all other Meta glasses products, using them as spatial surround sound “headphones” while watching a movie with surround sound is a fantastic experience. They also have spatial audio recording and truly excellent noise cancellation thanks to the 6-mic setup.

But the lack of the newer processor from Ray-Ban Meta Gen 3 means the glasses don’t support the new hearing aid function. To me, that’s a massive missed opportunity since these are audio-only glasses. When I asked Meta about it, they only said it wasn’t available on Ray-Ban Meta Audio for now, so this problem could be fixed in an upcoming update.

And that’s actually where Meta’s glasses might shine brightest: software updates. The company has been exemplary with update cadence on all of its products, and its smart glasses constantly get new, innovative features that surprise and delight. While I’m a little underwhelmed with the number of features available on these glasses today, this product will likely look quite different a year from now, a fact that’s backed up by years of Meta significantly improving product feature sets over time.

If you buy these glasses today, know that you’re mainly investing in them for the future. They’re fine enough for today’s usage, especially if you’re interested in a hands-free way to use Meta AI products like Muse or just want an open-ear way to listen to music and take phone calls, but Meta’s penchant for continually adding new features is, undoubtedly, going to be the highlight for everyone who ever buys them.

#10389. Which MCM Has the Best Revshare? A Developer’s Guide to Evaluating Ad Partners

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An ad partner’s revenue share looks easy to compare: keeping 90% sounds better than keeping 80%. But the percentage does not tell a developer what will happen when the integration reaches production. Extra JavaScript, unstable ad slots, opaque reporting, and difficult rollbacks can change the value of an apparently attractive deal.

For teams maintaining an advertising-funded website, choosing a Multiple Customer Management (MCM) partner is partly an engineering decision. The integration affects page templates, consent handling, third-party requests, account access, and monitoring. Those changes deserve the same scrutiny as a new analytics SDK or payment integration.

This guide explains how to compare revenue shares on a consistent basis, review integration requirements, and test an ad setup without sacrificing usability. The goal is a repeatable evaluation process, rather than a universal winner.

Google Ad Manager’s MCM system lets a parent publisher manage a child publisher’s account or delegated inventory. The commercial agreement defines the partner’s services and compensation. Before comparing offers, ask which revenue streams the quoted percentage covers and whether the reporting figure is before or after that fee.

Use three separate lines in your evaluation spreadsheet:

Revenue base: record the amount to which the partner’s percentage applies. Advertiser spending, exchange-reported revenue, and publisher earnings are different figures. Do not treat them as interchangeable.

Partner compensation: identify the percentage, fixed charges, and any optional services. Ask whether the same terms apply to every demand source and ad format.

Adjustments: distinguish estimated revenue from finalized earnings, including invalid-traffic deductions and other corrections. Document when adjustments appear and which party bears them.

A common mistake is applying the partner fee twice because a dashboard already displays net revenue. Label each exported metric before using it in a comparison.

The two delegation models have different implications for access, reporting, and payments. Google’s documentation is the starting point for checking the proposed setup.

Manage Account gives the parent edit access within the child’s account while the child retains access and permissions. Payment facilitation can allocate eligible earnings from the child’s primary Ad Exchange account, but it does not automatically cover every third-party revenue stream. Confirm the invitation’s payment percentage and the separate commercial agreement.

Manage Inventory lets the parent manage delegated inventory in its own account, without access to the child account. Reporting for that inventory sits in the parent account. Google pays the parent for the relevant inventory, and the parent pays the child according to their agreement. Ask how you will obtain reports and reconcile payments.

Before implementation, document who can change tags, ad units, consent settings, and demand configuration. Agree on access for debugging and exporting historical data. A developer should be able to trace a production request to its configuration owner.

Use this table as a request-for-information checklist. It does not rank providers or assert that any named service supports a particular implementation. Obtain current terms and technical documentation for the product you are considering.

Apply the same standards to every proposal. The following examples show what a developer should ask during evaluation; each question is relevant across providers.

MonetizeMore offers publisher monetization products, including PubGuru and Traffic Cop. When evaluating its proposal, connect the commercial discussion to concrete integration questions: which scripts will load, who owns the configuration, and how will reported earnings be reconciled?

Request a sample report showing the revenue base, partner fee, and deductions as separate fields. Check whether the proposed plan includes every service shown in the sales demonstration.

Ask for a written ownership map covering Ad Manager access, tag deployment, configuration changes, and incident response. Identify who can restore the previous setup if delivery fails.

During a trial, compare representative templates and devices. Record script requests and main-thread activity alongside net revenue, using the same measurement definitions for the existing setup.

Decision criterion: choose on measured results and documented responsibilities, rather than assuming that a quoted split predicts final earnings.

For any proposal involving a header-bidding wrapper, including one from Setupad, ask how the wrapper fits into your existing page lifecycle.

Review bidder configuration, auction timeouts, and when the ad server receives targeting information. A missing bid and an unfilled ad slot are different problems.

Check whether your team can inspect the active configuration and reproduce an issue on a test page. Agree on who approves changes to bidders or timeout values.

Measure the complete sequence: consent readiness, auction start, bid responses, ad request, and rendering. Avoid attributing every delay to the same component.

Decision criterion: the implementation should be observable enough to explain failures, with commercial terms verified separately.

When assessing Publisher Collective or another managed partner, establish how the proposed setup could be tested on a limited, agreed scope.

Ask whether rollout can be restricted to selected templates or an eligible traffic cohort. Do not assume partial testing is available until the partner confirms it.

Record device, geography, page type, and consent state so differences in traffic mix do not masquerade as a revenue improvement.

Keep a dated deployment log connecting each configuration version with the reporting period.

Decision criterion: a measured comparison should separate implementation changes from changes in audience or demand.

A proposal from AdPushup or another optimization provider may involve changes to ad placement. Review the actual proposed changes with both design and engineering.

Check spacing, responsive behavior, keyboard navigation, and whether sticky placements obscure content or controls.

Reserve space for expected ad dimensions and test what happens when no ad is returned.

Compare layout stability before and after the change.

Decision criterion: additional earnings should be assessed alongside usability and performance, with explicit acceptance criteria.

For an Ezoic proposal, as for any managed platform, start with the exact integration method offered to your site.

List required changes to scripts, plugins, caching, DNS, or delivery infrastructure, if any. Do not assume that every deployment uses the same method.

Test interactions with your consent manager, content security policy, caching rules, and existing analytics.

Document how to remove the integration and restore the prior configuration.

Decision criterion: the operational cost should be visible before the first production rollout.

When reviewing a Monumetric proposal, establish which implementation work belongs to your team and which belongs to the provider.

Ask who creates ad slots, updates templates, and validates responsive sizes.

Request a support escalation process with the diagnostic information required for an incident: URL, timestamp, device, consent state, and affected slot.

Confirm onboarding costs and payment terms in writing.

Decision criterion: routine changes and production failures should have a clear owner.

Chrome DevTools helps trace third-party requests in the Network panel and investigate long tasks in the Performance panel. Capture a baseline before deployment, then repeat the same navigation and interaction sequence after deployment. Use comparable device and network settings.

Google Publisher Console helps investigate Google Publisher Tag ad slots and delivery issues. For a Prebid-based setup, use Prebid’s debugging tools to inspect the auction path. These tools answer different questions: whether the auction behaved as expected and whether the ad server delivered the requested slot.

PageSpeed Insights provides performance diagnostics; distinguish its lab results from available field data. Add web-vitals to your own monitoring when you need measurements associated with a deployment or cohort. Track LCP, INP, and CLS alongside revenue instead of treating a single Lighthouse score as the complete user experience.

Reserve responsive space for ad slots before content arrives, and investigate shifts when empty slots collapse. SitePoint’s guide to web performance optimization explains related techniques for loading JavaScript, reducing layout work, and managing long tasks.

With the same traffic volume, revenue definition, and deduction rate, the break-even increase in pre-partner revenue is: (current publisher share ÷ new publisher share) − 1. Express shares consistently as fractions or percentages. The table below shows the required lift; it is not a forecast these thresholds only describe the arithmetic. Different ad density, audience mix, deduction rates, or reporting bases can invalidate the comparison. Normalize to the same denominator, such as revenue per thousand pageviews, and compare matched periods or properly assigned concurrent cohorts.

Assume two hypothetical setups each receive one million pageviews. Here, RPM means revenue per thousand pageviews before the partner fee. For this simplified example, deductions reduce that revenue before the publisher share is applied.

Partner A: $10 pre-partner RPM × 1,000 groups of pageviews × 0.90 after deductions × 0.90 publisher share = $8,100 net revenue.

Partner B: $12 pre-partner RPM × 1,000 groups of pageviews × 0.98 after deductions × 0.80 publisher share = $9,408 net revenue.

The difference is $1,308 for the period despite Partner B’s lower share. These are illustrative inputs, not provider performance claims. Faster payment improves cash-flow timing; it does not, by itself, increase the revenue earned.

Use Playwright with mocked responses or test creatives in a controlled environment to check slow loading, empty slots, blocked scripts, and navigation between routes. Verify that the page remains usable when the ad system fails. Automated tests should not click live advertisements or generate artificial monetized traffic.

Separate unit tests for configuration logic, integration tests for component boundaries, and browser tests for user-visible behavior. SitePoint’s explanation of unit, functional, and integration testing provides a useful foundation. Define rollback criteria before launch, keep the control configuration available, and review finalized revenue alongside performance data before expanding the deployment.

There is no universal answer from the percentage alone. Compare current written offers using the same revenue base, then evaluate finalized net earnings, performance, access, and implementation effort.

Not necessarily. Reports may differ in timezone, currency, transaction coverage, fee treatment, and finalization date. Reconcile those definitions before treating a discrepancy as an integration bug.

Ask how renewals, volume tiers, optional services, and rate changes are handled. Record the applicable terms with the configuration and reporting period used for your evaluation.

No. If deductions and the revenue base are otherwise comparable, an 80% share needs 12.5% more pre-partner revenue to match a 90% share. Measure whether that lift exists rather than assuming it.

Do not treat any protection claim as a guarantee. Ask how suspected invalid traffic is handled, what evidence is available, and how later adjustments are reflected in your reports and payments.

Choose the setup that produces verifiable net value while meeting the site’s performance and operational requirements. A good evaluation leaves your team with reproducible measurements, understandable reports, and a practical rollback plan—not just a favorable percentage.

#10387. Nvidia cracks down on overseas cloud AI GPU rentals for China

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Rent abroad or buy compliant cards: both of China’s routes to Nvidia compute are running short.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nvidia-based compute is getting scarce and expensive for Chinese AI labs on both of their main routes, buying export-compliant cards and renting abroad, and the Financial Times reports that Nvidia’s own crackdowns are part of the reason. Nvidia has told cloud partners worldwide that at least 51% of their services must go to non-Chinese firms, while Chinese companies have bought more than 1 million Nvidia gaming and workstation cards this year to cover the shortfall. Meanwhile, a B300 rack that sells for about $600,000 internationally reportedly fetched $2.8 million in China last month.

Chinese customers have ordered more than 6 million of Nvidia’s export-compliant RTX Pro 5500 cards. That is at least twice the 3 million that the company could reportedly manufacture in 2027. One source told CNBC that it was proposed that Moonshot pay 30% to 50% more upfront, in comparison to a non-Chinese entity.

Many Chinese groups have relied on overseas leases for advanced AI work, which can include model training, and these tech groups have been the biggest clients for data centers in Southeast Asia. Data centers unable to find non-Chinese clients could be forced to pull back supply, and it’s “difficult to find enough non-Chinese clients outside the US,” one Singapore-based group told the FT. While Nvidia hasn’t confirmed or denied its new rule, implementing it would ease criticism from U.S. lawmakers just as Chinese demand is growing.

What Nvidia told partners has come through verbal instructions, via sales and regional teams, and applies globally rather than just in Asia. Existing deals and leases are not changed, but new ones must comply. Nvidia’s whitelist more than halved its authorized Asian buyer count, which encouraged more Chinese groups to lease over other options, including smuggling. On top of this, Fleet Intelligence tracking is now required for Southeast Asian providers that want to become Nvidia Cloud Partners. All of these combined — whitelist, cap, and tracking — may act to alleviate U.S. concerns.

Among companies that have moved to renting, Moonshot has held discussions to rent capacity from neoclouds that host in Europe with Nvidia GPUs. Specifically, it’s looking for Nvidia Blackwell systems, which export controls bar from China; one source said the talks covered clusters of 8,000 or more Blackwell GPUs in Europe. These clusters would handle inference, serving models to end users, and the European talks for them happened after the White House said Moonshot accessed Blackwell chips through a facility in Thailand. The firm has been fervent about its intentions to secure more compute.

Moonshot isn't the only Chinese company looking to secure Nvidia chips for AI training. Tencent has a deal with Oracle to lease 100,000 GPUs in Southeast Asian data centers. ByteDance and Alibaba are also pursuing similar long-term leases, and most of these overseas data centers run chips like the forbidden B300. MiniMax, like Moonshot, also leases significant compute both at home and abroad.

The big reason is that domestic AI chip supply is simply not up to the task, although not for a lack of trying. Huawei is expected to hit 1.6 million chips this year, with about 1 million from Alibaba and Baidu, and 300,000 from Cambricon. Total supply this year will be up to 3.5 million, including about 1 million made overseas, against a demand that far exceeds 5 million. That number could more than double next year.

Part of the problem is that the export-compliant cards Chinese firms have been buying, mostly the RTX Pro 6000D, RTX Pro 5000, and RTX 5090, are mostly useful for simpler work such as prefill. Even so, the discontinued 6000D’s pricing in China has reportedly doubled. Huawei’s Ascend 910C outperforms them in most areas, but these lower-end options are still coveted, partly because of a preference for CUDA over Huawei’s CANN. Nvidia’s programming platform is supported by most AI frameworks and libraries, and much in-house code is optimized for it.

Then there’s the RTX Pro 5500, with 84GB of GDDR7 with ECC and 1,398 GB/s of memory bandwidth. The card runs on a full PCIe Gen 5 x16 interface, draws up to 600 W, and can be split into up to two MIG instances. That still leaves it well short of a B300, which has about 3.4 times the memory and 5.7 times the bandwidth, plus NVLink, which the 5500 lacks.

Export-compliant; samples in December, deliveries from Q1 2027

Licensed by the U.S. case by case; Beijing withholding approval for large-scale imports

Indicated orders from China are not precise, but the total of over six million includes more than three million from ByteDance, Alibaba, and Tencent. The big three ordered at about $11,400 each, for a total sticker price over $35 billion. This is difficult to fill when there may be fewer than three million produced next year, and partners might only actually get 5% to 10% of the original ask. Starting in 2027, much of Nvidia’s TSMC capacity may shift to Rubin, taking more production, while the RTX Pro 5500’s early samples are due in December.

On the legal side, it’s currently easier to rent than to own as current U.S. law does not give regulators broad authority over overseas cloud access. The Remote Access Security Act (RASA), which would extend U.S. export controls to that area, has sailed through the House but has not yet passed the Senate. Those directly affected — neoclouds and related financiers — price in a possible ban regardless, which drives the upfront premium.

China’s side is not completely clean, either, with about 600,000 H200s reportedly stuck in storage in Hong Kong awaiting shipment to the mainland. Beijing is withholding approvals for large-volume imports such as this. Only relatively small quantities have come in during recent months, for some big internet companies, with others coming through intermediaries and indirect routes in small quantities. The price: over $900,000 for an eight-card H200 server in China at the end of last month, about 3x the cost a year ago. This is despite the fact that H200 sales are already under U.S. license.

The first test that will show how many cards will actually land occurs in Q1 2027, when the RTX Pro 5500 is expected in commercial deliveries. For rentals, whether or not the Remote Access Security Act is a factor depends on a Senate vote, which is not yet scheduled. Nvidia’s Q3 results come in soon, on Nov. 17, which could bring some illumination.

With CEO Jensen Huang under scrutiny lately over reports that he was targeted by an alleged Chinese spy, which Nvidia’s board is now investigating, he’s going to feel the heat even more just a day later, when lawmakers have asked him to attend a closed-door briefing. He’s been pushing for laxer controls and more access, so the assumed 51% rule will also be put to the test.

Shane Downing is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware, covering consumer storage, PC hardware, and AI.

Woman who says she was dismissed for not signing up brought the suit to the US District Court for the District of Colorado.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A former employee of The Ethereum Foundation is suing the nonprofit, alleging disability discrimination, retaliation, unfair employment practices, and related claims, reports The Denver Post. 'The Ethereum Foundation Mandate,' a 38-page vision for the next 1,000 years, was distributed to the organization's ~250 employees in Q1 this year. On page 11 of the finalized document, there was a bizarre and rather extreme oath. This illustrated page essentially formed a suicide pledge and, unsurprisingly, scores of people didn't want to sign.

At the top of this story, you can see page 11 from the evidence submitted in the case of FULTON v. ETHEREUM ASIA PACIFIC LIMITED et al. Perhaps one could say it was illustrated like an anime version of Grimm’s fairy tales. Indeed, it contains more than a touch of the macabre, like all the best such stories.

On this particular page, reproduced in court documents, anyone working on the software used by the Ethereum Federation (EF) was asked to ensure that the source code and associated documents remained free and publicly accessible. Underlining this pledge, folks had to vow to “take his or her own life with a sword upon failure to uphold any of the pledges in this license, or upon modification or removal of any part of this license.” The message about falling on one’s sword was repeated in the illustration by a cartoon cat.

At the end of the 38-page document, employees picked one of two options, basically agreeing to commit to the direction of the mandate, or not. Choosing the former meant you signed up to this cult-like pact. The latter choice would mean that you were requesting a settlement payment and would “leave the EF without delay.”

This unsettling contractual suicide agreement went out as a draft in late February, and The Denver Post says that resistance to signing such an agreement was “swift and vocal.” About 80 of the Ethereum Federation’s 250-ish employees voted to nix the suicide pledge. Nevertheless, when the mandate was sent around in its final version in March, the suicide pledge remained – albeit in the form of a text with illustration – rather than in the body of the copy.

As the ‘Source Seppuku License’ had basically morphed into an artwork, rather than part of the main text of the mandate, the EF would subsequently argue that the suicide pledge to employees was just “part of the artwork of the mandate.” Importantly, Ethereum founder Vitalik Buterin insists that the artwork was “not meant to be taken literally.”

In March, resistance to the mandate, led by then-employee Kayleen Fulton, culminated in a response letter endorsing the general direction of the document but declining to tick any boxes, and declining to resign. Though this formal but quiet revolt bore 53 names, only Boulder woman Fulton and a colleague who helped organize the letter were fired.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The reasoning for Fulton being singled out was simple, according to a quote published by the Denver Post, cryptocurrency founder Buterin reportedly said that you should not “reward making a stink with outcomes.” By September, eight senior employees had left the EF, with most of the 53 protest letter signees either resigning or being terminated.

Fulton, who was on a salary of $195,000 per year as a lead in the Protocol Support team, is seeking unspecified damages from the Ethereum Foundation. She also disclosed her ovarian cancer diagnosis to the company on March 24, 2026, roughly two weeks before being fired. The lawsuit thus also claims interference with her rights under the Family and Medical Leave Act.

#10382. GhostAction Hacking Wave Fizzles, but Attackers Lurk on in GitHub

The supply chain attack campaign's shift toward developer endpoints and cloud access signals what could be next for the cybercrime outfit.

A new wave of GhostAction attacks has largely failed, but this supply chain campaign operator is well-positioned to cause more chaos down the line.

GhostAction, an ongoing campaign in which attackers target GitHub by compromising developer accounts and injecting GitHub Actions workflow files into repositories.

Workflows tell GitHub Actions what code to execute when events occur (such as when a commit is pushed or a pull request is opened); these malicious workflows rely on GitHub's high trust in these files (by design), seizing repository secrets, package publishing tokens, GitHub personal access tokens, credentials embedded in source code, and much more.

The idea is that an initial compromise could be leveraged into more compromises and access points across repositories, CI/CD systems, and cloud environments, not unlike many supply chain worms observed in the wild lately, albeit functioning differently.

Related:AI Makes Reverse Engineering Mainstream Software & Malware Far Easier

The campaign was first documented last year, and attackers continue to deploy GhostAction against organizations. What has changed is an apparent escalation of the technique beginning this month. During an earlier Oct. 8-12 wave, attackers pushed the security-audit.yml workflow into roughly 30,000 repositories, using compromised accounts to distribute credential-stealing code at scale. But GitGuardian on Oct. 27 published research highlighting further escalation. GitGuardian's Gaetan Ferry wrote that between Oct. 13 and 21, GhostAction's operator "pushed 72,000 commits to 23,000 public repositories," noting that 8,000 of them were new targets.

During this latest wave of the campaign, GitGuardian observed that once a developer account is compromised, the operator would deploy different payloads depending on which runner the repositories were hosted on. Runners are the systems that execute GitHub Actions workflows. Many organizations use GitHub-hosted runners, while others configure self-hosted runners that run inside their own environments.

These payloads include one called "seven workflows," which searches GitHub runner repositories and wikis for credentials including AI-provider API keys; the "Security-audit.yml" file seen in earlier GhostAction attacks that searches GitHub-hosted source code and Git history for credentials; self-hosted runner payloads targeting self-hosted CI runners; a payload targeting GitHub runners and AWS-requesting GitHub OIDC tokens (in order to assume AWS roles and achieve cloud account access); and the font stealer payload, which is the most notable workflow payload seen in recent attacks, due to its provenance.

Related:Microsoft Fixes RCE Bug in Windows Paint After Weeks of Confusion

The font stealer family installs a Node.js credential stealer on local developer machines, disguised as a VS Code task. The technique appears to be copied from PolinRider, a campaign that hides obfuscated loaders in developer repositories to steal credentials and has been associated with North Korea. The attacker also added PolinRider loaders between Oct. 22 and 26 to repositories already impacted by GhostAction. Ferry says that out of the five payloads, only the PolinRider-styled local stealer had a chance to successfully run, but GitGuardian has no evidence that any run actually occurred.

GitGuardian could not assess whether there was overlap between GhostAction and PolinRider, but hypothesized that GhostAction's operator possibly copied a public PolinRider launcher in order to target an overlapping set of victims.

Jenn Gile, cofounder of OpenSourceMalware (which previously covered the GhostAction attacks this month and detected signs of the PolinRider copycat a few weeks ago), tells Dark Reading that the PolinRider clone illustrates some of the inner workings of the GhostAction group.

"What we're seeing looks like imitation, not evolution. The fake-font technique in GhostAction is a copy of what DPRK's PolinRider campaign has been using nearly all of 2026," she says in an email. "The multiple payloads are real, but they're variations on a theme (five workflow versions over about ten days in one repo we examined), not a new capability. Historically, we might have called this evolution because it took real skill to figure out. But this looks a lot like an LLM helping the threat actor test different variants."

Related:Critical Rails Bug Threatens to Derail Vulnerable Servers

All of that said, GitHub identified and prevented execution of the malicious workflows, GitGuardian noted. However, importantly, the attackers still demonstrated persistent access to nearly 1,800 compromised accounts, many of which had already been used in earlier GhostAction activity; "1,382 of the 1,792 compromised accounts had already been used on Oct. 8, so the same stolen tokens keep working," according to Ferry.

Ferry tells Dark Reading that the campaign has been "highly ineffective on the malicious workflows side." But even if there's no sign this wave succeeded, the nature of the payloads (such as the one aimed at gaining cloud access) also shows where the attackers may intend to pivot next, toward internal networks where there are fewer platform-level protections that could thwart their efforts.

"We expect new waves to appear in the near future, with revised payloads," he explains. "This 'fail early and adapt fast' strategy has already been observed for this threat actor."

Given that targeted accounts or repositories remain compromised, repo owners should look for and delete the files that GitGuardian lists in the IoC section of the blog, on every branch. And, if signs of compromise are detected, users should revoke the account's tokens across the board and rotate every credential committed in the affected repository or its history, as well as every Actions secret and wiki credential.

Alex is an award-winning writer, journalist, and podcast host based in Boston. After cutting his teeth writing for independent gaming publications as a teenager, he graduated from Emerson College in 2016 with a Bachelor of Science in journalism. At Dark Reading, he covers a variety of cybersecurity topics, including the cybercrime ecosystem, open source security, and the intersection between AI and threat actors. In his spare time, Alex hosts the weekly Nintendo podcast, "Talk Nintendo Podcast," and works on personal writing projects, including two previously self-published science fiction novels.

#10381. Top 6 AI Video Generators in 2026: Features, Strengths, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A year ago, most AI video clips lasted a few seconds, had no sound and drifted off-model by the final frame. Today several tools produce 1080p or even 4K clips with synchronized audio, camera control and reference-based consistency. The market also moves quickly: OpenAI announced it was shutting down Sora in March 2026, so the list of tools worth using has changed.

This guide compares six AI video generators that developers, designers and marketers can use today. For each one you will see what it does well, where it falls short and who it suits. Plans, credits and model names change often, so confirm details on each vendor’s site before you commit.

OpenArt’s AI video generator puts a range of video models behind one interface. Its page lists models such as Veo 3.1, Kling, Seedance, Wan, Hailuo and PixVerse, so you can run the same prompt on different engines and compare the results without managing separate accounts.

Beyond text-to-video and image-to-video, it offers start and end frame control, video-to-video restyling, motion transfer from a reference clip, a character builder for consistency, lip sync and conversational editing for tasks such as swapping a background or relighting a scene. Resolution goes up to 4K on many models, with 1080p as the default, and clips are usually up to 15 seconds, with longer limits on a few models.

Strengths: model variety, a single credit pool, editing tools alongside generation and a shared library for your clips.

Limits: credit use varies by model, so it is worth testing short clips first. Commercial-use rights depend on your plan and on the model you pick, so check the pricing and terms pages.

Best for: creators and small teams that want to compare models quickly instead of committing to one.

Veo is Google DeepMind’s video model. Veo 3.1 generates sound effects, ambient noise and dialogue together with the video, supports image-to-video, first and last frame transitions, reference images for characters or objects, and outputs 1080p and 4K. You can reach it through the Gemini app, Google Flow and the Gemini API.

Strengths: audio generated with the picture, strong prompt adherence and an API that fits into developer workflows.

Limits: base clips are short, around 8 seconds, so longer scenes rely on scene extension. Dialogue quality in short segments is still being refined, and access and quotas depend on your Google plan.

Best for: developers and marketers who want audio and video from one prompt, or who plan to automate generation through an API.

Runway has been a staple of AI filmmaking since its early Gen models. Its pricing page lists Gen-4.5 and Aleph alongside partner models, and the paid plans include all video and image models, 4K upscaling and parallel generations. Higher tiers add features such as voice clones, brand kits and HDR or ProRes output.

Strengths: polished editing tools, video-to-video workflows and a professional output pipeline that suits post-production teams.

Limits: credits are consumed quickly on premium models, and on the lower paid plans they do not roll over from month to month.

Best for: video editors and agencies that need control over shots and want AI to fit into an existing editing process.

Kling from Kuaishou has built a reputation for natural motion and expressive characters. The current release, Kling 4.0, sits alongside tools such as Motion Control, a canvas workspace, an agent and MCP or CLI batch generation for teams that want to automate production.

Strengths: realistic movement, motion transfer and batch tools for producing many short clips.

Limits: the main site does not spell out all clip-length and resolution limits for each model, so check the model page you plan to use. Pricing and free credits vary by region and plan.

Best for: social video, product shots and character-driven clips where motion quality is the priority.

Luma’s Dream Machine is built on the Ray model family. Its current page lists Ray3.2 and Ray3.14, along with a “Modify video” tool that restyles footage, extends duration and reframes aspect ratios. It also lists camera-motion controls and high-quality EXR export for professional pipelines.

Strengths: reshooting or restyling existing footage, camera-motion control and pipeline-friendly export.

Limits: paid plans start at around $30 per month (or less when billed yearly), and heavy use pushes you into the higher tiers.

Best for: filmmakers and designers who want to modify and extend footage rather than only generate new clips.

Adobe Firefly offers text-to-video and image-to-video with Adobe’s own Firefly Video Model. It also provides partner models, including Google Veo 3.1, Luma Ray3, Kling and Runway Gen-4.5, so you can compare outputs from inside one product. An Assembler and AI video editor let you trim, reorder, extend and add voiceovers, music and sound effects.

Adobe states that the Firefly Video Model is trained on licensed content such as Adobe Stock and public domain material, and describes its output as safe for commercial use. Partner models may carry different terms, so read the details for whichever model you choose.

Strengths: a clear licensing story for the native model, integration with Adobe’s creative apps and an included editor.

Limits: most clips run up to 8 seconds, with some models reaching 15. Credits are allocated monthly, and premium partner models can use them faster.

Best for: brand and in-house teams that care about provenance and already work in Adobe tools.

The table summarizes what each vendor’s own pages say at the time of writing. “Entry paid plan” figures are US prices billed annually where the vendor offers that option.

Credit counts are hard to compare, so convert them to clips. Runway’s pricing page lists Gen-4.5 at 60 credits per 5-second clip, which means the 625 monthly credits on the Standard plan cover roughly ten clips. Adobe says its Standard plan’s 2,000 credits equal up to 40 five-second videos, and OpenArt’s Starter plan lists about 50 videos for 4,000 credits. Premium models cost more: the same Runway page lists Aleph 2.0 at 140 credits per 5 seconds.

Plan for waste. If you keep one usable clip out of every three attempts, your real cost per finished shot is about three times the sticker price. Test with short, low-resolution drafts, then render the winner at full quality.

Because every tool outputs short clips, the real work is assembly. A typical workflow is: write a shot list, generate two or three takes per shot, pick the best, normalize them to one format, join them and add captions and audio. FFmpeg handles the joining step from the command line:

Matching resolution, frame rate and codec before joining avoids glitches at the cut points. For character consistency across shots, reuse the same reference image in every prompt and keep descriptions of the subject identical.

If the clip ends up on your own site, treat it like any other media. Provide a poster image, load metadata only until the visitor presses play, and include captions so deaf and hard-of-hearing users, and anyone watching without sound, can follow along. The W3C’s guidance on captions and subtitles explains what to include, and the MDN video element reference covers the attributes below.

Also write a text description or transcript near the video. Search engines and screen reader users cannot see the footage, so the surrounding text is what explains it.

Short, structured prompts beat long, vague ones. Describe the elements in the order a director would:

Change one element at a time between takes so you can tell which change improved the result.

Marketing demos show the best of a tool, so run your own test. Use the same prompt, duration and aspect ratio on each tool, generate a few takes and score them against a short checklist. Keep notes on how many credits each usable clip costs.

Run the same test again with an image-to-video input if your workflow starts from product photos or illustrations. That often exposes differences in consistency that text prompts hide.

Which AI video generator is best? There is no single winner. Veo is strong for audio, Runway for editing, Kling for motion, Luma for modifying footage and Firefly for licensing clarity. A multi-model tool such as OpenArt helps you compare them.

Can AI video generators make long videos? Not in one pass. Most models create short clips, so longer videos are built by extending or stitching clips together.

Is Sora still available? OpenAI announced in March 2026 that it was shutting down the Sora app and API, so it is not a safe choice for new projects.

Can I use AI videos commercially? Often, but it depends on the plan and the underlying model. Read the current terms before publishing.

How do I keep a character consistent across clips? Reuse the same reference image, keep the subject description identical in every prompt, and use character or reference tools where the platform offers them.

Should I add captions to AI-generated video? Yes. Captions help accessibility and viewers watching without sound, and they give search engines text to index.

Do I need a powerful computer? No. These services run in the cloud, so a browser and a stable connection are enough.

The best AI video generator depends on what you make. Start with a short test prompt, compare a few tools, and keep the one that gives you usable clips at a sensible credit cost. If you are not sure where to begin, a multi-model option such as OpenArt lets you try several engines before you settle on one.

Everybody wants a piece of Jensen these days.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The baying of the hounds can be heard at a distance. Yesterday, Nvidia's Jensen Huang confirmed that it was indeed true that he had a romantic affair with Alice Huang (not related to the family), who U.S. intelligence services claim is a Chinese honeypot tasked with facilitating smuggling of the firm's advanced GPUs into China. Now, a class-action lawsuit and investigations have started flying from almost everywhere.

This development is not unexpected, as basic securities law allows shareholders to sue their investees if they misrepresent facts or otherwise perform actions that cause a drop in stock prices. They may have a point, as Nvidia's shares dipped significantly when the confirmation of Jensen's infidelity and the ramifications thereof hit the press everywhere.

The legal proceedings are bound to be numerous, but as of right now, the first class-action lawsuit was filed by the Rosen Law Firm. The suit claims that Nvidia and Jensen Huang failed to disclose or misled investors regarding Jensen's affair with Alice, lied about it when asked, raised national security concerns, and caused a subsequent loss of Nvidia's reputation. You can check out the case docket at PacerMonitor here.

While legal analysis is beyond the scope of this news, Nvidia did downplay Jensen's relationship with Alice, saying that "Megaspeed has been no different than many other regional cloud customers. Meetings with customers are part of a CEO’s job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year."

Other legal firms have launched their own investigations into the recent development, with essentially the same justification: Schall, Brown & Schwartz attributes a 5.5% pre-market-trading drop to Nvidia, as does Levi & Korsinsky. The office of Frank R. Cruz mentions an 8.8% figure related to intra-day exchanging, and is interested in shareholders with $50,000 in losses. The percentage-drop figure is 5.88% premarket for Bronstein, Gewritz & Grossman.

Every investigation and suit mentioned here requires no upfront fees from complainants, as with most class-action lawsuits. The underlying condition is often that the law firms involved get a cut of whichever damages or settlements may appear at the end of the lawsuit. Some of them, like the one by Levi & Korsinsky, even aim to relieve investors who've already sold their Nvidia shares and sold them at a loss, with no minimum amount required.

#10372. Google Messages homepage redesign: Pinned chat avatars, bottom search, corner Gemini

In addition to the new app icon, Google Messages for Android is testing a big homepage redesign that has three core components.

Update 11/7: Instead of pinned chats just appearing at the top of the list, Google Messages is trying a redesign that significantly elevates them above all conversations.

This redesign takes advantage of the existing background layer that’s currently just home to the logo/wordmark, search, and account menu.

You’ll see large contact photos appear above all chats, which pushes the list of other conversations down the page. Like iMessage on iOS, this layout has the downside of not showing any history or last sent information.

Meanwhile, Google is testing another change where search becomes a centered floating action button (FAB) alongside new chat. When you’re scrolling down the page, you’ll see “Search” in a pill-shaped button, with the other button minimized.

Hate the idea of just the pinned "bobble head" look. I like being able to view message tidbits. Plus, it's directly copying Apple (enough said). I have to use an iPhone at my job. I hate the pinned bubble conversation look and more importantly it's function.

Otherwise, you’ll see a circular magnifying glass icon and “Start chat” in the pill. The animation that accompanies this change looks nice and feels alive, while boosting one-handed usability.

Google has replaced the Gemini FAB with a button in the top-right corner next to your profile image. This matches the design leveraged by Workspace apps on desktop and mobile.

These updates are currently in the beta channel and not yet widely rolled out to all testers. Some users have all aspects of the redesign, but it appears that Google is rolling out the various components in a piecemeal fashion.
